<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/services/childProcessManager.ts">
/**
 * childProcessManager — Python subprocess lifecycle for the main process.
 *
 * Every WriteSpeare automation operation — search, extract, write,
 * validate, export — runs as a short-lived Python subprocess
 * spawned via `python main.py <subcommand> ...`. This module owns
 * the lifecycle of those subprocesses: it spawns them with the
 * correct executable, working directory, and environment; it
 * surfaces their stdout and stderr as line events; it kills their
 * entire process tree on demand; and it tracks every live manager
 * in a module-level registry so the app can find them at shutdown.
 *
 * The registry model — one manager per running task, keyed by taskId:
 *
 *   A task ID is a caller-supplied string that identifies a single
 *   running operation. Two concurrent operations (a search for
 *   topic A and an extraction for topic B) get two distinct task
 *   IDs and therefore two distinct managers; each manager's events
 *   belong exclusively to its own operation. The registry keyed
 *   by taskId is what allows a "kill everything" or "find the
 *   manager for topic X" call without the caller having to hold a
 *   reference to every manager it ever created.
 *
 *   The task ID is opaque to this module. A caller that uses
 *   `${topicId}-search` gets one behavior; a caller that uses a
 *   fresh UUID per operation gets another. The module's contract
 *   is only that the same taskId identifies the same manager
 *   (idempotent `createProcessManager`) and that different taskIds
 *   produce different managers.
 *
 * What this module does NOT do:
 *
 *   - It does not parse markers. The `::ROOKIE::<TYPE>::<json>`
 *     lines that `main.py` emits to stdout are parsed by the
 *     sibling `listenerBridge.ts` module, which attaches to the
 *     same ChildProcess stdout independently. This module emits
 *     raw stdout lines for any consumer that wants them (a
 *     developer inspecting the process, a future logging layer);
 *     the marker parsing and the IPC forwarding are the bridge's
 *     job, not this module's.
 *
 *   - It does not launch Chrome. Chrome is launched by the sibling
 *     `chromeLauncher.ts` module — a separate lifecycle with a
 *     separate registry. The port number the launcher assigns is
 *     passed to the Python script as a CLI argument (`--port`),
 *     and the script connects to that Chrome instance over CDP.
 *
 *   - It does not allocate ports. Ports are allocated by the
 *     sibling `portAllocator.ts` module, used by the launcher, and
 *     passed to the Python script as a CLI argument by the caller
 *     that built the operation's argument list.
 *
 *   - It does not know what a "topic" is. Topics, articles,
 *     reports, and every other domain concept live in the callers
 *     (searchService, extractionService, writerService, and so on
 *     — added in later session steps). This module operates on
 *     task IDs and argument arrays only.
 *
 *   - It does not write to a run-config file. The ROOKIE launcher
 *     read a config file that carried the plan path, the campaign
 *     ID, and the captcha policy. WriteSpeare's `main.py` has no
 *     such file — every operation's arguments arrive on the
 *     command line, and the captcha policy arrives via the
 *     WRITESPEARE_CAPTCHA_POLICY environment variable when the
 *     caller overrides the default.
 *
 * The environment variables the manager sets on every child:
 *
 *   PYTHONUTF8=1
 *       Force Python to use UTF-8 for all text I/O. Without this,
 *       Python on Windows defaults to the system code page (often
 *       cp1252), and a single emoji or a non-ASCII character in a
 *       scraped title crashes the process. Required for the
 *       pipeline to handle the web's actual content.
 *
 *   PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-8
 *       The complementary setting for stdin and stderr. Both are
 *       needed — PYTHONUTF8 handles the encoding, PYTHONIOENCODING
 *       handles the stream configuration.
 *
 *   PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
 *       Disable Python's block-buffered stdout when the output is
 *       a pipe. Without it, print() calls sit in an 8 KB in-memory
 *       buffer until the child exits, so a search's progress
 *       markers would all arrive at the renderer in a single burst
 *       AFTER the search finished — making the UI look frozen
 *       during the search and then suddenly complete. With this
 *       set, each marker flushes immediately and progress streams
 *       live.
 *
 *   PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH=<resolved path>
 *       Point Playwright at the bundled Chromium binaries instead
 *       of the default per-user cache. Without this, the packaged
 *       app's Python scripts would fail on a machine that has
 *       never run `playwright install chromium`, because
 *       Playwright would look in a directory that does not exist.
 *       The path is resolved from `app.isPackaged` so dev and
 *       packaged builds use different (correct) locations.
 *
 *   WRITESPEARE_CAPTCHA_POLICY=<policy>
 *       Set only when the caller supplies a `captchaPolicy` in
 *       `ProcessOptions`. When absent, `core/output.py` defaults
 *       the policy to 'ask' — the correct behavior for a normal
 *       interactive run. A caller that wants to auto-skip or
 *       auto-stop on captcha sets this explicitly.
 *
 * Dependencies: Node stdlib + electron + `tree-kill` (declared in
 * `apps/desktop/package.json`).
 */

import { spawn, type ChildProcess } from 'node:child_process';
import { EventEmitter } from 'node:events';
import path from 'node:path';
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';

import { app } from 'electron';
import treeKill from 'tree-kill';

// =============================================================================
//  ESM directory resolution
// =============================================================================
//
//  In the compiled output, this module lives at
//  `dist/features/research/services/childProcessManager.js`. The
//  path helpers below use `__dirname` to derive absolute paths to
//  the bundled Python runtime and the Python script source. ESM
//  does not provide `__dirname` as a global (unlike CommonJS), so
//  it is reconstructed here from `import.meta.url` via
//  `fileURLToPath` and `path.dirname`.

const __filename = fileURLToPath(import.meta.url);
const __dirname = path.dirname(__filename);

// =============================================================================
//  Public types
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The options accepted by `ChildProcessManager.start()`.
 */
export interface ProcessOptions {
  /**
   * Arguments passed to `main.py`, NOT including the script's own
   * path (the manager prepends it) and NOT including a `--control-mode`
   * flag (the caller includes it in this array when marker emission
   * is wanted).
   *
   * Example for a search operation:
   *
   *     ['search', '--query', 'small space gardening',
   *      '--keywords', 'container gardening,vertical planters',
   *      '--max-results', '20', '--topic-id', 'abc123',
   *      '--port', '9222', '--control-mode']
   */
  args: string[];

  /**
   * Working directory for the child process. Defaults to the
   * scripts folder (`<resources>/scripts` in packaged builds,
   * `src/resources/scripts` in development). The default is what
   * `main.py` expects — its `sys.path` bootstrap adds its own
   * directory to `sys.path`, so `core.*` and `research.*` imports
   * resolve regardless of cwd, but the default matches the
   * convention.
   */
  cwd?: string;

  /**
   * Additional environment variables for the child. Merged on top
   * of the manager's own environment (which includes
   * `process.env` plus the six WriteSpeare-specific variables
   * documented in the file header). A caller that passes a value
   * for a variable the manager already sets overrides the
   * manager's value.
   */
  env?: Record<string, string>;

  /**
   * Optional captcha policy for the child. When provided, the
   * manager sets `WRITESPEARE_CAPTCHA_POLICY` to this value in the
   * child's environment. When omitted, the variable is not set and
   * `core/output.py`'s `manual_action_required` defaults the
   * policy to `'ask'` — the correct behavior for a normal
   * interactive run.
   *
   * `'ask'`  — show the captcha modal and block for the user
   *            (this is what the default produces; passing it
   *            explicitly is equivalent to omitting the field).
   * `'skip'` — auto-skip the operation with exit code 101
   *            (`EXIT_CAPTCHA_SKIP`).
   * `'stop'` — auto-stop the run with exit code 100
   *            (`EXIT_STOPPED`).
   */
  captchaPolicy?: 'ask' | 'skip' | 'stop';

  /**
   * Optional timeout in milliseconds. When set and greater than
   * zero, the manager starts a timer on spawn; if the child has
   * not exited by the deadline, the manager emits a `timeout`
   * event and calls `kill()`. When omitted (or set to a
   * non-positive value), no timer is started and the child runs
   * until it exits on its own or the caller kills it.
   *
   * The default (no timeout) is correct for operations whose
   * duration depends on the target site's responsiveness — a
   * search may legitimately take thirty seconds on a slow network,
   * and a hard timeout that fires early would abort a working run.
   */
  timeout?: number;
}

/**
 * The events `ChildProcessManager` emits. This interface documents
 * the shapes a caller can expect when it subscribes; it is NOT a
 * compile-time constraint (Node's `EventEmitter` accepts any
 * listener signature), but it is the contract callers should rely
 * on when they write their handlers.
 *
 * Usage:
 *
 *     const manager = createProcessManager(taskId);
 *     manager.on('stdout', (line) => { ... });
 *     manager.on('exit', (code, signal) => { ... });
 *     await manager.start({ args: [...] });
 */
export interface ProcessEvents {
  /** Emitted for each complete line of child stdout. */
  stdout: (line: string) => void;

  /** Emitted for each complete line of child stderr. */
  stderr: (line: string) => void;

  /** Emitted when the child process exits on its own (not when
   *  it was killed by the manager — the `killed` event covers
   *  that case). */
  exit: (code: number | null, signal: string | null) => void;

  /** Emitted when the manager's `kill()` terminates the child. */
  killed: () => void;

  /** Emitted when the manager's timeout fires and the child is
   *  killed. */
  timeout: () => void;

  /** Emitted when the child process fails to spawn or encounters
   *  a runtime error (a broken pipe, a signal from an external
   *  source). */
  error: (error: Error) => void;
}

// =============================================================================
//  Path helpers
// =============================================================================
//
//  Every path helper is a pure function of `app.isPackaged` and
//  `__dirname`. None of them touch the filesystem — a helper that
//  returns a path the app cannot use will fail at the point of
//  use, with a clearer error than an `existsSync` check here would
//  produce. The exception is `getScriptPath`'s dev branch, which
//  does check for the compiled location's existence before falling
//  back — a nuance inherited from the ROOKIE source and preserved
//  here for the same reason.

/**
 * Absolute path to the Python interpreter the manager spawns.
 *
 *   Packaged: `<resources>/python/python.exe` (Windows),
 *             `<resources>/python/bin/python3` (macOS / Linux).
 *   Dev:      `'python'` — resolved by the OS's `PATH` lookup.
 *             A developer running the app from a terminal has a
 *             working Python on their PATH; requiring the embedded
 *             runtime for dev would mean every dev iteration has to
 *             go through the packaged build, which is not the
 *             workflow the plan intends.
 */
const getPythonExecutable = (): string => {
  if (app.isPackaged) {
    const basePath = process.resourcesPath;
    const platform = process.platform;
    if (platform === 'win32') {
      return path.join(basePath, 'python', 'python.exe');
    }
    if (platform === 'darwin' || platform === 'linux') {
      return path.join(basePath, 'python', 'bin', 'python3');
    }
    return path.join(basePath, 'python', 'python');
  }
  return 'python';
};

/**
 * Absolute path to `main.py`.
 *
 *   Packaged: `<resources>/scripts/main.py` — matches the
 *             `extraResources` mapping in `apps/desktop/package.json`.
 *   Dev:      `apps/desktop/src/resources/scripts/main.py` —
 *             resolved relative to this file's compiled location
 *             (`dist/features/research/services/`).
 */
const getScriptPath = (): string => {
  if (app.isPackaged) {
    return path.join(process.resourcesPath, 'scripts', 'main.py');
  }
  // Dev path: from dist/features/research/services/ up four levels
  // to the desktop app root, then into src/resources/scripts.
  const devPath = path.join(
    __dirname,
    '../../../..',
    'src',
    'resources',
    'scripts',
    'main.py'
  );
  return devPath;
};

/**
 * Absolute path to the working directory the child should use.
 *
 *   Packaged: `<resources>/scripts` — the folder containing
 *             `main.py` and the sibling `core/`, `research/`,
 *             `writer/`, `validation/`, and `export/` packages.
 *   Dev:      `apps/desktop/src/resources/scripts` — the same
 *             folder in the source tree.
 */
const getWorkingDirectory = (): string => {
  if (app.isPackaged) {
    return path.join(process.resourcesPath, 'scripts');
  }
  return path.join(
    __dirname,
    '../../../..',
    'src',
    'resources',
    'scripts'
  );
};

/**
 * Absolute path to the folder containing Playwright's bundled
 * Chromium binaries.
 *
 *   Packaged: `<resources>/python/ms-playwright` — matches the
 *             `extraResources` mapping.
 *   Dev:      `apps/desktop/src/resources/python/ms-playwright` —
 *             the folder the Python-runtime setup step installs
 *             Chromium into.
 *
 * Exported to the child via `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH`. See the
 * file header for why this is required.
 */
const getPlaywrightBrowsersPath = (): string => {
  if (app.isPackaged) {
    return path.join(process.resourcesPath, 'python', 'ms-playwright');
  }
  return path.join(
    __dirname,
    '../../../..',
    'src',
    'resources',
    'python',
    'ms-playwright'
  );
};

// =============================================================================
//  ChildProcessManager
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Manages one Python subprocess.
 *
 * Every instance is registered in the module-level `processManagers`
 * map (below) under its `taskId`. The map is the app's source of
 * truth for "which Python processes are currently running"; the
 * instance itself owns the actual child process handle, the
 * timeout timer, and the event emission.
 *
 * The class extends `EventEmitter`. A caller subscribes via the
 * standard `on(event, listener)` / `once(event, listener)` methods
 * with the event names documented in `ProcessEvents`.
 *
 * Construction:
 *
 *     const manager = createProcessManager(taskId);
 *     manager.on('stdout', handleStdout);
 *     manager.on('exit', handleExit);
 *     await manager.start({ args: [...] });
 *
 * The `createProcessManager` helper is the recommended way to
 * construct an instance — it goes through the registry and is
 * idempotent for the same task ID. Direct `new ChildProcessManager(...)`
 * is supported (the constructor is public) for tests that want an
 * unregistered instance, but a caller that constructs directly is
 * responsible for its own disposal.
 */
export class ChildProcessManager extends EventEmitter {
  /**
   * The task ID this manager was created under. Set at
   * construction and never changes. Used as the registry key in
   * the module-level map and included in every log line so a
   * developer scanning the main-process console can tell which
   * manager emitted which message when several operations run in
   * parallel.
   */
  public readonly taskId: string;

  /**
   * The live child process, or `null` when none is running. Set
   * by `start()`; cleared by the exit handler, the error handler,
   * and `kill()`. A caller that holds a manager reference can
   * observe this field to answer "is the child still alive?"
   * without going through the `isRunning()` method.
   */
  private process: ChildProcess | null = null;

  /**
   * Whether the current child (if any) was terminated by this
   * manager's `kill()` rather than exiting on its own. The
   * `exit` event is suppressed when this is `true` — the `killed`
   * event covers that case — so a caller's exit handler does not
   * fire for a kill it initiated itself.
   */
  private isKilled = false;

  /**
   * The pending timeout timer, or `null`. Set by `start()` when
   * the caller supplies a positive timeout; cleared by the exit
   * handler, the error handler, and `kill()`.
   */
  private timeoutId: NodeJS.Timeout | null = null;

  /**
   * @param taskId - The identifier this manager is registered
   *                 under. Must be a non-empty string. The
   *                 constructor throws if it is empty or
   *                 non-string, because an empty task ID would
   *                 silently collide with every other empty ID
   *                 in the registry.
   */
  public constructor(taskId: string) {
    super();
    if (typeof taskId !== 'string' || !taskId.trim()) {
      throw new Error(
        'ChildProcessManager requires a non-empty taskId.'
      );
    }
    this.taskId = taskId.trim();
  }

  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  //  start
  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

  /**
   * Spawn the child process.
   *
   * Returns a promise that resolves once the `spawn` call has
   * returned a handle. It does NOT wait for the child to finish —
   * the child's output and termination arrive as events, and the
   * caller subscribes to them before or immediately after calling
   * `start()`.
   *
   * If a child is already running under this manager, `start()`
   * calls `kill()` on it first (fire-and-forget — the previous
   * child's kill completes asynchronously) and then spawns the
   * new one. This matches the "a manager represents one running
   * operation" model; a caller that wants two concurrent
   * operations uses two managers.
   *
   * The promise resolves on success and rejects only if the
   * `spawn` call itself throws synchronously (a malformed
   * executable path, an EACCES on the interpreter). A spawn
   * failure that arrives asynchronously — a nonexistent
   * executable, a permission error — is delivered via the `error`
   * event; the promise has already resolved by then, and the
   * caller's error handler is what sees it.
   */
  public start(options: ProcessOptions): Promise<void> {
    return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
      // Ensure any previous process is killed. `kill()` is
      // fire-and-forget — the previous child's tree-kill
      // completes asynchronously, but we do not wait for it
      // before spawning the new child. That is safe because the
      // new child gets a fresh set of env vars and args; if the
      // old child's teardown still has a socket open somewhere,
      // the two children do not interact.
      if (this.process) {
        this.kill();
      }

      const pythonPath = getPythonExecutable();
      const scriptPath = getScriptPath();
      const cwd = options.cwd ?? getWorkingDirectory();
      const playwrightBrowsersPath = getPlaywrightBrowsersPath();

      // ---- Build the environment ----
      // Order of precedence (later entries win):
      //   1. process.env — inherit the main process's environment
      //      so PATH, SystemRoot, and the OS's own variables are
      //      available to the child.
      //   2. The six WriteSpeare-specific variables documented in
      //      the file header.
      //   3. WRITESPEARE_CAPTCHA_POLICY if the caller supplied
      //      options.captchaPolicy.
      //   4. options.env — caller-supplied overrides, applied last.
      const env: NodeJS.ProcessEnv = {
        ...process.env,
        PYTHONUTF8: '1',
        PYTHONIOENCODING: 'utf-8',
        PYTHONUNBUFFERED: '1',
        PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH: playwrightBrowsersPath,
      };
      if (options.captchaPolicy !== undefined) {
        env['WRITESPEARE_CAPTCHA_POLICY'] = options.captchaPolicy;
      }
      if (options.env !== undefined) {
        for (const [key, value] of Object.entries(options.env)) {
          env[key] = value;
        }
      }

      // ---- Assemble the argument list ----
      // Python is invoked as `python <scriptPath> <args...>` —
      // the script path is the first argument to the interpreter,
      // and everything else is passed through to main.py's
      // argparse.
      const args = [scriptPath, ...options.args];

      console.log(
        `🚀 Spawning Python for task ${this.taskId}: ` +
          `${pythonPath} ${args.join(' ')}`
      );
      console.log(`📁 Working directory: ${cwd}`);

      // ---- Spawn ----
      // stdio: ['pipe', 'pipe', 'pipe'] — all three streams are
      // piped. stdin is required because writeStdin() delivers
      // Skip/Stop/Continue commands to the child via its stdin.
      // stdout and stderr are required because the manager emits
      // their lines as events and because the listener bridge
      // (a separate consumer) attaches to stdout to parse markers.
      //
      // windowsHide: true — suppress the console window that
      // Windows would otherwise create for a spawned console
      // process. Without it, every Python spawn briefly flashes a
      // black window on the user's taskbar.
      try {
        this.process = spawn(pythonPath, args, {
          cwd,
          env,
          stdio: ['pipe', 'pipe', 'pipe'],
          detached: false,
          windowsHide: true,
        });
      } catch (err) {
        reject(
          err instanceof Error ? err : new Error(String(err))
        );
        return;
      }

      this.isKilled = false;

      // ---- Attach stdout handler ----
      // Lines are split on /\r?\n/ so that a Windows CRLF does not
      // leave a trailing \r on every line. A naive split('\n')
      // would produce lines ending in '\r', and the marker regex
      // in listenerBridge.ts (`^::ROOKIE::...`) would fail to
      // match — the CAPTCHA_REQUIRED and every other marker would
      // silently fail to parse and the GUI would stay dark.
      //
      // The split leaves a trailing empty element when the chunk
      // ends in \n (which every well-formed print() does); the
      // `.filter((line) => line.trim())` drops that empty line so
      // no consumer ever sees a phantom "" event.
      this.process.stdout?.on('data', (data: Buffer) => {
        const output = data.toString('utf-8');
        const lines = output.split(/\r?\n/);
        for (const line of lines) {
          if (line.trim().length > 0) {
            this.emit('stdout', line);
          }
        }
      });

      // ---- Attach stderr handler ----
      // Same splitting logic and same reasoning as the stdout
      // handler above. Kept separate because the two streams are
      // semantically distinct — stdout carries markers, stderr
      // carries uncaught tracebacks — and a caller may want to
      // route them to different destinations.
      this.process.stderr?.on('data', (data: Buffer) => {
        const output = data.toString('utf-8');
        const lines = output.split(/\r?\n/);
        for (const line of lines) {
          if (line.trim().length > 0) {
            this.emit('stderr', line);
          }
        }
      });

      // ---- Attach exit handler ----
      // Fires when the child terminates for any reason — a clean
      // exit, a crash, or a signal. The timeout is cleared (there
      // is nothing left to time out). The `exit` event is emitted
      // only if the termination was NOT initiated by `kill()` —
      // the `killed` event covers that case, and emitting both
      // would confuse callers that rely on exactly one of them
      // firing per termination.
      this.process.on('exit', (code, signal) => {
        if (this.timeoutId) {
          clearTimeout(this.timeoutId);
          this.timeoutId = null;
        }
        if (!this.isKilled) {
          this.emit('exit', code, signal);
        }
        this.process = null;
      });

      // ---- Attach error handler ----
      // Emitted by the ChildProcess for two categories of failure:
      // (a) the process could not be spawned (ENOENT, EACCES),
      // (b) the process could not be killed (a rare, system-level
      // condition). The manager does not try to distinguish these
      // — both are surfaced via the `error` event so the caller's
      // error path handles them uniformly.
      this.process.on('error', (error) => {
        this.emit('error', error);
        this.process = null;
      });

      // ---- Timeout ----
      // Only set when the caller explicitly asks for one. See the
      // ProcessOptions.timeout docstring for why the default is
      // no timeout.
      if (
        options.timeout !== undefined &&
        options.timeout > 0
      ) {
        this.timeoutId = setTimeout(() => {
          if (this.process && !this.isKilled) {
            this.emit('timeout');
            this.kill();
          }
        }, options.timeout);
      }

      resolve();
    });
  }

  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  //  writeStdin
  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

  /**
   * Write a command line to the child's stdin.
   *
   * The Skip / Stop / Continue IPC handlers call this to deliver
   * commands to `main.py`, which relays them to the currently
   * active feature script via `core/control_mode.py`. The
   * command strings the child understands are the ones that
   * module classifies: `'SKIP'`, `'STOP'`, `'CONTINUE'`, or the
   * empty string (which is equivalent to `'CONTINUE'`).
   *
   * Behaviour:
   *
   *   - A no-op when no process is running, stdin is closed, or
   *     the process has already been killed. A race between a
   *     user's click on Stop and the process exiting on its own
   *     must not throw — the user's intent ("stop the child") is
   *     already satisfied either way.
   *
   *   - A trailing newline is appended if the caller did not
   *     supply one. This lets a caller write `'SKIP'` or `'SKIP\n'`
   *     interchangeably.
   *
   *   - A write error (a broken pipe, EPIPE) is logged and
   *     swallowed. A closed stdin is a normal condition during
   *     process teardown and is not a reason to throw.
   *
   * @param line - The command line. See the docstring above for
   *               the accepted values.
   */
  public writeStdin(line: string): void {
    if (!this.process || !this.process.stdin || this.isKilled) {
      return;
    }
    try {
      const normalized = line.endsWith('\n') ? line : line + '\n';
      this.process.stdin.write(normalized);
    } catch (error) {
      console.error(
        `[childProcessManager] Failed to write to child stdin ` +
          `for task ${this.taskId}:`,
        error
      );
    }
  }

  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  //  kill
  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

  /**
   * Terminate the current child process and its entire process
   * tree.
   *
   * Uses `tree-kill` (via its callback API, called synchronously)
   * to walk the process tree and terminate every descendant.
   * `child.kill()` alone would leave Chrome processes and any
   * Python subprocesses orphaned on Windows — see the sibling
   * `chromeLauncher.ts`'s file header for the full rationale,
   * which applies identically here.
   *
   * Fire-and-forget: the underlying `tree-kill` completes
   * asynchronously, and the manager does not wait for it. A
   * "Stop" button that had to wait for every process in the
   * tree to acknowledge SIGTERM would feel laggy; the caller's
   * `killed` event fires as soon as the tree-kill has been
   * dispatched, not when every child has exited.
   *
   * Idempotent: calling `kill()` twice on the same manager is
   * safe. The second call sees no live process and emits `killed`
   * again (the event is not suppressed on the second call — a
   * caller that subscribes twice will get two `killed` events,
   * which is unusual but not incorrect).
   */
  public kill(): void {
    if (this.process && !this.isKilled) {
      const pid = this.process.pid;
      if (pid !== undefined) {
        console.log(
          `🛑 Killing process tree for task ${this.taskId} ` +
            `(PID: ${pid})`
        );
        treeKill(pid, 'SIGTERM', (error?: Error) => {
          if (error) {
            console.error(
              `❌ Error killing process tree for task ` +
                `${this.taskId}:`,
              error
            );
            // Fall back to a direct kill of the root process.
            // The children will be orphaned but will notice
            // their parent is gone shortly — a less clean
            // teardown than tree-kill, but better than leaving
            // the whole tree running.
            try {
              this.process?.kill('SIGKILL');
            } catch (e) {
              console.error('❌ Direct kill also failed:', e);
            }
          } else {
            console.log(
              `✅ Process tree killed for task ${this.taskId} ` +
                `(PID: ${pid})`
            );
          }
          this.isKilled = true;
          this.emit('killed');
          this.process = null;
        });
      } else {
        // No PID — the spawn failed before the child got an ID.
        // There is nothing to tree-kill; just mark the manager
        // as having no live process.
        try {
          this.process.kill('SIGKILL');
        } catch {
          // Swallowed — see the file header for why kill
          // failures are logged and tolerated.
        }
        this.isKilled = true;
        this.emit('killed');
        this.process = null;
      }
    } else {
      // Already killed or never started. Emit `killed` so a
      // caller awaiting the event is not left hanging.
      this.emit('killed');
    }

    if (this.timeoutId) {
      clearTimeout(this.timeoutId);
      this.timeoutId = null;
    }
  }

  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  //  Introspection
  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

  /**
   * Whether a child process is currently running under this
   * manager. `true` from the moment `start()` resolves until the
   * child exits or `kill()` completes; `false` otherwise.
   */
  public isRunning(): boolean {
    return this.process !== null && !this.isKilled;
  }

  /**
   * The child's process ID, or `null` when no child is running.
   * Useful for a diagnostic log line or a test assertion.
   */
  public getPid(): number | null {
    return this.process?.pid ?? null;
  }

  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
  //  dispose
  // ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

  /**
   * Kill the current child (if any) and remove every event
   * listener the caller attached.
   *
   * Called by `disposeProcessManager` and `disposeAll` when a
   * caller is done with a manager. A caller that keeps a manager
   * after `dispose()` will observe no further events — every
   * subscription has been removed.
   */
  public dispose(): void {
    this.kill();
    this.removeAllListeners();
  }
}

// =============================================================================
//  Registry
// =============================================================================
//
//  Every live manager is registered in this module-level map,
//  keyed by task ID. The map is the app's source of truth for
//  "which Python processes are currently running" — the shutdown
//  path iterates it to kill every subprocess before the app
//  exits, and a diagnostics panel can inspect it to show the
//  user what is running.
//
//  The registry is module-scoped and never persisted. Process
//  handles and PIDs are meaningless across app restarts; a fresh
//  launch starts with an empty registry.

/**
 * All live managers, keyed by task ID.
 */
const processManagers = new Map<string, ChildProcessManager>();

/**
 * Get or create the manager for a task ID.
 *
 * Idempotent for the same task ID: the second call returns the
 * existing instance rather than constructing a new one. This is
 * what prevents two callers from each spawning a Python process
 * for the same logical task — a race the registry exists to close.
 *
 * The returned manager is NOT started. The caller is expected to
 * attach event listeners and then call `.start(options)` itself.
 *
 * A caller that wants a fresh manager for a task ID that is
 * already registered calls `disposeProcessManager(taskId)` first;
 * the next `createProcessManager(taskId)` then constructs a new
 * instance.
 */
export const createProcessManager = (
  taskId: string
): ChildProcessManager => {
  const existing = processManagers.get(taskId);
  if (existing !== undefined) {
    return existing;
  }
  const manager = new ChildProcessManager(taskId);
  processManagers.set(taskId, manager);
  return manager;
};

/**
 * Look up the manager for a task ID, or `null` if none exists.
 *
 * Unlike `createProcessManager`, this does NOT construct a new
 * manager when the task ID is unknown. Use this when you want to
 * inspect or control an existing manager without side effects.
 */
export const getByTaskId = (
  taskId: string
): ChildProcessManager | null => {
  return processManagers.get(taskId) ?? null;
};

/**
 * Every manager whose child is currently running.
 *
 * A manager that has finished its operation but not yet been
 * disposed is NOT included — see `ChildProcessManager.isRunning()`.
 */
export const getAllRunning = (): ChildProcessManager[] => {
  return Array.from(processManagers.values()).filter((m) =>
    m.isRunning()
  );
};

/**
 * Kill every running manager's process tree, but leave the
 * managers themselves registered.
 *
 * Fire-and-forget — each `kill()` dispatches its tree-kill and
 * returns immediately. A "Stop All" click does not wait for four
 * separate tree-kills to complete before the IPC handler returns
 * to the renderer.
 *
 * Use this when the app needs to stop all active work but may
 * want to inspect manager state afterward (a Stop All button that
 * keeps the run cards visible, for example). For a full shutdown,
 * use `disposeAll()` — it kills AND removes.
 */
export const killAll = (): void => {
  for (const manager of processManagers.values()) {
    manager.kill();
  }
};

/**
 * Dispose one task's manager — kill its child (if any) and remove
 * it from the registry.
 *
 * After this returns, `createProcessManager(taskId)` will produce
 * a fresh instance rather than returning the disposed one.
 */
export const disposeProcessManager = (taskId: string): void => {
  const manager = processManagers.get(taskId);
  if (manager === undefined) {
    return;
  }
  manager.dispose();
  processManagers.delete(taskId);
};

/**
 * Dispose every manager and clear the registry.
 *
 * Called on app shutdown (see `before-quit` in `src/main/index.ts`)
 * so that a subsequent startup in the same process — a future
 * "restart app" flow, or a test runner — begins with an empty
 * registry.
 */
export const disposeAll = (): void => {
  for (const manager of processManagers.values()) {
    manager.dispose();
  }
  processManagers.clear();
};

// =============================================================================
//  Default export
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Every export bundled under one default object, matching the
 * pattern the sibling `portAllocator.ts` and `chromeLauncher.ts`
 * modules use. A caller that prefers named imports uses
 * `import { createProcessManager, disposeAll } from ...`; a
 * caller that prefers the default form uses
 * `import childProcessManager from ...` and destructures the
 * members it needs.
 */
export default {
  ChildProcessManager,
  createProcessManager,
  getByTaskId,
  getAllRunning,
  killAll,
  disposeProcessManager,
  disposeAll,
};
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/services/extractionService.ts">
/**
 * extractionService — orchestrates one extraction operation.
 *
 * The extraction stage takes the URLs the user selected from a
 * search's candidate list, opens each one in the app's persistent
 * Chrome instance, turns its HTML into clean Markdown via the
 * Python extractors, and writes one `.md` doc file per successful
 * URL into the topic's `sources/` folder. This module is the
 * main-process coordinator for that operation.
 *
 * ── The flow ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   1. Ensure Chrome is running. Reuse the app's persistent-
 *      profile Chrome if one is registered with the launcher;
 *      otherwise launch a fresh one with the app's chrome-profile
 *      path. Chrome is NOT killed at the end of the operation —
 *      it lives for the app's lifetime so that the user's
 *      DeepSeek login (and every other cookie in the profile)
 *      survives across operations. Shutdown is the app's
 *      `before-quit` handler's job.
 *
 *   2. Spawn `python main.py extract` with the operation's
 *      arguments and the Chrome port. The task ID is derived
 *      from the topic ID (`${topicId}-extract`) so that a
 *      cancel call can find the manager.
 *
 *   3. Subscribe to the manager's `stdout` event. Each line is
 *      parsed as a `::ROOKIE::<TYPE>::<json>` marker; a parsed
 *      marker is broadcast to every renderer window over the
 *      `research:marker` IPC channel — the same channel the
 *      listener bridge uses. EXTRACT_PROGRESS markers drive the
 *      live progress panel; EXTRACT_DONE carries the terminal
 *      savedCount/failedCount; CAPTCHA_REQUIRED opens the modal;
 *      TASK_START / TASK_END bracket the operation.
 *
 *   4. Wait for the child to exit. The exit code classifies the
 *      outcome:
 *
 *        0   → success. At least one document was saved.
 *        1   → either "ran but saved nothing" (an EXTRACT_DONE
 *              marker was seen with savedCount === 0) or a hard
 *              failure (no EXTRACT_DONE marker). The two are
 *              distinguished by the `sawExtractDone` flag.
 *        99  → the user clicked Skip. Return kind='user-skip'.
 *        100 → the user clicked Stop. Return kind='user-stop'.
 *        101 → the captcha policy was 'skip' and a captcha
 *              appeared. Return kind='user-skip'.
 *        any other non-zero → a generic failure. Return
 *              kind='error' with the process's exit code in the
 *              message.
 *
 *   5. Return the extraction result. The function never rejects —
 *      a failure is reported via the resolved result's
 *      `success: false` and `kind` fields, matching the pattern
 *      established by `searchService.runSearch`.
 *
 * ── The marker-routing design tradeoff ──────────────────────────
 *
 *   `listenerBridge.ts` was written to attach to a raw
 *   `ChildProcess` object. `childProcessManager.ts` does not
 *   expose its internal `ChildProcess` — its `process` field is
 *   private, and its `start()` method returns only a resolved
 *   `Promise<void>`. So this module cannot pass the child to
 *   `attachToProcess()`.
 *
 *   The alternative was to modify one of the two files, which the
 *   current session forbids. Instead, this module attaches its
 *   own listener to the manager's `stdout` events, parses
 *   markers with a small local parser (same algorithm as the
 *   bridge's), and broadcasts them itself using the same
 *   `MARKER_EVENT_CHANNEL` constant and the same
 *   `webContents.send` shape.
 *
 *   The renderer cannot tell the difference — it receives the
 *   same `{ type, payload }` events on the same channel it would
 *   have received from the bridge. The duplication is roughly
 *   twenty lines of parsing logic, and `searchService.ts` already
 *   contains the same twenty lines for the same reason. This
 *   file's copy is the second instance; a future third copy
 *   (a writer-stage or validation-stage service) would be the
 *   moment to rework the boundary, but for two services the
 *   duplication is cheaper than a shared abstraction whose only
 *   two callers are in the same folder.
 *
 * ── Why the extraction task ID differs from the search task ID ──
 *
 *   `searchService` uses `${topicId}-search` as its task ID. This
 *   module uses `${topicId}-extract`. The two are distinct so that
 *   a search and an extraction for the same topic can, in
 *   principle, run simultaneously without their manager registries
 *   colliding — the childProcessManager's registry is keyed by
 *   task ID and a collision would have the second `createProcessManager`
 *   call return the first's manager.
 *
 *   The consequence a reader should be aware of: the
 *   `research:cancel` IPC handler in `researchIPC.ts` currently
 *   looks up only the SEARCH task ID (`getSearchTaskId(topicId)`).
 *   Cancelling an in-flight extraction therefore does not, today,
 *   reach the extraction's manager. A future session that wants
 *   to close that gap would extend the cancel handler to look up
 *   both task IDs. The correct fix is not to reuse the search's
 *   task ID here, because doing so would defeat the registry's
 *   collision avoidance. See the PLAN DELTA logged with this
 *   file's creation for the full note.
 *
 * ── What this module does NOT do ────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   - It does not kill Chrome. Chrome is the app's shared,
 *     persistent-profile instance; killing it at the end of every
 *     extraction would force a re-login to DeepSeek on the next
 *     operation. Shutdown is `main/index.ts`'s `before-quit`
 *     handler's job.
 *
 *   - It does not persist doc metadata to the backend. The doc
 *     files land on the desktop's disk; the backend's
 *     `/api/documents` endpoint is written to by a separate step
 *     (a future session's documents feature, which reads the
 *     files this operation produced and POSTs their metadata).
 *     This module's job ends when the child process has exited
 *     and the markers have been broadcast.
 *
 *   - It does not retry failed URLs. The Python extractor's
 *     `EXTRACT_PROGRESS` marker reports per-URL status, and a
 *     failed URL stays failed for the batch. The user can retry
 *     it by running the extraction again with that URL alone —
 *     a natural flow the renderer supports by letting the user
 *     re-check a subset of the candidate list.
 *
 *   - It does not filter or validate the URLs beyond the shallow
 *     shape checks in `runExtract` (non-empty array, non-empty
 *     strings). The renderer's source list is the UI's filter;
 *     the Python extractor's dispatch is the content filter.
 *
 * Dependencies: Node stdlib + electron + the three sibling
 * services (`portAllocator` is used indirectly via
 * `chromeLauncher`).
 */

import { app, BrowserWindow } from 'electron';
import path from 'node:path';

import {
  MARKER_EVENT_CHANNEL,
  type MarkerEvent,
} from './listenerBridge.js';
import {
  getRunningInstances,
  launchChrome,
} from './chromeLauncher.js';
import {
  createProcessManager,
  disposeProcessManager,
  type ProcessOptions,
} from './childProcessManager.js';

// =============================================================================
//  Public types
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The classification of an extraction operation's outcome.
 *
 *   'success'        — the extraction ran and saved at least one
 *                      document. `success` is `true`.
 *   'no-documents'   — the extraction ran cleanly but saved
 *                      nothing. Every URL failed, or the batch
 *                      was empty after Python filtered it. The
 *                      user has nothing new to show for the run.
 *   'error'          — the extraction process failed for a
 *                      reason other than a user action. The
 *                      Python script could not import its
 *                      dispatch module, a Chrome connection
 *                      failed before the batch started, or the
 *                      process crashed.
 *   'user-skip'      — the user clicked Skip on a blocking
 *                      prompt, or the operation was cancelled.
 *   'user-stop'      — the user clicked Stop.
 */
export type ExtractionResultKind =
  | 'success'
  | 'no-documents'
  | 'error'
  | 'user-skip'
  | 'user-stop';

/**
 * The options `runExtract` accepts.
 */
export interface ExtractionServiceOptions {
  /** The topic's ID. Used to derive the task ID (so the cancel
   *  path can find the manager), and included in every emitted
   *  marker so the renderer routes the progress to the right
   *  topic's panel. */
  topicId: string;

  /** The URLs to extract. The user selected these from the
   *  candidate list; the order is preserved — the Python
   *  extractor writes doc files in the same order the user saw
   *  them. */
  urls: string[];

  /** The captcha policy to forward to the Python script. See
   *  `ProcessOptions.captchaPolicy` for the semantics. Omit to
   *  get the Python side's default of `'ask'`. */
  captchaPolicy?: 'ask' | 'skip' | 'stop';
}

/**
 * The result of one `runExtract` call. Never thrown — the promise
 * always resolves, and failures are described by the `success` /
 * `kind` / `error` fields.
 */
export interface ExtractionServiceResult {
  /** True only when `kind === 'success'`. */
  success: boolean;

  /** How many documents the extraction saved. Populated on
   *  success and on partial-save failures; zero when the
   *  operation failed before the batch ran. */
  savedCount: number;

  /** How many URLs the extractor attempted but could not save.
   *  Populated whenever the EXTRACT_DONE marker was seen,
   *  including on `'no-documents'` and on some `'error'` paths
   *  where the batch started but the process exited non-zero. */
  failedCount: number;

  /** The classification of the outcome. See
   *  `ExtractionResultKind`. */
  kind: ExtractionResultKind;

  /** A human-readable error message. Present whenever `success`
   *  is `false`. */
  error?: string;
}

// =============================================================================
//  Constants
// =============================================================================

/** The marker prefix that every Python script writes on stdout.
 *  Must match `core/output.py` and `listenerBridge.ts`. */
const MARKER_PREFIX = '::ROOKIE::';

/** The exit code the Python side uses for "user clicked Skip"
 *  (mirrors `core/output.py`'s `EXIT_SKIPPED`). */
const EXIT_SKIPPED = 99;

/** The exit code for "user clicked Stop" (`EXIT_STOPPED`). */
const EXIT_STOPPED = 100;

/** The exit code for "auto-skipped because captcha policy was
 *  'skip'" (`EXIT_CAPTCHA_SKIP`). */
const EXIT_CAPTCHA_SKIP = 101;

// =============================================================================
//  Helpers
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The absolute path to the persistent Chrome profile the app
 * uses. Deterministic across operations so that every Chrome
 * launched by the app shares one profile — and one DeepSeek
 * login.
 *
 * The path is `<userData>/chrome-profile`. On Windows that
 * resolves to `%APPDATA%/writespeare-desktop/chrome-profile`; on
 * macOS to `~/Library/Application Support/writespeare-desktop/chrome-profile`;
 * on Linux to `~/.config/writespeare-desktop/chrome-profile`. The
 * `userData` name is set by Electron from the app's package.json
 * `name` field.
 *
 * The path is the same string `searchService.ts` computes. The
 * duplication is intentional: the two services are peers, and a
 * shared constant module would be a new file outside the plan's
 * tree. If a third service ever needs the same path, the correct
 * change is to promote it into a small shared helper.
 */
const getChromeProfilePath = (): string => {
  return path.join(app.getPath('userData'), 'chrome-profile');
};

/**
 * Return the current app-wide Chrome instance's debug port,
 * launching a fresh Chrome if none is running.
 *
 * See the file header for why Chrome is reused across operations
 * rather than launched per operation — the persistent profile
 * holds the user's DeepSeek login and killing Chrome would force
 * a re-login.
 *
 * The function is `async` because launching Chrome (the slow path)
 * is async. The fast path — an existing instance — returns
 * synchronously wrapped in a resolved promise.
 *
 * The implementation mirrors `searchService.ts`'s helper of the
 * same name. See that file's docstring for the design rationale
 * on the fast path's `running[0]` access pattern.
 */
const ensureChromeRunning = async (): Promise<number> => {
  const running = getRunningInstances();
  const first = running[0];
  if (first !== undefined) {
    return first.port;
  }
  const instance = await launchChrome({
    userDataDir: getChromeProfilePath(),
  });
  return instance.port;
};

/**
 * Parse a single `::ROOKIE::<TYPE>::<json>` line into a
 * `MarkerEvent`, or return `null` for a non-marker line.
 *
 * Same algorithm as `listenerBridge.ts`'s private parser. The
 * duplication is documented in the file header — it exists
 * because the bridge's parser is not exported, and the two
 * consumers are in the same folder with only one other sibling
 * sharing the same twenty lines.
 */
const parseMarkerLine = (line: string): MarkerEvent | null => {
  if (!line.startsWith(MARKER_PREFIX)) {
    return null;
  }
  const afterPrefix = line.slice(MARKER_PREFIX.length);
  const typeEnd = afterPrefix.indexOf('::');
  if (typeEnd === -1) {
    return null;
  }
  const type = afterPrefix.slice(0, typeEnd);
  if (type.length === 0) {
    return null;
  }
  const jsonText = afterPrefix.slice(typeEnd + 2);
  let payload: unknown;
  try {
    payload = jsonText.length > 0 ? JSON.parse(jsonText) : {};
  } catch {
    return null;
  }
  return { type, payload };
};

/**
 * Broadcast a parsed marker to every renderer window.
 *
 * Same shape and same channel as `listenerBridge.broadcast` — the
 * renderer cannot tell which module emitted a given marker.
 */
const broadcastMarker = (event: MarkerEvent): void => {
  const windows = BrowserWindow.getAllWindows();
  for (const win of windows) {
    if (win.isDestroyed()) continue;
    win.webContents.send(MARKER_EVENT_CHANNEL, event);
  }
};

/**
 * Extract the `savedCount` and `failedCount` fields from an
 * `EXTRACT_DONE` marker's payload.
 *
 * A malformed payload — a non-object, a missing `savedCount`, a
 * negative value — yields zeros for both fields. The Python side
 * is expected to emit a well-formed payload; the guard exists so
 * a bug in the Python side produces a smaller count, not a crash
 * in the main process.
 *
 * The two counts are returned together because they come from the
 * same marker and are consumed together — a `savedCount` without
 * its `failedCount` would give the caller no way to know whether
 * the extraction partially succeeded or just ran on a short URL
 * list.
 */
const extractDoneCounts = (
  payload: unknown
): { savedCount: number; failedCount: number } => {
  if (typeof payload !== 'object' || payload === null) {
    return { savedCount: 0, failedCount: 0 };
  }
  const p = payload as Record<string, unknown>;
  const saved = p['savedCount'];
  const failed = p['failedCount'];
  return {
    savedCount:
      typeof saved === 'number' && saved >= 0 ? saved : 0,
    failedCount:
      typeof failed === 'number' && failed >= 0 ? failed : 0,
  };
};

/**
 * The deterministic task ID for an extraction operation on a
 * given topic. Exported so a future session's cancel handler (or
 * a diagnostic tool) can compute the same ID without duplicating
 * the naming convention.
 *
 * The task ID is what `createProcessManager` and
 * `disposeProcessManager` (and the manager registry) key on, so
 * any caller that needs to look up the extraction's manager must
 * agree on this value exactly.
 *
 * See the file header for the reasoning behind using a distinct
 * suffix from `searchService`'s `${topicId}-search`.
 */
export const getExtractionTaskId = (topicId: string): string => {
  return `${topicId}-extract`;
};

// =============================================================================
//  Public entry point
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Run an extraction operation and return the saved/failed counts,
 * or a structured failure.
 *
 * See the file header for the full flow. The function never
 * rejects — a failure is reported via the resolved result's
 * `success: false` and `kind` fields.
 *
 * The per-URL progress is delivered to the renderer through the
 * marker channel, not through the returned promise. The promise
 * resolves once the child has exited with the terminal counts.
 */
export const runExtract = async (
  options: ExtractionServiceOptions
): Promise<ExtractionServiceResult> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  // Two guards, both of which would produce confusing downstream
  // errors if allowed through. An empty URL list cannot be
  // extracted; an empty topic ID cannot be used as a task ID (the
  // manager's constructor rejects it with a less-clear message).
  if (options.urls.length === 0) {
    return {
      success: false,
      savedCount: 0,
      failedCount: 0,
      kind: 'error',
      error: 'No URLs provided for extraction.',
    };
  }
  if (options.topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return {
      success: false,
      savedCount: 0,
      failedCount: 0,
      kind: 'error',
      error: 'Topic ID is required.',
    };
  }

  // ---- Ensure Chrome is running ----
  // The port is returned to the Python script as a CLI argument.
  // If Chrome cannot be launched, the whole operation fails
  // before any Python process is spawned — an early, clear
  // failure instead of a confusing "could not connect to port
  // NNNN" from the Python side.
  let port: number;
  try {
    port = await ensureChromeRunning();
  } catch (err) {
    return {
      success: false,
      savedCount: 0,
      failedCount: 0,
      kind: 'error',
      error: `Could not launch Chrome: ${
        err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err)
      }`,
    };
  }

  // ---- Set up the process manager ----
  const taskId = getExtractionTaskId(options.topicId);

  // Dispose any manager that survived a previous operation on
  // this topic. A stale manager could happen if the previous
  // extraction crashed without going through `settle`, or if the
  // caller cancelled but did not dispose. Disposing is a no-op
  // when no manager exists for this task ID.
  disposeProcessManager(taskId);

  const manager = createProcessManager(taskId);

  return new Promise<ExtractionServiceResult>((resolve) => {
    let settled = false;
    // The terminal counts are populated when the EXTRACT_DONE
    // marker arrives. `sawExtractDone` is what distinguishes
    // "the batch ran but saved nothing" from "the batch never
    // started" when the exit code is 1.
    let lastSavedCount = 0;
    let lastFailedCount = 0;
    let sawExtractDone = false;

    /**
     * Finalize the operation: mark as settled, dispose the
     * manager (which kills the child if it is still running),
     * resolve the promise. Idempotent — a second call is a no-op.
     *
     * The manager is disposed BEFORE the resolve because
     * disposing is what stops any pending child from continuing
     * to emit events into a promise that has already resolved.
     */
    const settle = (result: ExtractionServiceResult): void => {
      if (settled) return;
      settled = true;
      disposeProcessManager(taskId);
      resolve(result);
    };

    // ---- stdout handler ----
    // Each line that parses as a marker is broadcast to every
    // renderer window. EXTRACT_DONE markers also update the
    // local counts so the exit handler can return them, and
    // set the `sawExtractDone` flag.
    manager.on('stdout', (line: string) => {
      const marker = parseMarkerLine(line);
      if (marker === null) return;
      broadcastMarker(marker);
      if (marker.type === 'EXTRACT_DONE') {
        const counts = extractDoneCounts(marker.payload);
        lastSavedCount = counts.savedCount;
        lastFailedCount = counts.failedCount;
        sawExtractDone = true;
      }
    });

    // ---- exit handler ----
    // The exit code classifies the outcome. `code` is `number |
    // null` — a null arrives when the process was terminated by a
    // signal rather than exiting on its own.
    manager.on(
      'exit',
      (code: number | null, _signal: string | null) => {
        if (code === 0) {
          settle({
            success: true,
            savedCount: lastSavedCount,
            failedCount: lastFailedCount,
            kind: 'success',
          });
          return;
        }
        if (code === EXIT_SKIPPED || code === EXIT_CAPTCHA_SKIP) {
          settle({
            success: false,
            savedCount: lastSavedCount,
            failedCount: lastFailedCount,
            kind: 'user-skip',
            error: 'Extraction skipped by user.',
          });
          return;
        }
        if (code === EXIT_STOPPED) {
          settle({
            success: false,
            savedCount: lastSavedCount,
            failedCount: lastFailedCount,
            kind: 'user-stop',
            error: 'Extraction stopped by user.',
          });
          return;
        }
        // Non-zero, non-user codes. Distinguish "the batch ran
        // but saved nothing" from "the batch never started" by
        // whether the terminal marker was seen.
        if (sawExtractDone && lastSavedCount === 0) {
          settle({
            success: false,
            savedCount: 0,
            failedCount: lastFailedCount,
            kind: 'no-documents',
            error: 'Extraction completed but saved no documents.',
          });
          return;
        }
        settle({
          success: false,
          savedCount: lastSavedCount,
          failedCount: lastFailedCount,
          kind: 'error',
          error: `Extraction process exited with code ${
            code ?? 'unknown'
          }.`,
        });
      }
    );

    // ---- error handler ----
    // The manager's `error` event fires when the child fails to
    // spawn (ENOENT, EACCES) or encounters a runtime error.
    manager.on('error', (err: Error) => {
      settle({
        success: false,
        savedCount: lastSavedCount,
        failedCount: lastFailedCount,
        kind: 'error',
        error: err.message,
      });
    });

    // ---- timeout handler ----
    // Fires when the caller set a timeout and the child exceeded
    // it. `runExtract` does not set one by default, so this path
    // only fires if a future caller overrides the default.
    manager.on('timeout', () => {
      settle({
        success: false,
        savedCount: lastSavedCount,
        failedCount: lastFailedCount,
        kind: 'error',
        error: 'Extraction timed out.',
      });
    });

    // ---- killed handler ----
    // Fires when the manager's `kill()` terminates the child
    // directly (a hard cancel that bypasses the graceful
    // stdin-based Skip/Stop flow). Treating it as a user skip is
    // the correct semantic — the user asked for the operation to
    // end, and the operation ended.
    manager.on('killed', () => {
      settle({
        success: false,
        savedCount: lastSavedCount,
        failedCount: lastFailedCount,
        kind: 'user-skip',
        error: 'Extraction was cancelled.',
      });
    });

    // ---- Build the arguments ----
    // The Python subcommand takes the operation name, the
    // operation's inputs, and the debug port. `--control-mode`
    // is always passed — the whole point of this operation is
    // the marker stream that goes back to the renderer.
    //
    // The URL list is joined with commas because `main.py`'s
    // `--urls` flag accepts a comma-separated string and splits
    // it on its own side. A URL that contains a comma would
    // break the split — a rare but real possibility for a URL
    // with a comma in its query string. The Python side's
    // handler (`_run_extract` in `main.py`) uses a simple
    // `.split(",")` rather than a URL-aware parser, so the
    // contract is exactly "comma-separated list". A future
    // session that needed to support URLs with commas would
    // change the argument shape to a JSON-encoded array and
    // update `main.py`'s handler in the same change.
    const args: string[] = [
      'extract',
      '--topic-id', options.topicId,
      '--urls', options.urls.join(','),
      '--port', String(port),
      '--control-mode',
    ];

    const startOptions: ProcessOptions = { args };
    if (options.captchaPolicy !== undefined) {
      startOptions.captchaPolicy = options.captchaPolicy;
    }

    // ---- Spawn ----
    // `start()` resolves once the child is spawned. Its promise
    // can reject if the spawn call itself throws; the catch
    // converts a rejection into the same `settle` the error
    // handler uses, so the caller always sees a resolved result.
    void manager.start(startOptions).catch((err: unknown) => {
      settle({
        success: false,
        savedCount: 0,
        failedCount: 0,
        kind: 'error',
        error: err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err),
      });
    });
  });
};

// =============================================================================
//  Default export
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Every export bundled under one default object, matching the
 * convention of the sibling service modules (`searchService`,
 * `chromeLauncher`, `childProcessManager`). A caller that prefers
 * named imports uses `import { runExtract } from ...`; a caller
 * that prefers the default form uses
 * `import extractionService from ...` and destructures.
 */
export default {
  runExtract,
  getExtractionTaskId,
};
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/services/listenerBridge.ts">
/**
 * listenerBridge — the marker-stream bridge for the automation pipeline.
 *
 * Every Python script the main process spawns writes two kinds of
 * output to stdout:
 *
 *   1. Human-readable log lines. Plain text, no prefix. Written by
 *      `core.utils.log_info` / `log_warning` / `log_error`. The
 *      bridge ignores these — they are for the developer who reads
 *      the terminal, not for the GUI.
 *
 *   2. Structured markers. Lines of the exact form
 *
 *          ::ROOKIE::<TYPE>::<compact-json>\n
 *
 *      Written by `core.output.emit_marker`. Every marker carries a
 *      marker type string (`SEARCH_PROGRESS`, `EXTRACT_DONE`,
 *      `CAPTCHA_REQUIRED`, and so on) and a JSON payload whose shape
 *      is documented per-marker-type in `core/output.py`. The bridge
 *      parses these lines and forwards them to the renderer.
 *
 * This module's job is narrow and specific:
 *
 *     ATTACH  → to a spawned Python child process's stdout
 *     BUFFER  → partial lines across stream chunk boundaries
 *     PARSE   → each complete line into {type, payload}
 *     BROADCAST → to every renderer window on one IPC channel
 *
 * It does NOT:
 *
 *   - spawn processes (that is `childProcessManager.ts`)
 *   - know when a process exits (that is the caller's concern; this
 *     module detaches automatically when the child's stdout closes)
 *   - interpret the payload (that is the renderer's concern)
 *   - validate that a marker's payload matches its declared type
 *     (again the renderer's concern — the bridge is a transport)
 *   - handle stderr (Python's log output goes to stdout; stderr is
 *     reserved for uncaught tracebacks, which the caller logs)
 *   - map exit codes to anything (that is the caller's concern —
 *     `childProcessManager` sees the exit event and classifies it;
 *     the bridge only sees stdout)
 *
 * Why the bridge exists as a separate module:
 *
 *   Without it, every service that spawns Python — searchService,
 *   extractionService, and later the writer and validation
 *   services — would duplicate the same parsing logic: buffering
 *   chunks, splitting on `\n`, stripping `\r`, checking for the
 *   `::ROOKIE::` prefix, splitting on the second `::`, calling
 *   `JSON.parse` inside a try/catch, and calling
 *   `webContents.send` on every renderer window. That is roughly
 *   forty lines of fiddly code per service, and it would drift.
 *   The bridge centralises it into one place where it can be
 *   tested once and trusted everywhere.
 *
 * The single-channel design:
 *
 *   The bridge emits every marker over one IPC channel,
 *   `research:marker`, with the marker type carried inside the
 *   event payload as a field rather than encoded in the channel
 *   name. This is deliberate. A design that used one channel per
 *   marker type (`research:progress`, `research:result`,
 *   `research:captcha`, …) would require every new marker type to
 *   be added to three places: `core/output.py`, this file, and the
 *   preload's channel allowlist. The single-channel design requires
 *   adding a new marker type to exactly one place — the renderer's
 *   dispatcher — and everything else flows through unchanged.
 *
 *   The trade-off is that the renderer has to `switch` on the type
 *   string instead of registering a handler per channel. That switch
 *   is a single function in one place, and it is the correct place
 *   for the logic — the renderer is the only layer that knows what
 *   to do with a `SEARCH_RESULT` versus a `CAPTCHA_REQUIRED`.
 *
 * Line buffering — why the bridge does not assume one chunk = one
 * line:
 *
 *   Node's `stream.on('data')` fires whenever the underlying OS
 *   pipe has bytes available. A single `print(...)` in Python can
 *   arrive at the bridge as:
 *
 *     * one chunk (the common case for short lines)
 *     * two or more chunks (a long line split across the OS pipe
 *       buffer boundary)
 *     * a chunk containing the tail of one line and the head of
 *       the next
 *
 *   The bridge therefore maintains a per-stream text buffer. On
 *   each `data` event, it appends the incoming chunk to the buffer
 *   and then drains every complete line (terminated by `\n`) from
 *   the front. Any trailing text without a newline stays in the
 *   buffer until the next chunk arrives — or until the stream
 *   ends, at which point the bridge flushes whatever is left
 *   (a marker emitted without a trailing newline is unusual but
 *   possible if the process is killed mid-write).
 *
 * The CRLF handling:
 *
 *   Python on Windows writes `\r\n` for a `print(...)` by default
 *   when the stream is in text mode. The bridge strips a trailing
 *   `\r` from every line before parsing, so a marker emitted on
 *   Windows parses exactly as it would on Linux.
 *
 * The auto-detach on stream end:
 *
 *   When the child process exits, its stdout stream emits `end`.
 *   The bridge listens for that event and detaches its listeners
 *   automatically. The caller does NOT have to remember to detach
 *   — a spawned child that runs to completion cleans up its
 *   listeners on its own. The `attachToProcess` function still
 *   RETURNS a manual detach function, so a caller that wants to
 *   detach early (a cancellation path that kills the process
 *   before stdout closes) has a way to do so.
 *
 * Why the module is a singleton, and why that is fine:
 *
 *   Node's module cache guarantees that this file is evaluated
 *   exactly once per process. Every service that imports
 *   `attachToProcess` gets the same function, closing over the
 *   same module-level state. There is no class instance to
 *   construct, no `getInstance()` to call, no factory — the module
 *   itself IS the singleton.
 *
 *   The one piece of module-level state is `MARKER_EVENT_CHANNEL`
 *   — a constant string. Every marker emitted by every child
 *   process travels over that one channel. The bridge keeps no
 *   per-process state at the module level; the buffer lives inside
 *   the `attachToProcess` closure, one per spawned process. This
 *   is what lets two searches running in parallel (a user who
 *   starts a second topic while the first is still researching)
 *   have independent buffers without interfering with each other.
 *
 * Contract with the renderer:
 *
 *   The renderer's preload exposes `window.electronAPI.on(channel,
 *   callback)` — a generic listener registration (see
 *   `src/main/preload.ts`). A renderer component that wants to
 *   receive markers calls:
 *
 *       window.electronAPI.on('research:marker', (event) => {
 *         // event is a MarkerEvent: { type, payload }
 *       });
 *
 *   The preload does not type or validate the event — it is passed
 *   through verbatim. Type information flows from this module's
 *   exported `MarkerEvent` interface, which the renderer-side
 *   dispatcher should narrow at runtime before using any payload
 *   field.
 *
 * Contract with `core/output.py`:
 *
 *   The marker type strings this module's `MarkerType` union
 *   declares are the SAME strings that `core/output.py` emits.
 *   That file is the authoritative source — if a new marker type
 *   is ever added to the pipeline, it is added to
 *   `core/output.py` first, then to this file's union, then to the
 *   renderer's dispatcher. The bridge will forward a marker whose
 *   type is not in the union (it uses the type string as an opaque
 *   value), so a new marker type added only to `core/output.py`
 *   will reach the renderer intact — the union in this file is a
 *   documentation aid, not a runtime filter.
 */

import { BrowserWindow } from 'electron';
import type { ChildProcess } from 'node:child_process';

// =============================================================================
//  Public types
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The marker types the WriteSpeare pipeline currently emits.
 *
 * Every string in this union is a constant declared in
 * `apps/desktop/src/resources/scripts/core/output.py`. That file is
 * the authoritative source for these strings; this union is a
 * mirror that exists so a TypeScript consumer — the renderer's
 * dispatcher, a test, a future admin tool — has a named type to
 * switch on.
 *
 * A marker whose type is not a member of this union is still
 * forwarded by the bridge. The bridge uses `MarkerType | string`
 * as the type of the `type` field specifically so that a marker
 * added to `core/output.py` without a corresponding update here
 * does not get silently dropped.
 */
export type MarkerType =
  | 'TASK_START'
  | 'TASK_END'
  | 'SEARCH_PROGRESS'
  | 'SEARCH_RESULT'
  | 'EXTRACT_PROGRESS'
  | 'EXTRACT_DONE'
  | 'WRITER_PROGRESS'
  | 'WRITER_DONE'
  | 'VALIDATION_PROGRESS'
  | 'VALIDATION_DONE'
  | 'CAPTCHA_REQUIRED'
  | 'CAPTCHA_SOLVED'
  | 'MANUAL_ACTION';

/**
 * A parsed marker event, ready to cross the IPC boundary.
 *
 * The `type` field is typed as `MarkerType | string` rather than
 * `MarkerType` alone, for the forward-compatibility reason
 * documented on `MarkerType` above: a marker emitted by a script
 * that adds a type this union does not yet know about will still
 * reach the renderer with its type string intact.
 *
 * The `payload` field is typed as `unknown`, not a discriminated
 * union over `type`. A discriminated union would force the bridge
 * to know the shape of every marker's payload — a coupling that
 * would require editing this file every time `core/output.py`
 * changes a payload. Instead, the payload is opaque at this layer
 * and each consumer narrows it: the renderer's dispatcher knows
 * that a `SEARCH_RESULT` carries a `candidates` array, the
 * `CAPTCHA_REQUIRED` handler knows it carries a `prompt` string,
 * and so on. The bridge does not need to know any of that.
 */
export interface MarkerEvent {
  type: MarkerType | string;
  payload: unknown;
}

// =============================================================================
//  Constants
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The single IPC channel over which every marker reaches the
 * renderer. See the file header for the rationale behind using one
 * channel instead of one-per-marker-type.
 *
 * The channel name is namespaced to `research` because the majority
 * of markers come from the search and extraction stages, and because
 * the plan's file tree places this module under
 * `features/research/services/`. The `writer` and `validation`
 * stages ALSO emit markers over this same channel — the renderer's
 * dispatcher routes them to their respective stores by marker type,
 * not by channel.
 *
 * Exported so that the preload and any test can reference the
 * channel by name without hard-coding the string literal. Under
 * the current architecture, the preload's `on(channel, callback)`
 * method is a generic listener that takes the channel as an
 * argument, so no code outside this module and the renderer's
 * subscription call needs to name the channel — but exporting the
 * constant is the correct hygiene for the day a wrapper like
 * `onMarker(callback)` is added on top of the generic listener.
 */
export const MARKER_EVENT_CHANNEL = 'research:marker';

/**
 * The literal prefix that every marker line starts with. Must match
 * `core/output.py`'s marker emission, which writes
 * `f"::ROOKIE::{marker_type}::{json_payload}"`.
 *
 * Declared as a local constant rather than imported from a shared
 * location because the two sides of this contract are one Python
 * file and one TypeScript file, and the plan's tree provides no
 * shared constants module that spans the two languages. If the
 * prefix ever changes, both files change in the same commit — a
 * fact that is worth the small duplication.
 */
const MARKER_PREFIX = '::ROOKIE::';

// =============================================================================
//  Public API
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Attach the bridge to a spawned Python child process's stdout,
 * and return a cleanup function that detaches it.
 *
 * The child is expected to have been spawned with its stdout piped
 * — the `stdio: ['pipe', 'pipe', 'pipe']` configuration (or its
 * equivalent). A child with no stdout stream (a process spawned
 * with `stdio: 'ignore'`, or one whose stdout was set to `null`)
 * produces a no-op cleanup function and no listeners.
 *
 * Behavior:
 *
 *   1. Attaches a `data` listener to the child's stdout. Each time
 *      the underlying pipe delivers bytes, the bridge appends them
 *      to a per-process buffer, then drains every complete line
 *      from the front of the buffer. Each complete line is passed
 *      to the parser; a parsed marker is forwarded to every
 *      renderer window.
 *
 *   2. Attaches an `end` listener that fires when the child's
 *      stdout closes (which happens when the child exits, or when
 *      the parent closes its end of the pipe). On `end`, the
 *      bridge flushes whatever partial line remains in the buffer,
 *      then removes both listeners.
 *
 *   3. Returns a cleanup function that removes the listeners
 *      immediately, without waiting for `end`. A caller that kills
 *      the child early (a user cancellation, a timeout) can call
 *      this to release the listeners right away. After the
 *      cleanup function has been called once, calling it again is
 *      a no-op.
 *
 * The bridge does NOT listen for the child's `exit` or `close`
 * events. Those are the caller's concern — `childProcessManager`
 * (a sibling of this module in the plan's file tree) classifies
 * the exit code and reports a terminal status. The bridge only
 * cares about stdout's content.
 *
 * Concurrency:
 *
 *   A caller that spawns two Python processes in parallel (two
 *   topics researching simultaneously) calls `attachToProcess`
 *   twice — once per child. Each call creates an independent
 *   buffer that closes over its own locals; the two calls do not
 *   interfere. The bridge broadcasts on the same channel either
 *   way — the renderer distinguishes the two streams by the
 *   `topicId` field carried in each marker's payload.
 *
 * @param child - The spawned Python child process.
 * @returns A cleanup function that detaches the bridge's
 *          listeners. Safe to call more than once.
 */
export const attachToProcess = (child: ChildProcess): (() => void) => {
  const stdout = child.stdout;
  if (!stdout) {
    // The child was spawned without a piped stdout. Nothing to
    // bridge; return a no-op cleanup so the caller's uniform
    // "detach when done" pattern does not need a null check.
    return () => undefined;
  }

  // ---- Per-process state ----
  // The buffer and the `detached` flag live inside this closure,
  // so a second call to `attachToProcess` gets its own copy. See
  // the "Concurrency" note above.
  let buffer = '';
  let detached = false;

  // ---- Drain helper ----
  // Given the accumulated buffer text, split it on `\n` and process
  // every complete line. Returns the trailing (unterminated) text
  // that should remain in the buffer for the next chunk.
  //
  // The function is written as a loop rather than a `split('\n')`
  // because `split` allocates a temporary array for the whole
  // buffer on every chunk — a small but unnecessary allocation when
  // a large chunk carries many short lines. The loop slices the
  // buffer one line at a time and discards each after processing.
  const drain = (text: string): string => {
    let remaining = text;
    while (true) {
      const newlineIndex = remaining.indexOf('\n');
      if (newlineIndex === -1) {
        // No complete line remains. Everything from here to the
        // end of the buffer stays for the next chunk.
        return remaining;
      }
      // Extract the line (without its terminating \n).
      let line = remaining.slice(0, newlineIndex);
      // Strip a trailing \r (CRLF from Windows Python's text mode).
      if (line.endsWith('\r')) {
        line = line.slice(0, -1);
      }
      remaining = remaining.slice(newlineIndex + 1);
      // Process the line — non-marker lines are ignored inside.
      processLine(line);
    }
  };

  // ---- Data handler ----
  const onData = (chunk: string | Buffer): void => {
    if (detached) return;
    const text = typeof chunk === 'string' ? chunk : chunk.toString('utf-8');
    buffer += text;
    buffer = drain(buffer);
  };
