  // ---- End handler ----
  // When the child's stdout closes, flush whatever partial text
  // remains (a marker written without a trailing newline, which
  // happens if the process was killed mid-write). Then detach the
  // listeners so the stream can be garbage-collected.
  const onEnd = (): void => {
    if (detached) return;
    if (buffer.length > 0) {
      let line = buffer;
      if (line.endsWith('\r')) {
        line = line.slice(0, -1);
      }
      buffer = '';
      processLine(line);
    }
    detach();
  };

  // ---- Detach ----
  // Idempotent: the `detached` flag makes a second call a no-op.
  // The `off` calls (rather than `removeListener` or `removeAllListeners`)
  // remove only the two listeners this specific attachment added,
  // leaving any other listeners on the stream untouched. That
  // matters because a caller may have attached its own `data`
  // handler to the same stdout (for logging, or for a progress
  // counter) — this module must not remove them.
  const detach = (): void => {
    if (detached) return;
    detached = true;
    stdout.off('data', onData);
    stdout.off('end', onEnd);
  };

  // ---- Attach ----
  stdout.on('data', onData);
  stdout.on('end', onEnd);

  return detach;
};

// =============================================================================
//  Internal — line processing
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Process one complete line of child-process stdout.
 *
 * Non-marker lines are silently ignored. A line is a marker if and
 * only if it starts with `::ROOKIE::`. Everything else — the log
 * lines produced by `core.utils.log_info` / `log_warning` /
 * `log_error`, blank lines, Python's own tracebacks on stdout (if
 * any) — is skipped without any action.
 *
 * Silently skipping non-marker lines is the correct behavior
 * because:
 *
 *   1. The bridge is not a logger. Its job is to route structured
 *      progress, not to reproduce the developer-facing log in the
 *      GUI. The renderer has its own logging (`shared/utils/logger.ts`)
 *      for anything that belongs in the GUI.
 *
 *   2. The alternative — forwarding every line as a generic
 *      "output" event — would drown the renderer's dispatcher in
 *      noise that has no defined shape and no consumer.
 */
const processLine = (line: string): void => {
  if (line.length === 0) return;
  if (!line.startsWith(MARKER_PREFIX)) return;
  const event = parseMarkerLine(line);
  if (event !== null) {
    broadcast(event);
  }
};

// =============================================================================
//  Internal — marker parsing
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Parse a single `::ROOKIE::<TYPE>::<json>` line into a
 * `MarkerEvent`.
 *
 * Returns `null` (and logs to the main-process console) if the
 * line is malformed in any way:
 *
 *   * No second `::` separator (a prefix but no type boundary).
 *   * An empty type string (`::ROOKIE::::<json>`, which the
 *     emitter never produces but a hand-written test might).
 *   * A JSON payload that does not parse.
 *
 * A malformed marker is dropped, not forwarded. The alternative —
 * forwarding a marker with an empty type or a garbage payload —
 * would push the validation burden into the renderer's dispatcher,
 * where a malformed marker would either be silently ignored (a
 * second failure point, harder to debug) or throw (a crash the
 * bridge could have prevented).
 *
 * The main-process console is the correct destination for these
 * errors. It is visible to the developer via the terminal that
 * launched Electron, it does not interrupt the renderer, and it
 * does not count against the renderer's error budget.
 *
 * Parsing algorithm:
 *
 *   Given the line `::ROOKIE::SEARCH_RESULT::{"topicId":"abc",...}`:
 *
 *     1. Strip the leading `::ROOKIE::` prefix.
 *     2. Find the next `::` — everything before it is the type.
 *        Everything after it is the JSON payload text.
 *     3. Parse the payload text with `JSON.parse`. The text is
 *        compact JSON, no trailing whitespace, no comments.
 *     4. Return `{ type, payload }`.
 */
const parseMarkerLine = (line: string): MarkerEvent | null => {
  const afterPrefix = line.slice(MARKER_PREFIX.length);
  const typeEnd = afterPrefix.indexOf('::');
  if (typeEnd === -1) {
    console.error(
      '[listenerBridge] malformed marker — no type separator:',
      line
    );
    return null;
  }

  const type = afterPrefix.slice(0, typeEnd);
  if (type.length === 0) {
    console.error(
      '[listenerBridge] malformed marker — empty type:',
      line
    );
    return null;
  }

  const jsonText = afterPrefix.slice(typeEnd + 2);
  let payload: unknown;
  try {
    payload = jsonText.length > 0 ? JSON.parse(jsonText) : {};
  } catch (err) {
    console.error(
      '[listenerBridge] malformed marker — invalid JSON:',
      err,
      'line:',
      line
    );
    return null;
  }

  return { type, payload };
};

// =============================================================================
//  Internal — broadcast
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Send a parsed marker to every renderer window.
 *
 * The loop over `BrowserWindow.getAllWindows()` handles the
 * multi-window case. The current app has exactly one window, but
 * the plan's architecture does not forbid a future session from
 * opening a second (a DevTools-only window, a side-by-side preview,
 * a modal window). The loop is cheap, and the alternative —
 * assuming a single window via `getAllWindows()[0]` — would
 * silently stop delivering events to a second window the moment
 * one exists.
 *
 * Each window is checked for `isDestroyed()` before `.send(...)`.
 * Electron throws if a message is sent to a destroyed webContents,
 * and the check is a one-line guard against an async race (a window
 * closing between the `getAllWindows()` call and the `send`).
 *
 * The send is fire-and-forget. Electron's IPC does not provide
 * delivery guarantees, and the bridge has no useful response to a
 * failure — the renderer's listener is either attached or it is
 * not, and there is no retry that would help. A failure is
 * therefore not caught; an Electron-internal error during `send`
 * would indicate a bug in Electron or a violation of its API
 * contract, not a runtime condition this module can recover from.
 */
const broadcast = (event: MarkerEvent): void => {
  const windows = BrowserWindow.getAllWindows();
  for (const win of windows) {
    if (win.isDestroyed()) continue;
    win.webContents.send(MARKER_EVENT_CHANNEL, event);
  }
};
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/services/portAllocator.ts">
/**
 * portAllocator — Chrome DevTools Protocol debug-port allocation.
 *
 * Every Chrome instance the WriteSpeare pipeline drives over CDP
 * needs its own dedicated debug port. Two Chrome instances cannot
 * share a port — the second one fails to open its debug server
 * and the pipeline loses control of it. The port therefore has to
 * be unique per running instance, freed when the instance is
 * killed, and re-usable by the next instance that needs one.
 *
 * This module owns that allocation. It is the single source of
 * truth for "which ports are currently in use" in the main
 * process.
 *
 * The problem it solves:
 *
 *   The plan's user journey allows two topics to research in
 *   parallel — a user who starts a second topic while the first is
 *   still running gets two concurrent Chrome instances. Each one
 *   needs a port, and neither can know in advance which port the
 *   other chose. A naive "just use 9222" approach would produce
 *   two Chrome launches competing for the same port: one wins, one
 *   silently hands off to the winner, and the second Chrome's
 *   automation loses its debug connection. The pipeline would
 *   stall with no clear error.
 *
 *   The allocator prevents that by handing out a distinct port to
 *   each caller until the caller explicitly releases it. Ports are
 *   held for the entire lifetime of a Chrome instance — from the
 *   moment the caller asks for one until the moment the Chrome
 *   process is killed — so two concurrent instances always get two
 *   distinct ports.
 *
 * The allocation strategy:
 *
 *   Ports are drawn from the range [CHROME_BASE_PORT,
 *   CHROME_BASE_PORT + PORT_RANGE_SIZE). The allocator scans the
 *   range from the base upward and returns the first port that is
 *   not currently in use. This is the standard "find the first free
 *   slot in a fixed range" algorithm — cheap, deterministic (a
 *   fresh allocation returns the base port when nothing is held),
 *   and simple to reason about.
 *
 *   If every port in the range is in use, the allocator throws.
 *   The alternative — extending the range indefinitely — would
 *   hide a leak: a caller that failed to release its port would
 *   accumulate ports forever, and the failure would surface not as
 *   a clear error but as Chrome instances launching on ports far
 *   outside anything the firewall or a user's mental model
 *   expects. A bounded range plus a throw on exhaustion makes the
 *   leak visible the moment it happens.
 *
 *   The range size (PORT_RANGE_SIZE) is chosen to be generously
 *   larger than the number of concurrent Chrome instances the plan
 *   ever contemplates. The plan's user journey shows one or two
 *   parallel topics; a hundred-port range leaves two orders of
 *   magnitude of headroom.
 *
 * Port re-use:
 *
 *   A released port is immediately available for the next
 *   allocation. The allocator does NOT wait for the operating
 *   system's TIME_WAIT state to expire before re-using a port,
 *   because a Chrome debug port is a listening socket on a live
 *   process — the OS does not put it into TIME_WAIT on the
 *   process's exit. TIME_WAIT applies to outbound connections
 *   that closed, not to listening sockets. A fresh Chrome process
 *   can bind to the same port immediately after the previous one
 *   exits, and the allocator's behavior (return the port as soon
 *   as the caller releases it) matches that reality.
 *
 * Thread safety:
 *
 *   The allocator is used exclusively in the main process, which
 *   is single-threaded in Node's event-loop model. Two `await`ed
 *   call sequences interleave at their `await` points, but the
 *   `allocatePort` and `releasePort` functions are both
 *   synchronous — no `await`, no callback, no microtask boundary.
 *   That means the allocator's state cannot be corrupted by two
 *   concurrent callers: each function runs to completion before
 *   the next one starts. No locking is needed.
 *
 * What this module does NOT do:
 *
 *   - It does not launch Chrome. The `chromeLauncher.ts` module
 *     (a sibling in the plan's tree) is responsible for that; it
 *     calls `allocatePort()` before launching and `releasePort()`
 *     after killing the Chrome process.
 *
 *   - It does not track which port belongs to which Chrome
 *     instance. The allocator knows only "in use" or "not in use";
 *     the caller that received the port remembers what it launched
 *     on it. This keeps the allocator's state minimal and makes it
 *     trivially replaceable — a future session that wants to add
 *     per-port metadata would extend the caller, not this module.
 *
 *   - It does not probe the OS to check whether a port is
 *     physically free. A port that Chrome has bound on a live
 *     process will not be returned by `allocatePort` because it
 *     is marked in use; a port that an unrelated process on the
 *     user's machine has bound is outside this module's concern
 *     (the range 9222+ is not a well-known port range that other
 *     software typically uses). If Chrome's launch fails because
 *     its debug port is occupied by something else, the failure
 *     surfaces in `chromeLauncher.ts`, and the caller's error path
 *     is what recovers.
 *
 *   - It does not persist allocations across app restarts. Ports
 *     are process-local. When the app exits, every Chrome instance
 *     it spawned is killed and every port is freed. A fresh launch
 *     starts with an empty allocation set.
 */

// =============================================================================
//  Constants
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The first port the allocator will hand out, and the lowest port
 * in the allocation range.
 *
 * 9222 is Chrome's own default remote-debugging port — the port a
 * developer gets when they launch Chrome with
 * `--remote-debugging-port=9222` and no other value. Choosing it
 * as the base for WriteSpeare means:
 *
 *   1. A developer who manually starts Chrome on 9222 and then
 *      runs the app can be told "connect to port 9222" without a
 *      translation step.
 *
 *   2. The Python side's `core/browser.py` declares the same
 *      default (`DEFAULT_DEBUG_PORT = 9222`). A caller that does
 *      not specify a port on the Python side gets the same
 *      behavior as a caller that asks the allocator for a port
 *      and gets 9222.
 *
 *   3. The port is outside the well-known range (0–1023), so no
 *      privileged access is required to bind it.
 */
export const CHROME_BASE_PORT = 9222;

/**
 * The number of ports in the allocation range, starting at
 * `CHROME_BASE_PORT`.
 *
 * The range is `[CHROME_BASE_PORT, CHROME_BASE_PORT +
 * PORT_RANGE_SIZE)` — one hundred ports, 9222 through 9321. This
 * is vastly larger than the number of concurrent Chrome instances
 * the plan contemplates (one or two in the common case), so
 * exhaustion of the range indicates a bug — a caller that never
 * released a port — rather than a legitimate capacity limit.
 *
 * The bound exists so that a leak surfaces as a clear error
 * (`throw` in `allocatePort`) rather than as a slow drift into
 * unusable port numbers. See the file header for the full
 * rationale.
 */
export const PORT_RANGE_SIZE = 100;

/**
 * The highest port in the allocation range, inclusive.
 *
 * Derived from the two constants above so that changing
 * `PORT_RANGE_SIZE` propagates correctly without a second edit.
 *
 * The `- 1` is what makes the value inclusive: with
 * `CHROME_BASE_PORT = 9222` and `PORT_RANGE_SIZE = 100`, the range
 * is `[9222, 9321]` — one hundred distinct port numbers, exactly
 * matching `PORT_RANGE_SIZE`.
 *
 * Module-private — a caller that wants the range's bounds should
 * compute them from the two exported constants. Not re-exported so
 * that a future change to the range size does not require auditing
 * external call sites.
 */
const MAX_PORT = CHROME_BASE_PORT + PORT_RANGE_SIZE - 1;

// =============================================================================
//  Module state
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The set of ports currently held by callers.
 *
 * A port that has been returned by `allocatePort` and not yet
 * released via `releasePort` is a member of this set. The set is
 * the allocator's single piece of mutable state — the entire
 * mechanism is "ask the set what is free, add on allocate, remove
 * on release".
 *
 * A `Set<number>` rather than a `boolean[]` sized to the range:
 * the set grows to exactly the size of the concurrent allocations
 * (one to three elements in practice) and its membership test is
 * O(1). An array indexed by `port - CHROME_BASE_PORT` would
 * allocate the full one-hundred-element backing store on first use
 * and offer no advantage over the set for the sizes involved.
 *
 * Not exported. A caller that needs to inspect the current
 * allocation state uses `getInUsePorts()`, which returns a
 * snapshot copy — see that function's docstring for why the copy
 * matters.
 */
const inUsePorts: Set<number> = new Set<number>();

// =============================================================================
//  Public API — allocation
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Reserve a port for a Chrome instance and return its number.
 *
 * The port is guaranteed to be unused by any other caller of this
 * module at the moment of return — the allocator scans for the
 * first free slot and marks it in use before returning. A
 * subsequent call returns a DIFFERENT port (until the first is
 * released), so two concurrent Chrome launches always get two
 * distinct ports.
 *
 * Throws if every port in the range is currently in use. See the
 * file header for why throwing is the correct response to
 * exhaustion (it surfaces a leak as a clear error) rather than
 * silently extending the range.
 *
 * Synchronous — no `await`, no callback. The caller can call this
 * from any code path, including a top-level statement or a
 * non-async function.
 *
 * @returns The allocated port number, in the range
 *          `[CHROME_BASE_PORT, CHROME_BASE_PORT + PORT_RANGE_SIZE)`.
 * @throws  `Error` if every port in the range is in use.
 */
export const allocatePort = (): number => {
  // Scan from the base upward. The loop is bounded by
  // `PORT_RANGE_SIZE` iterations — if no free port is found in
  // that many tries, every port in the range is held.
  for (let i = 0; i < PORT_RANGE_SIZE; i += 1) {
    const candidate = CHROME_BASE_PORT + i;
    if (!inUsePorts.has(candidate)) {
      inUsePorts.add(candidate);
      return candidate;
    }
  }

  throw new Error(
    `portAllocator: no free port in the range ` +
      `[${CHROME_BASE_PORT}, ${MAX_PORT}]. ` +
      `${inUsePorts.size} port(s) in use. ` +
      `This usually indicates a caller that failed to release a port ` +
      `after killing its Chrome instance.`
  );
};

/**
 * Release a port back to the pool.
 *
 * After this call, the port is available for the next allocation.
 * The caller is expected to call this ONLY after the Chrome
 * process bound to the port has exited — the allocator does not
 * verify that, because it has no way to (it does not hold a
 * reference to any Chrome process, by design).
 *
 * Releasing a port that was never allocated, or that has already
 * been released, is a no-op. The alternative — throwing — would
 * create a footgun where a cleanup path that runs on both success
 * and failure has to track whether it has already released. A
 * `Set.delete` that returns `false` for a not-present value is
 * the correct semantic: the caller's intent ("this port should no
 * longer be held") is satisfied either way.
 *
 * Synchronous. Safe to call from any context.
 *
 * @param port - The port number previously returned by
 *               `allocatePort`. A port outside the allocation
 *               range is silently ignored — the same no-op
 *               semantic as releasing a port that was never
 *               allocated.
 */
export const releasePort = (port: number): void => {
  inUsePorts.delete(port);
};

/**
 * Release every port currently held.
 *
 * Intended for app-shutdown cleanup: when the app exits and the
 * `before-quit` handler has killed every Chrome instance, the
 * allocator's state is reset to empty so that a subsequent
 * allocation (in the unlikely event one happens during shutdown)
 * starts from a clean slate.
 *
 * Not intended as a per-operation cleanup. A caller that wants to
 * free a single port uses `releasePort`; calling `releaseAllPorts`
 * after an operation would free ports held by OTHER callers, which
 * is exactly the bug the allocator exists to prevent.
 *
 * Also useful in tests: a test that exercises the allocator can
 * call this in its `beforeEach` to guarantee a known starting
 * state without needing a fresh module instance.
 *
 * Synchronous. Idempotent — calling it when nothing is held is a
 * no-op.
 */
export const releaseAllPorts = (): void => {
  inUsePorts.clear();
};

// =============================================================================
//  Public API — introspection
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Return the set of ports currently in use.
 *
 * Returns a fresh `readonly number[]` on every call — a snapshot,
 * not a live view. The caller cannot mutate the allocator's
 * internal state by mutating the returned array, and the returned
 * array does not change if the allocator's state changes after
 * the call. Both properties are what a caller wants when
 * inspecting state for a log line or a test assertion.
 *
 * The array is sorted ascending so that a log line reading
 * "ports in use: 9222, 9224" is deterministic regardless of the
 * order in which the ports were allocated. The sort is a small
 * extra cost (at most one hundred elements) and pays for itself
 * the first time a developer reads the output.
 *
 * Exported for tests and for a future diagnostics endpoint. The
 * production code paths in the pipeline do not call this — they
 * hold references to their own allocated ports and do not need to
 * query the allocator.
 */
export const getInUsePorts = (): readonly number[] => {
  return Array.from(inUsePorts).sort((a, b) => a - b);
};
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/topics/pages/Topics.tsx">
/**
 * WriteSpeare — topics home page.
 *
 * This is the app's default authenticated view — the screen a
 * signed-in user lands on immediately after logging in, and the
 * one they see every time they open the app with an active
 * session. It has one job: show the user's topics, and give them
 * a way to create a new one.
 *
 * The page has four visual states, driven by the store's `status`
 * and `error` fields and by whether the `topics` array is empty:
 *
 *   1. Loading      — `status === 'loading'` and no topics are
 *                     currently shown. A centered spinner.
 *
 *   2. Error        — `status === 'error'`. A banner with the
 *                     error message and a "Try again" button that
 *                     re-runs `loadTopics()`.
 *
 *   3. Empty        — `status === 'idle'` and `topics.length === 0`.
 *                     A friendly empty state with a call to
 *                     action that opens the New Topic modal.
 *
 *   4. Populated    — `status === 'idle'` and `topics.length > 0`.
 *                     A responsive grid of `<TopicCard />`.
 *
 * The distinction between (1) and (3) matters: a list that is
 * empty while a fetch is in progress is not the same as a list
 * that is empty because the user has no topics. The former should
 * show a spinner; the latter should invite the user to create
 * their first topic. This page keys off `status === 'loading'`
 * for the spinner, not off `topics.length === 0`.
 *
 * What the page does NOT do:
 *
 *   - It does not open the topic's detail view. That is the
 *     `<TopicCard />`'s job — the card renders a link (or a
 *     button that navigates) to `/dashboard/topics/:id`. The
 *     page composes the cards into a grid and stops there.
 *
 *   - It does not delete or duplicate topics. Those operations
 *     are invoked from the card's own menu — the card has the
 *     topic object in hand and the actions are colocated with
 *     it. The page's role is to render the list, not to mediate
 *     every operation a topic can undergo.
 *
 *   - It does not paginate. The plan's topics endpoint returns
 *     the user's full list, and the plan does not describe a
 *     paginated view. A user with hundreds of topics would see
 *     all of them; if a future session wants pagination, the
 *     correct change is a new endpoint parameter and a pager
 *     component, not a change to this page's layout.
 *
 *   - It does not filter. The topics API accepts an optional
 *     `?status=` filter, and the store's `loadTopics` forwards
 *     it, but the plan does not describe a filter UI on the
 *     home page. If a future session adds one, the state for
 *     the current filter lives here and the fetch call grows
 *     the argument.
 *
 * The empty-state copy:
 *
 *   The empty state invites the user to create their first
 *   topic with a short prompt — "Create your first topic" —
 *   and a button that opens the same New Topic modal the
 *   header button opens. The design uses the same primary
 *   color and the same iconography as the rest of the app, so
 *   the empty state reads as part of the product rather than as
 *   a placeholder screen.
 *
 * Modal ownership:
 *
 *   The `NewTopicModal` is a controlled child of this page —
 *   the page owns the boolean that determines whether it is
 *   open, and the modal reports back through `onClose` when
 *   the user dismisses it or completes the create flow. This
 *   mirrors the pattern of `Sidebar` in `Layout` (the parent
 *   owns the state, the child is presentational), and it means
 *   the page can open the modal from two places (the header
 *   button and the empty-state button) without threading an
 *   imperative API through a ref.
 *
 *   If a future session wants to open the modal from a
 *   component outside this page, the correct change is to
 *   promote the modal to an imperative API (via the modal
 *   context that `shared/components/Modal.tsx` already
 *   provides) — but the two-call-sites-in-one-page case does
 *   not justify that yet.
 *
 * Forward references:
 *
 *   This file imports `TopicCard` and `NewTopicModal` from the
 *   siblings `../components/TopicCard.js` and
 *   `../components/NewTopicModal.js`. Both are scheduled for
 *   later steps in this session (#58 and #59 respectively) and
 *   do not exist yet. Their absence produces two `TS2307`
 *   errors on the import lines until they are created. This is
 *   the same forward-reference pattern that appeared in
 *   Session 5 (Sidebar and Layout waiting on `useAuth`) and in
 *   Session 4 (App.tsx waiting on every feature page).
 */

import { useEffect, useState } from 'react';
import {
  AlertCircle,
  FileText,
  Loader2,
  Plus,
} from 'lucide-react';

import { useTopics } from '../hooks/useTopics.js';
import TopicCard from '../components/TopicCard.js';
import NewTopicModal from '../components/NewTopicModal.js';

// ============================================================
//  Public types
// ============================================================

/**
 * The props accepted by `Topics`. Currently empty — the page
 * takes no props and derives everything from the store and the
 * router context.
 */
// eslint-disable-next-line @typescript-eslint/no-empty-object-type
export interface TopicsProps {
  // Reserved for future additions. See file header.
}

// ============================================================
//  Topics
// ============================================================

/**
 * The topics home page. See the file header for the page's four
 * visual states, its scope, and the reasoning behind the modal
 * ownership and the empty-state design.
 */
export const Topics = (_props: TopicsProps = {}) => {
  const { topics, status, error, loadTopics } = useTopics();

  // Whether the New Topic modal is currently open. Owned here
  // and passed to the modal as a controlled prop — see the file
  // header for the reasoning.
  const [isNewTopicModalOpen, setIsNewTopicModalOpen] = useState(false);

  // ---- Initial fetch ----
  // Load the user's topics on mount. The empty dependency array
  // means this runs once per mount of the Topics page. Navigating
  // between topics and their detail views unmounts and remounts
  // this page each time, so a subsequent visit re-fetches — a
  // deliberate choice for the current design. The store is not
  // persisted and the backend is the source of truth for the
  // list, so a fresh fetch on every mount keeps the view in sync
  // with whatever changes might have happened elsewhere.
  //
  // The `void` on the call discards the promise; the store's
  // `status` field is what the render reacts to, and the caller
  // has no further work to do when the promise resolves.
  useEffect(() => {
    void loadTopics();
  }, [loadTopics]);

  // ---- Modal handlers ----

  const openNewTopicModal = (): void => {
    setIsNewTopicModalOpen(true);
  };

  const closeNewTopicModal = (): void => {
    setIsNewTopicModalOpen(false);
  };

  // ---- Render ----
  return (
    <div className="mx-auto flex h-full w-full max-w-6xl flex-col px-6 py-8">
      {/* ---- Header ---- */}
      <header className="mb-8 flex flex-wrap items-center justify-between gap-4">
        <div>
          <h1 className="text-2xl font-semibold text-text dark:text-neutral-100">
            Topics
          </h1>
          <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-neutral-500 dark:text-neutral-400">
            Research, write, and validate your articles.
          </p>
        </div>

        <button
          type="button"
          onClick={openNewTopicModal}
          className="inline-flex items-center gap-2 rounded-lg bg-primary px-4 py-2 text-sm font-medium text-white transition-colors hover:bg-primary-dark focus-visible:outline-none"
        >
          <Plus size={18} aria-hidden="true" />
          <span>New Topic</span>
        </button>
      </header>

      {/* ---- Body ---- */}
      <div className="flex-1">
        <TopicsBody
          topics={topics}
          status={status}
          error={error}
          onRetry={() => void loadTopics()}
          onNewTopic={openNewTopicModal}
        />
      </div>

      {/* ---- New Topic modal ---- */}
      <NewTopicModal
        open={isNewTopicModalOpen}
        onClose={closeNewTopicModal}
      />
    </div>
  );
};

// ============================================================
//  TopicsBody — internal view
// ============================================================
//
//  The four visual states (loading, error, empty, populated) are
//  expressed as a small internal component rather than as a
//  sequence of ternaries in the page's JSX. The split keeps the
//  page's return value to two sections (header, body) and lets
//  the body's rendering be read as a flat sequence of
//  `if`-style early returns.

interface TopicsBodyProps {
  topics: ReturnType<typeof useTopics>['topics'];
  status: ReturnType<typeof useTopics>['status'];
  error: ReturnType<typeof useTopics>['error'];
  onRetry: () => void;
  onNewTopic: () => void;
}

const TopicsBody = ({
  topics,
  status,
  error,
  onRetry,
  onNewTopic,
}: TopicsBodyProps) => {
  // ---- 1. Loading ----
  // Shown when a fetch is in flight and there are no topics to
  // display. If topics are already loaded (a re-fetch is in
  // progress), the existing list is shown instead — see the
  // populated branch below.
  if (status === 'loading' && topics.length === 0) {
    return (
      <div className="flex h-64 items-center justify-center">
        <Loader2
          size={28}
          className="animate-spin text-primary"
          aria-hidden="true"
        />
        <span className="sr-only">Loading topics…</span>
      </div>
    );
  }

  // ---- 2. Error ----
  // Shown when the last fetch failed. The banner carries the
  // error message the store captured; the button re-runs the
  // fetch.
  if (status === 'error' && error) {
    return (
      <div
        role="alert"
        className="mx-auto flex max-w-lg flex-col items-center gap-3 rounded-xl border border-red-200 bg-red-50 p-6 text-center dark:border-red-900 dark:bg-red-950/60"
      >
        <AlertCircle
          size={24}
          className="text-red-600 dark:text-red-400"
          aria-hidden="true"
        />
        <p className="text-sm text-red-900 dark:text-red-100">{error}</p>
        <button
          type="button"
          onClick={onRetry}
          className="rounded-lg bg-red-600 px-4 py-2 text-sm font-medium text-white transition-colors hover:bg-red-700 focus-visible:outline-none"
        >
          Try again
        </button>
      </div>
    );
  }

  // ---- 3. Empty ----
  // Shown when the fetch succeeded but the user has no topics.
  // The call to action opens the same modal the header button
  // opens.
  if (topics.length === 0) {
    return (
      <div className="mx-auto flex max-w-md flex-col items-center gap-4 py-16 text-center">
        <div className="flex h-14 w-14 items-center justify-center rounded-2xl bg-neutral-100 text-neutral-500 dark:bg-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-400">
          <FileText size={28} aria-hidden="true" />
        </div>
        <div>
          <h2 className="text-lg font-semibold text-text dark:text-neutral-100">
            No topics yet
          </h2>
          <p className="mt-1 text-sm text-neutral-500 dark:text-neutral-400">
            Create your first topic to start the research and
            writing pipeline.
          </p>
        </div>
        <button
          type="button"
          onClick={onNewTopic}
          className="inline-flex items-center gap-2 rounded-lg bg-primary px-4 py-2 text-sm font-medium text-white transition-colors hover:bg-primary-dark focus-visible:outline-none"
        >
          <Plus size={18} aria-hidden="true" />
          <span>New Topic</span>
        </button>
      </div>
    );
  }

  // ---- 4. Populated ----
  // A responsive grid of topic cards. The grid collapses to one
  // column on narrow windows and expands to two or three columns
  // on wider ones — the app runs at a minimum of 1024 px per the
  // window's minWidth setting, which comfortably fits two
  // columns, and can expand to three on a large monitor.
  return (
    <div className="grid grid-cols-1 gap-4 sm:grid-cols-2 xl:grid-cols-3">
      {topics.map((topic) => (
        <TopicCard key={topic._id} topic={topic} />
      ))}
    </div>
  );
};

// ============================================================
//  Default export
// ============================================================

/**
 * The `Topics` component as the default export. `renderer/App.tsx`
 * imports this with the default form
 * (`import Topics from '.../Topics.js'`), so this is the export
 * that keeps the route tree resolving.
 */
export default Topics;
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/components/SearchProgress.tsx">
/**
 * SearchProgress — the live search panel for the Sources tab.
 *
 * Renders one of four visual states, driven by the research
 * hook's `status` field for the topic this panel was given:
 *
 *   1. IDLE (no candidates in any bucket, no error)
 *      A short invitation with a "Start Search" button. The
 *      button calls `runFullSearch(topic._id, topic.title,
 *      topic.keywords, bucketConfig)`, which runs four searches
 *      — one per time-range bucket — sequentially. As each
 *      search's markers arrive, the store's per-bucket handlers
 *      populate the candidate record and clear that bucket's
 *      progress slot, and this panel re-renders into the
 *      SEARCHING state until every bucket has finished.
 *
 *   2. SEARCHING (status === 'searching')
 *      A spinner with an overall progress bar ("N of M across
 *      four buckets — X%") and a secondary line naming the
 *      currently-active bucket and its own progress. The
 *      progress data comes from the hook's `searchProgress`
 *      field, which is a four-bucket record — each bucket's
 *      slot is either `null` (not running) or a live
 *      `SearchProgress` object carrying the page count, the
 *      count found so far, and the count requested. A "Cancel"
 *      button lets the user abort; it calls the hook's `cancel`
 *      action, which hard-kills the Python child (see
 *      `researchIPC.ts` for the graceful-vs-hard distinction).
 *
 *   3. ERROR (status === 'error')
 *      A red banner with the topic's error message and two
 *      buttons: "Retry" (re-runs `runFullSearch`) and "Dismiss"
 *      (clears the error without retrying).
 *
 *   4. COMPLETE (any candidates exist, status back to 'idle')
 *      A green summary line — "N candidates found" — with a
 *      "Search again" button. The candidate list itself is
 *      rendered below this panel by the sibling `SourceList`
 *      component; this panel only reports the count.
 *
 * ── Why the hook call now takes `topic._id` ─────────────────────
 *
 *   PR-1 of the store-isolation refactor (Q4.3: "refactor all
 *   feature stores to key by topicId in the same pass") changed
 *   `useResearch`'s signature from `useResearch()` to
 *   `useResearch(topicId)`. The hook now reads per-topic slots
 *   from the research store — `candidatesByTopic[topicId]`,
 *   `searchProgressByTopic[topicId]`, `statusByTopic[topicId]`,
 *   `errorByTopic[topicId]` — so two components rendering
 *   different topics see independent state and the same
 *   component re-renders only when its own topic's data changes.
 *
 *   This component always has the topic in hand — it receives
 *   the `Topic` object as its only prop — so passing `topic._id`
 *   to the hook is zero-friction.
 *
 * ── The bucket config derivation — a transient PR-1 measure ─────
 *
 *   `runFullSearch` takes a `BucketConfig` that names how many
 *   candidates each of the four time-range buckets should
 *   collect. The definitive source of that config is the
 *   topic's own `searchBuckets` field, which lands in PR-2's
 *   schema change (Item 3 / C2 — the bucket layout is the
 *   single source of truth for how many sources to collect).
 *
 *   Until PR-2 ships, this component derives a config from the
 *   topic's existing `sourceCount`: the total is split evenly
 *   across the four buckets, with any remainder placed on the
 *   first bucket so the four counts always sum to the topic's
 *   `sourceCount`. That even-split rule is the same rule PR-2's
 *   migration script uses for legacy topics ("synthesize
 *   searchBuckets from sourceCount split evenly across four
 *   buckets"), so the transient derivation here is consistent
 *   with the eventual schema value. When PR-2 lands, this
 *   derivation is replaced with a direct read of
 *   `topic.searchBuckets`; the rest of the component is
 *   unchanged.
 *
 * ── Why this panel is separate from SourceList ──────────────────
 *
 *   Same reasoning as before the refactor. The plan's Part 1.9
 *   file tree declares them as separate components, and the
 *   Sources tab renders them stacked (see `TopicDetail.tsx`).
 *   `SearchProgress` owns the ACTION (start a search, cancel it,
 *   retry it) and the live state that belongs to the search
 *   itself; `SourceList` owns the SELECTION UI (checkbox list,
 *   "Fetch Selected" button) and — per PR-3 — the four
 *   collapsible per-bucket sections. A user who is looking at
 *   the candidate list is past the "start a search" decision,
 *   and the two concerns do not belong in one component.
 *
 * ── Why the runFullSearch call takes `topic.keywords` ───────────
 *
 *   Same reasoning as before the refactor. The Python search
 *   script carries the keywords in its marker payload for the
 *   renderer's display but does not use them to construct the
 *   search — the search runs on the title alone, per the plan's
 *   user journey. Passing the keywords through lets the
 *   `SEARCH_RESULT` marker's payload stay complete so the
 *   renderer's metadata row can render a keyword count for the
 *   operation.
 *
 * ── Why the component is NOT responsible for its own guard ──────
 *
 *   Same reasoning as before the refactor. The Sources tab is
 *   only ever mounted inside the topic-detail page, which the
 *   layout's auth gate has already verified. The `topic` prop
 *   it receives is a populated `Topic` object from
 *   `activeTopic`.
 *
 * ── What this component does NOT do ────────────────────────────
 *
 *   - It does not render the candidate list. That is
 *     `SourceList`'s job — see the sibling file.
 *
 *   - It does not persist the search results. See the store's
 *     file header for the non-persistence rationale.
 *
 *   - It does not auto-start the search on mount. The user
 *     clicks "Start Search" — the plan's user journey makes the
 *     search a deliberate action, not something that fires the
 *     moment a page opens.
 *
 *   - It does not call `loadCandidates` on mount. The topic-
 *     detail page owns the fetch-on-open behavior; this panel
 *     only renders state the page (or the panel's own "Start
 *     Search") has already populated.
 *
 *   - It does not detect the captcha modal. When the Python
 *     script hits a captcha, the main process emits a
 *     `CAPTCHA_REQUIRED` marker and the automation store opens
 *     the modal. This panel keeps showing the SEARCHING state
 *     underneath — the modal is on top, and dismissing it either
 *     resumes or aborts the search, at which point this panel's
 *     state catches up via the store.
 */

import { useCallback, useMemo } from 'react';
import {
  AlertCircle,
  CheckCircle2,
  Loader2,
  Search,
} from 'lucide-react';

import { useResearch } from '../hooks/useResearch.js';
import type {
  BucketConfig,
  BucketKey,
} from '../store/researchStore.js';
import type { Topic } from '../../shared/types/index.js';

// ============================================================
//  Public types
// ============================================================

/**
 * The props accepted by `SearchProgress`.
 *
 * The panel takes the full `Topic` object rather than a set of
 * individual fields, because every field it uses — `_id` (as the
 * topic identifier for the search), `title` (as the query), and
 * `keywords` (as the payload metadata) — travels together as one
 * unit. A caller that somehow had the title but not the id would
 * have a different problem that no amount of prop-splitting would
 * solve.
 */
export interface SearchProgressProps {
  /** The topic whose search this panel represents. */
  topic: Topic;
}

// ============================================================
//  Internal constants
// ============================================================
//  The four bucket keys, in run order. The array is typed as
//  `readonly BucketKey[]` so the compiler guarantees every
//  string is a legal member of the union — a typo would be a
//  type error, not a silent runtime surprise.
//
//  The same order is what `runFullSearch` uses when it runs the
//  four buckets sequentially; keeping the array here in the same
//  order means the panel's "current bucket" line names the
//  bucket the user would expect to see next.
//
//  `BUCKET_LABELS` maps each key to the display label the panel
//  and the user journey use — "Any time", "Past year", etc. —
//  declared as a `Record<BucketKey, string>` so the compiler
//  enforces exhaustiveness.
//
//  `BUCKET_CONFIG_FIELD` maps each bucket key to the
//  corresponding field name on `BucketConfig`. The two type
//  systems use different casing on purpose — the keys are
//  lowercase slugs matching the marker protocol, and the config
//  fields are the camelCase names the store declares — and this
//  map is the single place where the two are associated.

const BUCKET_KEYS: readonly BucketKey[] = [
  'anytime',
  'year',
  'month',
  'week',
];

const BUCKET_LABELS: Record<BucketKey, string> = {
  anytime: 'Any time',
  year: 'Past year',
  month: 'Past month',
  week: 'Past week',
};

const BUCKET_CONFIG_FIELD: Record<BucketKey, keyof BucketConfig> = {
  anytime: 'anyTime',
  year: 'pastYear',
  month: 'pastMonth',
  week: 'pastWeek',
};

// ============================================================
//  SearchProgress
// ============================================================

/**
 * The search panel. See the file header for the four states and
 * the reasoning behind the component's scope.
 */
export const SearchProgress = ({ topic }: SearchProgressProps) => {
  const {
    candidates,
    candidatesByBucket,
    status,
    searchProgress,
    error,
    runFullSearch,
    cancel,
    clearError,
  } = useResearch(topic._id);

  // ---- Bucket config derivation ----
  // See the file header's "bucket config derivation" note for
  // why this is derived from `topic.sourceCount` today and will
  // read `topic.searchBuckets` once PR-2 lands. The even split
  // with remainder on the first bucket guarantees the four
  // counts always sum to `topic.sourceCount`.
  const bucketConfig = useMemo((): BucketConfig => {
    const total = topic.sourceCount;
    const per = Math.floor(total / 4);
    const remainder = total - per * 4;
    return {
      anyTime: per + remainder,
      pastYear: per,
      pastMonth: per,
      pastWeek: per,
    };
  }, [topic.sourceCount]);

  // ---- Start / retry ----
  // The same handler serves both the idle-state "Start Search"
  // button, the error-state "Retry" button, and the complete
  // state's "Search again" button — the underlying operation is
  // identical in all three cases: run the four-bucket search.
  //
  // The `useCallback` dependencies are all stable references —
  // `runFullSearch` is a `useCallback`-wrapped adapter from the
  // hook, and the four topic fields are read from the immutable
  // `Topic` prop. The handler therefore has a stable identity
  // across renders, so a component that listed it in a
  // `useEffect` dependency array would not re-run the effect on
  // every render.
  //
  // `topic.keywords` is a `readonly string[]` on the type, but
  // `runFullSearch` accepts a mutable `string[]`. The spread
  // `[...]` produces a mutable copy, which is what the hook
  // expects; passing the readonly array directly would be a
  // type error under `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`' strict
  // readonly handling.
  const handleStart = useCallback((): void => {
    void runFullSearch(
      topic._id,
      topic.title,
      [...topic.keywords],
      bucketConfig
    );
  }, [
    runFullSearch,
    topic._id,
    topic.title,
    topic.keywords,
    bucketConfig,
  ]);

  // ---- Cancel ----
  const handleCancel = useCallback((): void => {
    void cancel(topic._id);
  }, [cancel, topic._id]);

  // ---- Dismiss error ----
  const handleDismiss = useCallback((): void => {
    clearError(topic._id);
  }, [clearError, topic._id]);

  // ---- Derived per-bucket progress ----
  //
  // `searchProgress` from the hook is a four-bucket record: each
  // bucket's slot is either `null` (not running) or a live
  // `SearchProgress` object. The `runFullSearch` action runs the
  // four buckets sequentially, so at most one slot is non-null
  // at any moment — the currently-active bucket.
  //
  // `currentBucket` is the key of that bucket, or `null` when no
  // bucket is currently running (which happens only in the
  // transition between two buckets' runs, a sub-second window).
  //
  // `foundTotal` sums the completed buckets' candidate counts
  // (from `candidatesByBucket`) plus the currently-active
  // bucket's live count (from its progress slot). The two
  // sources are mutually exclusive per bucket: a completed
  // bucket's progress is cleared and its candidates are in the
  // candidates record; a running bucket's candidates record is
  // empty and its count is in the progress slot.
  //
  // `requestedTotal` is the sum of the four configured counts
  // from `bucketConfig`. It does not change during a run.
  const currentBucket: BucketKey | null = useMemo(() => {
    for (const key of BUCKET_KEYS) {
      if (searchProgress[key] !== null) {
        return key;
      }
    }
    return null;
  }, [searchProgress]);

  const foundTotal = useMemo((): number => {
    let total = 0;
    for (const key of BUCKET_KEYS) {
      total += candidatesByBucket[key].length;
      total += searchProgress[key]?.found ?? 0;
    }
    return total;
  }, [candidatesByBucket, searchProgress]);

  const requestedTotal = useMemo((): number => {
    return (
      bucketConfig.anyTime +
      bucketConfig.pastYear +
      bucketConfig.pastMonth +
      bucketConfig.pastWeek
    );
  }, [bucketConfig]);

  // ---- State 1: SEARCHING ----
  // The live progress panel. Shown while `status === 'searching'`
  // regardless of how many candidates have been collected so far —
  // the whole point is that the user can see partial progress as
  // it happens.
  //
  // Two progress indicators are shown side by side:
  //
  //   * The overall progress bar. Its numerator is `foundTotal`
  //     (sum of completed buckets' candidate counts plus the
  //     running bucket's live count) and its denominator is
  //     `requestedTotal` (the sum of the four configured bucket
  //     counts). The percentage is derived from the ratio and
  //     clamped to 100.
  //
  //   * The current bucket's own line. The bucket is the one
  //     whose progress slot is non-null; its own count comes
  //     from that slot's `found` field, and its requested count
  //     comes from the corresponding field on `bucketConfig`.
  //     The bucket's label is looked up from `BUCKET_LABELS`.
  //
  // A user who has configured one bucket with zero candidates
  // will not see that bucket's progress — `runFullSearch` skips
  // zero-count buckets entirely (Q8.3), and the skipped buckets
  // contribute zero to both the numerator and the denominator.
  if (status === 'searching') {
    const currentBucketProgress =
      currentBucket !== null ? searchProgress[currentBucket] : null;
    const currentRequested =
      currentBucket !== null
        ? bucketConfig[BUCKET_CONFIG_FIELD[currentBucket]]
        : 0;
    const overallPercent =
      requestedTotal > 0
        ? Math.min(
            100,
            Math.round((foundTotal / requestedTotal) * 100)
          )
        : 0;

    return (
      <div
        role="status"
        aria-live="polite"
        className={
          'flex items-start gap-3 rounded-xl border ' +
          'border-neutral-200 bg-background p-4 ' +
          'dark:border-neutral-800'
        }
      >
        <Loader2
          size={20}
          className="mt-0.5 shrink-0 animate-spin text-primary"
          aria-hidden="true"
        />
        <div className="min-w-0 flex-1">
          <p
            className={
              'text-sm font-medium text-text ' +
              'dark:text-neutral-100'
            }
          >
            Searching the web…
          </p>

          {/* ---- Overall progress ---- */}
          <div className="mt-2">
            <div
              className={
                'flex items-center justify-between text-xs ' +
                'text-neutral-500 dark:text-neutral-400'
              }
            >
              <span>Overall</span>
              <span className="tabular-nums">
                {foundTotal} of {requestedTotal} · {overallPercent}%
              </span>
            </div>
            <div
              className={
                'mt-1 h-1.5 w-full overflow-hidden rounded-full ' +
                'bg-neutral-200 dark:bg-neutral-800'
              }
              role="progressbar"
              aria-valuenow={overallPercent}
              aria-valuemin={0}
              aria-valuemax={100}
            >
              <div
                className="h-full bg-primary transition-all duration-300"
                style={{ width: `${overallPercent}%` }}
              />
            </div>
          </div>

          {/* ---- Current bucket progress ---- */}
          {currentBucket !== null && currentBucketProgress !== null && (
            <p
              className={
                'mt-2 text-xs text-neutral-500 ' +
                'dark:text-neutral-400'
              }
            >
              {BUCKET_LABELS[currentBucket]}:{' '}
              <span className="tabular-nums">
                {currentBucketProgress.found} of {currentRequested}
              </span>
            </p>
          )}
        </div>
        <button
          type="button"
          onClick={handleCancel}
          className={
            'shrink-0 rounded-lg border border-neutral-300 ' +
            'bg-background px-3 py-1.5 text-xs font-medium ' +
            'text-text transition-colors ' +
            'hover:bg-neutral-100 ' +
            'focus-visible:outline-none ' +
            'dark:border-neutral-700 dark:hover:bg-neutral-800 ' +
            'dark:text-neutral-100'
          }
        >
          Cancel
        </button>
      </div>
    );
  }

  // ---- State 2: ERROR ----
  // The store's error field is set by every failure path: a
  // rejected IPC promise, a search service that returned
  // `{ success: false }`, a captcha-driven user skip, or a
  // timeout. All of them produce a user-facing message, and all of
  // them are handled the same way — show the banner, offer Retry
  // and Dismiss.
  if (status === 'error' && error !== null) {
    return (
      <div
        role="alert"
        className={
          'flex flex-col gap-3 rounded-xl border ' +
          'border-red-200 bg-red-50 p-4 ' +
          'dark:border-red-900 dark:bg-red-950/60'
        }
      >
        <div className="flex items-start gap-2">
          <AlertCircle
            size={18}
            className="mt-0.5 shrink-0 text-red-600 dark:text-red-400"
            aria-hidden="true"
          />
          <p
            className={
              'min-w-0 flex-1 text-sm text-red-900 ' +
              'dark:text-red-100'
            }
          >
            {error}
          </p>
        </div>
        <div className="flex items-center justify-end gap-2">
          <button
            type="button"
            onClick={handleDismiss}
            className={
              'rounded-lg border border-red-300 bg-transparent ' +
              'px-3 py-1.5 text-xs font-medium text-red-800 ' +
              'transition-colors hover:bg-red-100 ' +
              'focus-visible:outline-none ' +
              'dark:border-red-800 dark:text-red-200 ' +
              'dark:hover:bg-red-900/40'
            }
          >
            Dismiss
          </button>
          <button
            type="button"
            onClick={handleStart}
            className={
              'rounded-lg bg-red-600 px-3 py-1.5 text-xs ' +
              'font-medium text-white transition-colors ' +
              'hover:bg-red-700 focus-visible:outline-none'
            }
          >
            Retry
          </button>
        </div>
      </div>
    );
  }

  // ---- State 3: COMPLETE ----
  // Candidates exist in the store and the operation is not
  // running. The panel reports the count and offers a re-search
  // (useful when the topic's title or keywords have changed, or
  // when the user wants to refresh the candidate list from a
  // different angle).
  if (candidates.length > 0) {
    return (
      <div
        className={
          'flex items-center gap-3 rounded-xl border ' +
          'border-emerald-200 bg-emerald-50 p-4 ' +
          'dark:border-emerald-900 dark:bg-emerald-950/60'
        }
      >
        <CheckCircle2
          size={20}
          className="shrink-0 text-emerald-600 dark:text-emerald-400"
          aria-hidden="true"
        />
        <div className="min-w-0 flex-1">
          <p
            className={
              'text-sm font-medium text-emerald-900 ' +
              'dark:text-emerald-100'
            }
          >
            {candidates.length}{' '}
            {candidates.length === 1 ? 'candidate' : 'candidates'} found
          </p>
          <p
            className={
              'mt-0.5 text-xs text-emerald-800/80 ' +
              'dark:text-emerald-200/80'
            }
          >
            Review the list below and pick the sources to extract.
          </p>
        </div>
        <button
          type="button"
          onClick={handleStart}
          className={
            'shrink-0 rounded-lg border ' +
            'border-emerald-300 bg-transparent px-3 py-1.5 ' +
            'text-xs font-medium text-emerald-800 ' +
            'transition-colors hover:bg-emerald-100 ' +
            'focus-visible:outline-none ' +
            'dark:border-emerald-800 dark:text-emerald-200 ' +
            'dark:hover:bg-emerald-900/40'
          }
        >
          Search again
        </button>
      </div>
    );
  }

  // ---- State 4: IDLE ----
  // Nothing has been searched yet. The invitation and the primary
  // call to action.
  return (
    <div
      className={
        'flex items-center gap-3 rounded-xl border ' +
        'border-neutral-200 bg-background p-4 ' +
        'dark:border-neutral-800'
      }
    >
      <div
        className={
          'flex h-10 w-10 shrink-0 items-center justify-center ' +
          'rounded-full bg-neutral-100 text-neutral-500 ' +
          'dark:bg-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-400'
        }
      >
        <Search size={18} aria-hidden="true" />
      </div>
      <div className="min-w-0 flex-1">
        <p
          className={
            'text-sm font-medium text-text ' +
            'dark:text-neutral-100'
          }
        >
          Ready to search
        </p>
        <p
          className={
            'mt-0.5 text-xs text-neutral-500 ' +
            'dark:text-neutral-400'
          }
        >
          Find candidate source pages for this topic on the web.
        </p>
      </div>
      <button
        type="button"
        onClick={handleStart}
        className={
          'shrink-0 rounded-lg bg-primary px-4 py-2 text-sm ' +
          'font-medium text-white transition-colors ' +
          'hover:bg-primary-dark focus-visible:outline-none'
        }
      >
        Start Search
      </button>
    </div>
  );
};

// ============================================================
//  Default export
// ============================================================

/**
 * The `SearchProgress` component as the default export.
 * `TopicDetail.tsx` imports it with the default form
 * (`import SearchProgress from '.../SearchProgress.js'`) — see the
 * forward reference in that file's imports — so this is the
 * export that resolves the reference.
 */
export default SearchProgress;
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/components/SourceCandidate.tsx">
/**
 * SourceCandidate — one row in the candidate list.
 *
 * Renders a single search result as a selectable list item. Every
 * candidate produced by the search — regardless of engine, platform,
 * or content — has the same four pieces of candidate information
 * the row can show:
 *
 *   url        the source page's absolute URL
 *   title      the page's title, as displayed on the SERP
 *   snippet    a short preview of the page's content
 *   platform   a slug identifying the site — 'reddit', 'quora',
 *              'medium', 'twitter', or 'generic'
 *
 * Plus two pieces of pipeline state the parent supplies:
 *
 *   rangeCount the number of time-range buckets this URL was found
 *              in. A value greater than 1 renders a "found in N
 *              ranges" badge next to the platform badge,
 *              preserving the signal that the URL was deduplicated
 *              from multiple buckets (per the Q8.6 decision).
 *   failure    the URL's last extraction failure record, or null
 *              when the extraction succeeded or has not yet been
 *              attempted. A non-null value switches the row into
 *              its failed visual state — a red border, a
 *              red-tinted background, a reason tooltip on hover,
 *              and a retry icon button in the action group (per
 *              the Q2.2 and Q2.3 decisions).
 *
 * And the row renders those pieces in one consistent layout:
 *
 *   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 *   │ ☑  [reddit]  How to garden in a tiny apartment     ↗    │
 *   │              reddit.com/r/gardening/...                  │
 *   │              Living in a rental often means less…        │
 *   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
 *
 * ── Fully controlled, no internal state ─────────────────────────
 *
 *   The row owns no state of its own. Its checkbox's checked value
 *   comes from the `selected` prop, and every interaction is
 *   reported back through the props — the parent (`SourceList`)
 *   is what holds the selection set and decides what a click means.
 *
 *   This is the standard controlled-component pattern, and it is
 *   what makes `SourceList`'s auto-select-on-new-search effect
 *   (which rebuilds the whole selection set) work: the parent's
 *   state is the single source of truth, and the rows are pure
 *   renderers of it.
 *
 * ── Click model: row toggles, link opens ────────────────────────
 *
 *   Two clickable affordances in one row, with distinct effects:
 *
 *     1. Clicking anywhere on the row except the title link
 *        toggles the candidate's selection. This is the primary
 *        interaction — the user is triaging a list, and the fastest
 *        way to include or exclude a source is to click the row.
 *
 *     2. Clicking the title link itself opens the URL in the user's
 *        default browser, WITHOUT toggling the selection. The user
 *        is previewing the source before deciding whether to keep
 *        it — a legitimate and common interaction, and toggling
 *        the checkbox as a side effect of previewing would be
 *        surprising.
 *
 *   The distinction is implemented by stopping propagation on the
 *   link's click handler. The link's click bubbles up to the row's
 *   handler by default; the `event.stopPropagation()` call in the
 *   link's handler prevents that, so the row's toggle does not fire
 *   for a click that was meant to open the source.
 *
 *   The link is rendered as a `<button>` (not an `<a href>`) because
 *   the actual navigation goes through `window.electronAPI.utils.openExternal`
 *   — the OS's default browser — not through the Electron window's
 *   own navigation. An `<a href="https://...">` would either try to
 *   navigate the Electron window itself (wrong — the app's window is
 *   the dashboard, not a browser) or require a `target="_blank"`
 *   that Electron does not reliably intercept. The explicit
 *   `openExternal` call is the correct mechanism and works on every
 *   platform.
 *
 * ── The checkbox: a native input, styled ────────────────────────
 *
 *   The checkbox is a real `<input type="checkbox">`, not a
 *   custom-styled `<div>`. This is deliberate: the native input
 *   brings keyboard activation (Space toggles), accessibility
 *   semantics (the browser announces "checked" / "unchecked"), and
 *   focus management for free. A custom-styled div would require
 *   re-implementing all three, and would inevitably get one of them
 *   subtly wrong. The styling sits on top of the native input via
 *   Tailwind's `accent-primary` and standard sizing classes; the
 *   input's semantics are untouched.
 *
 *   The checkbox's `onChange` is wired to the same toggle the row
 *   body uses. A click on the checkbox fires the checkbox's own
 *   handler AND bubbles to the row's handler — but because the
 *   checkbox's handler does not stop propagation, the row's toggle
 *   would fire a second time, producing a net no-op. The fix is the
 *   checkbox's own `event.stopPropagation()` in its handler — the
 *   checkbox handles the toggle, and the row's handler is skipped.
 *
 *   The same reasoning applies to the external-link button, but with
 *   the opposite outcome: the link's handler DOES call
 *   `stopPropagation`, and it does NOT toggle the selection, because
 *   "open this URL" and "select this URL" are different intents.
 *
 * ── Disabled state ──────────────────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   When the `disabled` prop is true (the parent sets it while an
 *   extraction is in flight), every interactive element in the row
 *   is disabled and the row's click handler short-circuits. The
 *   visual treatment is the same `opacity-60` the rest of the app
 *   uses for disabled controls, applied to the whole row, so the
 *   transition into and out of the disabled state reads as a single
 *   unit rather than as several small changes to the checkbox, the
 *   link, and the text.
 * 
 * 
 * ── The failed state ────────────────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   A row whose `failure` prop is non-null renders in the failed
 *   visual state. Three visual changes and one behavioral one:
 *
 *     1. The row's border and background tint switch to red. The
 *        red treatment takes precedence over the selected tint —
 *        a failed candidate can still be selected, but the
 *        failure is the more urgent signal, and the selected
 *        state remains available in the checkbox.
 *
 *     2. The row's `title` attribute (the browser's native
 *        tooltip) carries the failure reason. Hovering the row
 *        surfaces the message without the user having to open a
 *        modal or navigate away. Inner elements with their own
 *        `title` attributes (the candidate title paragraph, the
 *        URL display line, the external-link button) keep their
 *        own tooltips when hovered directly; the failure reason
 *        shows on the row's remaining area.
 *
 *     3. A retry icon button appears in the action group, next to
 *        the external-link button. Clicking it calls the
 *        `onRetry` callback with the candidate's URL. The same
 *        `stopPropagation` pattern the external-link button uses
 *        keeps the row's toggle from firing on a retry click.
 *
 *   The row does NOT block the failed candidate's other
 *   interactions. A user can still toggle its checkbox (a failed
 *   URL that has since been manually re-fetched, or one the user
 *   wants to skip on the next batch), and can still click the
 *   external-link button to look at the source page. The failure
 *   state is a signal, not a lock.
 *
 * ── The multi-range badge ───────────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   A row whose `rangeCount` is greater than 1 renders a small
 *   "N ranges" badge next to the platform badge. The badge
 *   preserves the signal that the URL appeared in multiple
 *   time-range buckets and was deduplicated by the store — see
 *   the Q8.6 decision. A URL found by four buckets is a stronger
 *   source than one found by one, and the badge lets the user see
 *   that without the row appearing four times in the list.
 *
 *   The badge does not appear when `rangeCount` is 1 or less. A
 *   value of 1 is the default — the URL was found by exactly one
 *   bucket — and rendering a "1 range" badge for every row would
 *   be noise.
 *
 * ── What this component does NOT do ────────────────────────────
 *
 *   - It does not hold the selection state. See the "fully
 *     controlled" note above.
 *
 *   - It does not navigate the Electron window. The title link
 *     opens the URL in the user's default browser via the preload's
 *     `utils.openExternal` method. The app's own window is the
 *     dashboard, and it never navigates to external pages.
 *
 *   - It does not fetch or preview the candidate's content. The
 *     "fetch and show the doc file" flow is the Documents feature's
 *     job (Session 12) — this component's link is the escape hatch
 *     for a user who wants to look at the source in a real browser
 *     before deciding whether to include it.
 *
 *   - It does not own the failure state. The parent (`SourceList`)
 *     holds the failed-URL records and decides what a retry click
 *     does — typically a call into the research store's
 *     `retryFailed` action. This component reads the `failure`
 *     prop, renders the failed visual state, and reports the
 *     retry intent through the `onRetry` callback; it never
 *     mutates anything.
 *   - It does not render a favicon, a per-platform icon, or any
 *     image. The platform is surfaced as a text badge in the design
 *     system's neutral palette; a site icon (fetched from a
 *     favicon service, or from a bundled image library) would add
 *     network traffic and an external dependency for a cosmetic
 *     gain. If a future session wants icons, the change is a
 *     `<img src=...>` inserted next to the badge in this file, and
 *     the badge's own rendering is unaffected.
 */

import { useCallback, type MouseEvent } from 'react';
import { ExternalLink, RotateCcw } from 'lucide-react';

import type {
  FailedUrlRecord,
  SearchCandidate,
} from '../store/researchStore.js';

// ============================================================
//  Public types
// ============================================================

/**
 * The props accepted by `SourceCandidate`.
 *
 * The shape is the contract established by `SourceList.tsx`'s
 * `<SourceCandidate ... />` call site — every field named here is
 * passed by that parent, and every field the parent passes is
 * declared here. A change to either side is a compile-time error
 * at the other, which is exactly the coupling the interface is
 * for.
 */
export interface SourceCandidateProps {
  /** The candidate to render. */
  candidate: SearchCandidate;

  /** Whether the row's checkbox is currently checked. */
  selected: boolean;

  /** Whether every interaction in the row is disabled. True
   *  while an extraction is in flight; the parent sets this so
   *  the user cannot change the selection during extraction. */
  disabled: boolean;

  /** Called when the user toggles the row's selection. The
   *  argument is the candidate's URL — the same value the
   *  parent's `Set<string>` uses as a key. Called with the URL
   *  regardless of whether the toggle turns the selection on or
   *  off; the parent interprets the current state from its own
   *  Set. */
  onToggle: (url: string) => void;

  /** The number of time-range buckets this URL was found in. A
   *  value greater than 1 renders the "found in N ranges" badge
   *  next to the platform badge. A value of 1 — the default the
   *  parent supplies when the URL was found by exactly one
   *  bucket — renders no badge. */
  rangeCount: number;

  /** The URL's last extraction failure record, or null when the
   *  extraction succeeded, has not been attempted, or the
   *  failure record is not available. A non-null value switches
   *  the row into its failed visual state — see the file
   *  header's "The failed state" section. */
  failure: FailedUrlRecord | null;

  /** Called when the user clicks the retry icon button on a
   *  failed row. The argument is the candidate's URL — the same
   *  key the parent's failed-URL record uses. The callback is
   *  never invoked on a non-failed row because the retry button
   *  is not rendered. */
  onRetry: (url: string) => void;
}

// ============================================================
//  Platform badge
// ============================================================

/**
 * The per-platform display label for the badge. Falls back to the
 * raw `platform` string when the platform is not one of the known
 * slugs — a value the search might legitimately produce (a future
 * extractor for a site the mapping does not yet know about) would
 * render as the raw slug rather than as an empty string.
 */
const PLATFORM_LABELS: Record<string, string> = {
  reddit: 'Reddit',
  quora: 'Quora',
  medium: 'Medium',
  twitter: 'Twitter',
  generic: 'Web',
};

/**
 * The badge's Tailwind class set, keyed by platform. Each
 * platform gets its own tint so a mixed list is scannable at a
 * glance — a reader who is looking specifically for Reddit
 * threads can find them without reading every row.
 *
 * The fallback (for an unknown platform) is the neutral class
 * set, which is the same treatment `generic` gets. An unknown
 * platform does not deserve its own color — its identity is
 * unclear, and giving it one would imply a classification the
 * codebase has not actually made.
 */
const PLATFORM_CLASSES: Record<string, string> = {
  reddit:
    'bg-orange-100 text-orange-800 ' +
    'dark:bg-orange-950/60 dark:text-orange-200',
  quora:
    'bg-red-100 text-red-800 ' +
    'dark:bg-red-950/60 dark:text-red-200',
  medium:
    'bg-neutral-200 text-neutral-800 ' +
    'dark:bg-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-200',
  twitter:
    'bg-sky-100 text-sky-800 ' +
    'dark:bg-sky-950/60 dark:text-sky-200',
  generic:
    'bg-neutral-100 text-neutral-700 ' +
    'dark:bg-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-300',
};

/**
 * The fallback class set for an unknown platform — identical to
 * the `generic` entry above. Kept as a separate constant so a
 * future change to the generic styling does not silently change
 * the unknown-platform styling as a side effect.
 */
const FALLBACK_BADGE_CLASS =
  'bg-neutral-100 text-neutral-700 ' +
  'dark:bg-neutral-800 dark:text-neutral-300';

// ============================================================
//  Helpers
// ============================================================

/**
 * Return a shortened display form of a URL suitable for the
 * row's single-line URL display — the host plus a truncated
 * path.
 *
 * The full URL is available in the `title` attribute on the
 * element that renders this string, so the user can hover to see
 * the entire address. The displayed form is deliberately short:
 * the row is dense, and a full 200-character URL would either
 * wrap to a second line or force the row to scroll horizontally.
 *
 * The function never throws — a malformed URL (which the search
 * service's own normalization should have rejected) falls back to
 * the raw input, so the row still shows something readable.
 */
const shortenUrl = (url: string): string => {
  try {
    const parsed = new URL(url);
    const host = parsed.hostname.replace(/^www\./, '');
    const path = parsed.pathname;
    const search = parsed.search;

    // The host by itself, for a bare domain.
    let display = host;

    // A truncated path — the first segment (or two) plus an
    // ellipsis when the path is longer than the budget.
    if (path && path !== '/') {
      const segments = path.split('/').filter((s) => s.length > 0);
      const head = segments.slice(0, 2).join('/');
      display += '/' + head;
      if (segments.length > 2 || head.length < path.length - 1) {
        display += '/…';
      }
    }

    // A query string is not shown at all — it is almost never
    // meaningful to the user deciding whether to extract the
    // page.
    void search;

    return display;
  } catch {
    return url;
  }
};

// ============================================================
//  SourceCandidate
// ============================================================

/**
 * One row in the candidate list. See the file header for the
 * component's responsibilities, its click model, and the reasoning
 * behind the checkbox and link treatments.
 */
export const SourceCandidate = ({
  candidate,
  selected,
  disabled,
  onToggle,
  rangeCount,
  failure,
  onRetry,
}: SourceCandidateProps) => {
  // ---- Toggle the selection ----
  // Called by the row's click handler and by the checkbox's own
  // onChange handler (see the file header for the
  // stopPropagation dance that keeps the two from double-firing).
  const handleToggle = useCallback((): void => {
    if (disabled) return;
    onToggle(candidate.url);
  }, [disabled, onToggle, candidate.url]);

  // ---- Row click ----
  // A click anywhere on the row that was not intercepted by an
  // inner handler toggles the selection. The `disabled` check
  // inside `handleToggle` short-circuits the call when the row
  // is disabled.
  const handleRowClick = useCallback((): void => {
    handleToggle();
  }, [handleToggle]);

  // ---- Checkbox change ----
  // The native input fires `change` when the user activates it
  // (by clicking or by pressing Space while focused). The
  // `stopPropagation` prevents the event from bubbling to the
  // row's click handler, which would toggle the selection a
  // second time and produce a net no-op.
  const handleCheckboxChange = useCallback(
    (event: MouseEvent<HTMLInputElement>): void => {
      event.stopPropagation();
      handleToggle();
    },
    [handleToggle]
  );

  // ---- External link click ----
  // Opens the URL in the user's default browser. The
  // `stopPropagation` prevents the row's click handler from also
  // firing — the user wanted to LOOK at the source, not to
  // select or deselect it.
  const handleLinkClick = useCallback(
    (event: MouseEvent<HTMLButtonElement>): void => {
      event.stopPropagation();
      if (disabled) return;
      void window.electronAPI.utils.openExternal(candidate.url);
    },
    [disabled, candidate.url]
  );

  
  // ---- Retry click ----
  // Opens the retry flow for a failed row. The `stopPropagation`
  // prevents the row's click handler from also firing — the user
  // wanted to RETRY the extraction, not to toggle the row's
  // selection. Same pattern as the external-link button above.
  const handleRetryClick = useCallback(
    (event: MouseEvent<HTMLButtonElement>): void => {
      event.stopPropagation();
      if (disabled) return;
      onRetry(candidate.url);
    },
    [disabled, onRetry, candidate.url]
  );

  // ---- Derived display values ----
  const platformLabel =
    PLATFORM_LABELS[candidate.platform] ?? candidate.platform;
  const platformClass =
    PLATFORM_CLASSES[candidate.platform] ?? FALLBACK_BADGE_CLASS;
  const displayUrl = shortenUrl(candidate.url);

  // Whether the row renders in its failed visual state. See the
  // file header's "The failed state" section for the full set of
  // effects this flag drives.
  const isFailed = failure !== null;

  // Whether the row renders its multi-range badge. A `rangeCount`
  // of 1 (or less, which cannot occur but is defended against
  // here) is the default state and renders no badge. See the file
  // header's "The multi-range badge" section.
  const showRangeBadge = rangeCount > 1;

  // The row's native tooltip. When the row is failed, the
  // tooltip carries the failure reason so a user who wonders why
  // the row is red can read the message without opening a modal.
  // A non-failed row has no tooltip at this level — the inner
  // elements' own `title` attributes serve their own purposes.
  const rowTitle = isFailed
    ? `Extraction failed: ${failure.reason}`
    : undefined;

  return (
    <div
      role="checkbox"
      aria-checked={selected}
      aria-disabled={disabled}
      tabIndex={disabled ? -1 : 0}
      onClick={handleRowClick}
      title={rowTitle}
      className={
        'flex cursor-pointer gap-3 rounded-xl border p-3 ' +
        'transition-colors ' +
        (disabled
          ? 'cursor-not-allowed opacity-60 '
          : '') +
        (isFailed
          ? 'border-red-300 bg-red-50 ' +
            'dark:border-red-900 dark:bg-red-950/40 '
          : selected
            ? 'border-primary bg-primary/5 ' +
              'dark:border-primary-light dark:bg-primary-light/5 '
            : 'border-neutral-200 bg-background ' +
              'hover:border-neutral-300 hover:bg-neutral-50 ' +
              'dark:border-neutral-800 ' +
              'dark:hover:border-neutral-700 ' +
              'dark:hover:bg-neutral-800/40')
      }
    >
      {/* ---- Checkbox ---- */}
      {/* Wrapped in a small flex container so the checkbox sits on
          the first text line rather than being vertically centred
          against a multi-line content block — the standard list-
          item convention (the control aligns with the first line
          of text, not with the row's middle). */}
      <div className="flex shrink-0 items-start pt-0.5">
        <input
          type="checkbox"
          checked={selected}
          onChange={() => {
            /* The `onChange` is required for the controlled input
             *  to update its checked state, but the actual toggle
             *  logic lives in the click handler — see the file
             *  header. The empty callback here is intentional; the
