             *  real work is done by the sibling `onClick` and the
             *  row's `onClick`, both of which are wired to
             *  `handleToggle`. */
          }}
          onClick={handleCheckboxChange}
          disabled={disabled}
          className={
            'h-4 w-4 cursor-pointer rounded border-neutral-300 ' +
            'text-primary accent-primary ' +
            'disabled:cursor-not-allowed ' +
            'focus-visible:outline-none ' +
            'dark:border-neutral-600 dark:bg-neutral-950'
          }
          aria-hidden="true"
        />
      </div>

      {/* ---- Content ---- */}
      <div className="min-w-0 flex-1">
        {/* Title + platform badge + range badge + action buttons */}
        <div className="flex items-start gap-2">
          <span
            className={
              'shrink-0 rounded-full px-2 py-0.5 text-[10px] ' +
              'font-medium uppercase tracking-wide ' +
              platformClass
            }
          >
            {platformLabel}
          </span>
          {showRangeBadge && (
            <span
              className={
                'shrink-0 rounded-full bg-primary/10 ' +
                'px-2 py-0.5 text-[10px] font-medium ' +
                'uppercase tracking-wide text-primary-dark ' +
                'dark:bg-primary-light/20 ' +
                'dark:text-primary-light'
              }
              title={`Found in ${rangeCount} time ranges`}
            >
              {rangeCount} ranges
            </span>
          )}
          <p
            className={
              'line-clamp-2 min-w-0 flex-1 text-sm font-medium ' +
              'text-text dark:text-neutral-100'
            }
            title={candidate.title}
          >
            {candidate.title}
          </p>
          {isFailed && (
            <button
              type="button"
              onClick={handleRetryClick}
              disabled={disabled}
              aria-label="Retry extraction"
              title={`Retry extraction (${failure.reason})`}
              className={
                'shrink-0 rounded-md p-1 text-red-600 ' +
                'transition-colors ' +
                'hover:bg-red-100 hover:text-red-800 ' +
                'disabled:cursor-not-allowed ' +
                'focus-visible:outline-none ' +
                'dark:text-red-400 ' +
                'dark:hover:bg-red-950/60 ' +
                'dark:hover:text-red-200'
              }
            >
              <RotateCcw size={14} aria-hidden="true" />
            </button>
          )}
          <button
            type="button"
            onClick={handleLinkClick}
            disabled={disabled}
            aria-label="Open source in browser"
            title="Open source in browser"
            className={
              'shrink-0 rounded-md p-1 text-neutral-400 ' +
              'transition-colors ' +
              'hover:bg-neutral-100 hover:text-primary ' +
              'disabled:cursor-not-allowed ' +
              'focus-visible:outline-none ' +
              'dark:hover:bg-neutral-800 ' +
              'dark:hover:text-primary-light'
            }
          >
            <ExternalLink size={14} aria-hidden="true" />
          </button>
        </div>

        {/* URL display */}
        <p
          className={
            'mt-0.5 truncate text-xs text-neutral-500 ' +
            'dark:text-neutral-400'
          }
          title={candidate.url}
        >
          {displayUrl}
        </p>

        {/* Snippet */}
        {candidate.snippet.length > 0 && (
          <p
            className={
              'mt-1 line-clamp-2 text-xs leading-snug ' +
              'text-neutral-600 dark:text-neutral-300'
            }
          >
            {candidate.snippet}
          </p>
        )}
      </div>
    </div>
  );
};

// ============================================================
//  Default export
// ============================================================

/**
 * The `SourceCandidate` component as the default export.
 * `SourceList.tsx` imports it with the default form
 * (`import SourceCandidate from './SourceCandidate.js'`), so
 * this is the export that resolves that file's forward
 * reference.
 */
export default SourceCandidate;
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/hooks/useResearch.ts">
/**
 * useResearch — the renderer's research hook.
 *
 * The single entry point through which the app's components read
 * and manipulate the research feature's state for ONE topic. It
 * sits over `useResearchStore` and mirrors the role
 * `useWriter(topicId)` and `useValidation()` play for their
 * respective features:
 *
 *   - The store owns the state. The hook does not add any.
 *
 *   - The store owns the async actions that reach the IPC layer
 *     and, through it, the main process. The hook does not
 *     reimplement them.
 *
 *   - The hook's only job is to give a component a single, stable
 *     object with everything the research feature exposes for one
 *     topic — the per-topic data fields and the actions.
 *
 * ── The hook's signature ────────────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   `useResearch(topicId)` — the topic ID argument is required.
 *   The hook reads the topic's per-topic slots from the store via
 *   selectors keyed on that ID, so two components rendering
 *   different topics see independent state, and the same
 *   component re-renders only when its own topic's state changes.
 *
 *   This mirrors `useWriter(topicId)`'s shape. Every feature hook
 *   that drives a per-topic pipeline stage takes the topic ID as
 *   its first argument, so the pattern is uniform across the
 *   writer, research, documents, and validation features.
 *
 * ── The hook's API ──────────────────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   Data fields (all read from the topic passed to the hook):
 *
 *     candidates         The topic's candidates, flattened across
 *                        all four time-range buckets.
 *     candidatesByBucket The topic's per-bucket candidate record
 *                        (each bucket key present; empty array
 *                        for a bucket the search has not touched).
 *     status             The topic's lifecycle state — 'idle',
 *                        'searching', 'extracting', or 'error'.
 *     searchProgress     The topic's per-bucket live search
 *                        progress.
 *     extractProgress    The topic's live extraction progress, or
 *                        null when no extraction is in flight.
 *     extractedCount     How many doc files the topic's last
 *                        extraction saved.
 *     failed             The topic's failed-URL records, loaded
 *                        from `failures.json` on first open.
 *     error              The topic's last error message, or null.
 *
 *   Actions:
 *
 *     runFullSearch(topicId, title, keywords, bucketConfig)
 *                        Run the four-bucket search. Wraps the
 *                        store's `runFullSearch` action; resolves
 *                        with a boolean (true on success).
 *
 *     runSearch(topicId, title, keywords?, bucket?)
 *                        Backward-compat single-bucket search,
 *                        kept for the "Search again" flow that
 *                        re-searches one time range without
 *                        touching the others. Wraps
 *                        `runFullSearch` with a single-bucket
 *                        config; the bucket argument defaults to
 *                        'anytime' when omitted.
 *
 *     fetchSelected(topicId, urls)
 *                        Extract the selected URLs. Wraps the
 *                        store's `runExtraction` action; resolves
 *                        with the number of docs saved.
 *
 *     retryFailed(topicId)
 *                        Re-run extraction for the topic's failed
 *                        URLs only. Wraps the store's
 *                        `retryFailed` action.
 *
 *     loadCandidates(topicId)
 *                        Read the topic's saved candidates and
 *                        failures from disk on first open. Wraps
 *                        the store's `loadCandidates` action.
 *
 *     cancel(topicId)    Cancel whatever research operation is
 *                        running for the topic.
 *
 *     clearCandidates(topicId)
 *                        Empty the topic's candidate list.
 *
 *     clearError(topicId)
 *                        Clear the topic's last error message.
 *
 * ── Why the hook has both `runFullSearch` and `runSearch` ───────
 *
 *   The store's `runFullSearch` action searches every configured
 *   bucket in sequence. It is the primary search action and the
 *   one the Sources tab's "Start Search" button uses.
 *
 *   The hook also keeps `runSearch` for the "Search again" flow
 *   within a single bucket. That flow wants to re-run one time
 *   range without touching the others; `runSearch` implements it
 *   by calling `runFullSearch` with a config that puts the
 *   default count in the requested bucket and zero everywhere
 *   else. The store's `runFullSearch` treats a zero-count bucket
 *   as "skip", so only the requested bucket actually searches.
 *
 *   Keeping both names lets the single-bucket flow continue to
 *   read as "a search" at the call site, while the code underneath
 *   is the same machinery in both cases.
 *
 * ── Why the hook exposes per-bucket state ───────────────────────
 *
 *   The store holds a four-bucket candidate record and a four-
 *   bucket progress record per topic, and the hook surfaces the
 *   specific topic's slot for each. `candidatesByBucket` carries
 *   the full record so a caller that wants to render four
 *   collapsible sections (one per bucket) has everything in one
 *   place. `candidates` is the flattened view across all four
 *   buckets, for callers that want a single list.
 *
 *   `searchProgress` is likewise a per-bucket record — the UI can
 *   show per-bucket progress bars and an overall progress bar
 *   (derived by summing the four buckets' counts) from the same
 *   field.
 *
 * ── Why the hook renames the store's actions ────────────────────
 *
 *   The plan's Part 1.9 annotation names two actions — `runSearch`
 *   and `fetchSelected`. The store's own actions are named
 *   `runFullSearch`, `runExtraction`, and `retryFailed` — the
 *   names it uses internally and in its own documentation. The
 *   hook exposes the plan-mandated names where the plan names
 *   them, and the store's names everywhere else.
 *
 * ── Why the actions are `useCallback`-wrapped ───────────────────
 *
 *   Zustand stores return stable action references for the life
 *   of the store, so returning them directly would already be
 *   stable. But the adapters that rename or reshape the store's
 *   actions (`runFullSearch`, `runSearch`) are thin wrappers, and
 *   a freshly-defined function inside a hook body has a new
 *   identity on every render. `useCallback` with the store's
 *   action as its sole dependency gives the wrapper a stable
 *   identity for as long as the store is alive.
 *
 * ── Why `fetchSelected` takes `(topicId, urls)` and not just `urls` ─
 *
 *   The plan's Part 1.9 annotation is `fetchSelected(urls)`. That
 *   signature is missing the topic ID, which the store needs:
 *   `runExtraction(topicId, urls)` writes `activeTopicId` and
 *   passes the topic to the IPC handler, which the main process
 *   uses to name the output folder on disk. Dropping the topic ID
 *   from the hook's signature would force the hook to read it
 *   from the store's `activeTopicId` — the value written by the
 *   last `runFullSearch` call.
 *
 *   Reading the topic ID from the store would work in the common
 *   case (the user searches topic A, then extracts topic A's
 *   selected URLs), but it would be wrong under the less common
 *   case where the user navigates between topics without
 *   searching — the store's `activeTopicId` would still name the
 *   first topic, and the extraction would write to the wrong
 *   folder.
 *
 *   The hook's signature therefore takes the topic ID explicitly.
 *   The component that calls it has the topic in hand (it was
 *   rendered from the topic's detail page), so passing it through
 *   is not friction — it is the correct amount of information for
 *   the operation to be unambiguous.
 *
 * ── Why the hook subscribes to each field separately ────────────
 *
 *   Same reasoning as `useTopics` and `useWriter`: Zustand
 *   re-renders a component when any of the selectors it
 *   subscribed to returns a different value, and subscribing
 *   per-field gives the finest-grained re-render behaviour.
 *
 *   Every selector additionally keys on the hook's `topicId`
 *   argument — a selector that reads
 *   `s.candidatesByTopic[topicId]` changes identity only when
 *   that specific topic's data changes, not when another topic's
 *   does. That per-topic keying is what makes the hook safe to
 *   mount twice for two different topics on the same page.
 *
 * ── What this hook does NOT do ──────────────────────────────────
 *
 *   - It does not call `runFullSearch` or `loadCandidates` on
 *     mount. The hook is a passive reader; the component that
 *     mounts it decides when to fetch. The Sources tab calls
 *     `runFullSearch` from a click handler and `loadCandidates`
 *     from a mount effect on the topic detail page.
 *
 *   - It does not guard against the "no session" case. The
 *     research feature is authenticated-only; its components
 *     render inside the layout's auth gate, which does not mount
 *     its children until a session exists. The hook trusts that
 *     invariant.
 *
 *   - It does not persist anything. The store is transient by
 *     design — see `researchStore.ts`'s file header.
 *
 *   - It does not expose `activeTopicId` or the internal
 *     `_handleMarker` dispatcher. The former is a store-internal
 *     bookkeeping field the components do not need; the latter is
 *     called by the module-load listener, never by a component.
 */

import { useCallback, useMemo } from 'react';

import {
  useResearchStore,
  type BucketConfig,
  type BucketKey,
  type CandidatesByBucket,
  type ExtractProgress,
  type FailedUrlRecord,
  type ResearchStoreStatus,
  type SearchCandidate,
  type SearchProgress,
} from '../store/researchStore.js';

// ============================================================
//  Public types
// ============================================================

/**
 * The shape of the object returned by `useResearch(topicId)`.
 *
 * Exported so that a helper function that takes "an instance of
 * the research hook" as a parameter can be typed against the
 * interface without redeclaring the shape. A component that
 * destructures the return value does not need to reference the
 * interface at all.
 *
 * Every data field is read from the topic the hook was called
 * with. Every action takes the topic ID as its first argument so
 * a caller that holds a topic reference in a callback does not
 * have to close over the hook's own `topicId` — the two are the
 * same value in practice, but passing it explicitly keeps the
 * call site readable and matches the shape `useWriter(topicId)`
 * establishes.
 */
export interface ResearchHookValue {
  // ---- Data ----

  /** The topic's candidates, flattened across all four time-range
   *  buckets. Empty until the first search completes for this
   *  topic. */
  candidates: SearchCandidate[];

  /** The topic's per-bucket candidate record. Every bucket key is
   *  present; a bucket the search has not touched holds an empty
   *  array. */
  candidatesByBucket: CandidatesByBucket;

  /** The topic's lifecycle state. See `ResearchStoreStatus` in
   *  `researchStore.ts` for the meaning of each value. */
  status: ResearchStoreStatus;

  /** The topic's per-bucket live search progress. A bucket's slot
   *  is null when that bucket is not currently searching. */
  searchProgress: Record<BucketKey, SearchProgress | null>;

  /** The topic's live extraction progress, or null when no
   *  extraction is in flight for this topic. */
  extractProgress: ExtractProgress | null;

  /** How many doc files the topic's last extraction saved. Zero
   *  before the first extraction and after a clear. */
  extractedCount: number;

  /** The topic's failed-URL records, loaded from the topic's
   *  `failures.json` file on first open. Empty until loaded, and
   *  when the file is absent. */
  failed: FailedUrlRecord[];

  /** The topic's last error message from a search or extraction,
   *  or null. */
  error: string | null;

  // ---- Actions ----

  /** Run the four-bucket search for a topic. Resolves with `true`
   *  when every configured bucket's IPC call succeeded, `false` on
   *  any failure. */
  runFullSearch: (
    topicId: string,
    title: string,
    keywords: string[],
    bucketConfig: BucketConfig
  ) => Promise<boolean>;

  /** Backward-compat single-bucket search. Re-runs one time-range
   *  bucket without touching the others (the store's
   *  `runFullSearch` skips zero-count buckets). The `bucket`
   *  argument defaults to `'anytime'` when omitted. */
  runSearch: (
    topicId: string,
    title: string,
    keywords?: string[],
    bucket?: BucketKey
  ) => Promise<boolean>;

  /** Extract the selected URLs into doc files. Resolves with the
   *  number of docs saved (zero on failure, with `error` set). */
  fetchSelected: (
    topicId: string,
    urls: string[]
  ) => Promise<number>;

  /** Re-run extraction for the topic's failed URLs only. Resolves
   *  with the number of docs saved by the retry. */
  retryFailed: (topicId: string) => Promise<number>;

  /** Read the topic's saved candidates and failed-URL records
   *  from disk. Resolves once the load completes. Subsequent
   *  calls for a topic that already has a candidates slot are
   *  no-ops. */
  loadCandidates: (topicId: string) => Promise<void>;

  /** Cancel whatever research operation is running for the topic. */
  cancel: (topicId: string) => Promise<void>;

  /** Empty the topic's candidate list. */
  clearCandidates: (topicId: string) => void;

  /** Clear the topic's last error message. */
  clearError: (topicId: string) => void;
}

// ============================================================
//  Internal constants
// ============================================================
//  Shared empty containers with stable module-level identity.
//  The hook returns these when a topic has no data yet — a fresh
//  object per render would change identity on every render, which
//  would defeat memoization on the consumer side and would make
//  any `useEffect` keyed on the field re-run on every render.
//
//  The objects are never mutated by this module or (by contract)
//  by any consumer. A consumer that wants to add to one of the
//  arrays is expected to build a new array — the same rule that
//  applies to every other container the store hands out.

const EMPTY_BUCKETS: CandidatesByBucket = {
  anytime: [],
  year: [],
  month: [],
  week: [],
};

const EMPTY_PROGRESS_BUCKETS: Record<
  BucketKey,
  SearchProgress | null
> = {
  anytime: null,
  year: null,
  month: null,
  week: null,
};

const EMPTY_FAILED: FailedUrlRecord[] = [];

// ============================================================
//  The hook
// ============================================================

/**
 * Return the research feature's state and its actions. See the
 * file header for the hook's role, its relationship to the store,
 * and the reasoning behind the design choices.
 */
export const useResearch = (topicId: string): ResearchHookValue => {
  // ---- Data subscriptions ----
  // One selector per data field, each keyed on the hook's
  // `topicId` argument. See the file header for why the fields
  // are read individually and why the keying matters.
  //
  // The raw selectors can each return `undefined` when the topic
  // has no entry in the corresponding record. The derived
  // bindings below apply the module-level fallback constants so
  // the interface's non-optional fields always carry a value.
  const candidatesByBucketRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.candidatesByTopic[topicId]
  );
  const statusRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.statusByTopic[topicId]
  );
  const searchProgressRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.searchProgressByTopic[topicId]
  );
  const extractProgressRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.extractProgressByTopic[topicId]
  );
  const extractedCountRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.extractedCountByTopic[topicId]
  );
  const failedRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.failedByTopic[topicId]
  );
  const errorRaw = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.errorByTopic[topicId]
  );

  // ---- Derived fields ----
  const candidatesByBucket: CandidatesByBucket =
    candidatesByBucketRaw ?? EMPTY_BUCKETS;
  const status: ResearchStoreStatus = statusRaw ?? 'idle';
  const searchProgress: Record<
    BucketKey,
    SearchProgress | null
  > = searchProgressRaw ?? EMPTY_PROGRESS_BUCKETS;
  const extractProgress: ExtractProgress | null =
    extractProgressRaw ?? null;
  const extractedCount: number = extractedCountRaw ?? 0;
  const failed: FailedUrlRecord[] = failedRaw ?? EMPTY_FAILED;
  const error: string | null = errorRaw ?? null;

  // The flattened view across all four buckets. Memoized on the
  // per-topic bucket record's identity so a consumer that
  // depends on `candidates` does not see a new array on every
  // render — the memo's dependency is stable across unrelated
  // store updates because the raw per-topic slot's identity does
  // not change when another topic's data changes.
  const candidates = useMemo((): SearchCandidate[] => {
    return [
      ...candidatesByBucket.anytime,
      ...candidatesByBucket.year,
      ...candidatesByBucket.month,
      ...candidatesByBucket.week,
    ];
  }, [candidatesByBucket]);

  // ---- Action subscriptions ----
  // Zustand actions are stable references for the life of the
  // store, so selecting them here does not cause re-renders.
  const storeRunFullSearch = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.runFullSearch
  );
  const storeRunExtraction = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.runExtraction
  );
  const storeRetryFailed = useResearchStore((s) => s.retryFailed);
  const storeCancelOperation = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.cancelOperation
  );
  const storeLoadCandidates = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.loadCandidates
  );
  const storeClearCandidates = useResearchStore(
    (s) => s.clearCandidates
  );
  const storeClearError = useResearchStore((s) => s.clearError);

  // ---- Adapters ----
  // Each adapter wraps a store action to give it a stable
  // identity across renders. See the file header for why.

  const runFullSearch = useCallback(
    async (
      id: string,
      title: string,
      keywords: string[],
      bucketConfig: BucketConfig
    ): Promise<boolean> => {
      return storeRunFullSearch(id, title, keywords, bucketConfig);
    },
    [storeRunFullSearch]
  );

  // The backward-compat single-bucket search. The default bucket
  // is 'anytime' and the default count is 20 — the same value
  // the IPC handler's pre-bucketed `DEFAULT_MAX_RESULTS` used,
  // so an existing caller that provided only a title gets the
  // same behavior it used to. The store's `runFullSearch` skips
  // zero-count buckets, so only the requested bucket searches.
  const runSearch = useCallback(
    async (
      id: string,
      title: string,
      keywords?: string[],
      bucket: BucketKey = 'anytime'
    ): Promise<boolean> => {
      const bucketConfig: BucketConfig = {
        anyTime: bucket === 'anytime' ? 20 : 0,
        pastYear: bucket === 'year' ? 20 : 0,
        pastMonth: bucket === 'month' ? 20 : 0,
        pastWeek: bucket === 'week' ? 20 : 0,
      };
      return storeRunFullSearch(
        id,
        title,
        keywords ?? [],
        bucketConfig
      );
    },
    [storeRunFullSearch]
  );

  const fetchSelected = useCallback(
    async (id: string, urls: string[]): Promise<number> => {
      return storeRunExtraction(id, urls);
    },
    [storeRunExtraction]
  );

  const retryFailed = useCallback(
    async (id: string): Promise<number> => {
      return storeRetryFailed(id);
    },
    [storeRetryFailed]
  );

  const loadCandidates = useCallback(
    async (id: string): Promise<void> => {
      return storeLoadCandidates(id);
    },
    [storeLoadCandidates]
  );

  const cancel = useCallback(
    async (id: string): Promise<void> => {
      return storeCancelOperation(id);
    },
    [storeCancelOperation]
  );

  const clearCandidates = useCallback(
    (id: string): void => {
      storeClearCandidates(id);
    },
    [storeClearCandidates]
  );

  const clearError = useCallback(
    (id: string): void => {
      storeClearError(id);
    },
    [storeClearError]
  );

  // ---- Return ----
  // A single object literal with all sixteen members. The order
  // matches the interface above so a reader comparing the two
  // does not have to hunt for a member's position.
  return {
    candidates,
    candidatesByBucket,
    status,
    searchProgress,
    extractProgress,
    extractedCount,
    failed,
    error,
    runFullSearch,
    runSearch,
    fetchSelected,
    retryFailed,
    loadCandidates,
    cancel,
    clearCandidates,
    clearError,
  };
};

// ============================================================
//  Default export
// ============================================================

/**
 * The hook as the default export, matching the convention used by
 * every other hook and store in the codebase. Consumers that
 * prefer the named form use `import { useResearch } from ...`;
 * consumers that prefer the default form use
 * `import useResearch from ...`. Both resolve to the same hook.
 */
export default useResearch;
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/ipc/researchIPC.ts">
/**
 * researchIPC — IPC handlers for the research feature (main process).
 *
 * Registers the six channels the renderer's
 * `window.electronAPI.research.*` methods invoke:
 *
 *   research:search        — run a search operation on a topic.
 *                            Delegates to `searchService.runSearch`.
 *   research:extract       — fetch and extract a batch of URLs.
 *                            Delegates to `extractionService.runExtract`.
 *   research:getCandidates — read the topic's saved candidate list
 *                            from `<topicFolder>/candidates.json`.
 *   research:getFailures   — read the topic's failed-URL records
 *                            from `<topicFolder>/failures.json`.
 *   research:clearFailures — delete the topic's `failures.json`
 *                            file.
 *   research:cancel        — stop the currently-running research
 *                            operation for a topic.
 *
 * Each handler:
 *
 *   1. Validates the arguments the renderer sent. A malformed
 *      argument — a non-string topicId, an empty URL array, a
 *      missing title — is rejected with a specific error message
 *      before any service is called. The service would also
 *      reject these (each service validates its own inputs), but
 *      the handler's check produces a clearer error and avoids
 *      the overhead of a service call that is guaranteed to
 *      fail.
 *
 *   2. Delegates to the service. The service is where the
 *      interesting work happens — spawning Python, watching
 *      markers, waiting for a terminal result. The handler does
 *      not duplicate any of that logic.
 *
 *   3. Returns the result in the shared `ApiResponse<T>` envelope.
 *      `{ success: true, data }` on success, `{ success: false,
 *      error }` on failure. The renderer's preload passes this
 *      envelope through unchanged, so the renderer's service
 *      layer sees the same shape it would see from the backend's
 *      HTTP API.
 *
 * Why the `research:search` handler's return value does not
 * carry the full candidate list:
 *
 *   The search service returns a `SearchServiceResult` whose
 *   `candidates` field carries the full list of `SearchCandidate`
 *   objects (url, title, snippet, platform). But the renderer
 *   receives that list through a DIFFERENT path — the
 *   `research:marker` event stream that the search service
 *   broadcasts as the Python script emits `SEARCH_RESULT`. The
 *   renderer's `researchStore` listens on that channel and
 *   populates the candidate list from the marker payload.
 *
 *   The handler's IPC return value is therefore a SUMMARY, not
 *   the data itself: it carries the list of URLs as a convenience
 *   for callers that only want the URLs (a test, a diagnostic
 *   tool), and it carries the terminal success/failure status. If
 *   the marker channel and the IPC return value disagree — a race
 *   where the marker was not yet delivered when the IPC resolved
 *   — the marker channel's data wins, because the marker channel
 *   is the streaming source of truth and the IPC return value is
 *   a summary.
 *
 *   The full candidate list, in its bucket-keyed shape, is
 *   available via the separate `research:getCandidates` channel,
 *   which reads the `candidates.json` file that the search service
 *   writes after a successful search. That channel is what the
 *   renderer's `loadCandidates` action uses on topic open; the
 *   `research:search` return value is not used for that purpose.
 *
 * The cancel path:
 *
 *   `research:cancel` looks up the process manager for the
 *   topic's search operation and calls its `kill()`, which
 *   tree-kills the Python subprocess (and any child processes it
 *   spawned). This is a HARD cancel — the graceful path, which
 *   writes a `STOP` command to the child's stdin, is available
 *   via `manager.writeStdin('STOP\n')` and is used internally by
 *   the automation store's Stop button. The IPC cancel is the
 *   escape hatch for the case where the graceful path is not
 *   working (the script is hung on a network call) or where the
 *   user wants the operation to end immediately.
 *
 *   The handler returns `{ success: true, data: null }` whether
 *   or not a manager was found. Cancelling an operation that has
 *   already finished is not an error — the user's intent ("the
 *   operation should not be running") is satisfied either way.
 *
 * The extractionService forward reference:
 *
 *   The `research:extract` handler imports `runExtract` from
 *   `../services/extractionService.js`. That file is created in
 *   Session 12, step 91 — a later session than this one. Until it
 *   exists, the import at the top of this file produces a single
 *   `TS2307` error. That is EXPECTED and documented in the
 *   TypeScript Integrity Check below.
 *
 *   The alternative — writing the handler with a stub body that
 *   throws "not yet implemented" — is forbidden by the strict
 *   protocol's "no stubs" rule. The handler is written in full,
 *   referencing the module that will exist; the forward-reference
 *   pattern is the same one used throughout this codebase.
 *
 * What this file does NOT do:
 *
 *   - It does not parse markers. Marker parsing and forwarding is
 *     the search service's job (and eventually the extraction
 *     service's job). The IPC handler sees only the terminal
 *     result.
 *
 *   - It does not manage Chrome. Chrome lifecycle is owned by
 *     `chromeLauncher.ts`, which the services call. The IPC
 *     handler has no visibility into Chrome.
 *
 *   - It does not touch the encrypted store. Research operations
 *     are stateless from the main process's perspective; any
 *     persistence happens through the backend's HTTP API, which
 *     the services call directly when they need to.
 *
 *   - It does not validate the schema of the service's result.
 *     The service is written to always resolve with a
 *     well-formed `SearchServiceResult` or `ExtractionServiceResult`;
 *     the handler trusts that contract and forwards the shape.
 */

import { ipcMain, app, type IpcMainInvokeEvent } from 'electron';
import { promises as fs } from 'node:fs';
import path from 'node:path';

import {
  runSearch,
  getSearchTaskId,
  type BucketKey,
  type CandidatesByBucket,
  type SearchCandidate,
} from '../services/searchService.js';
import { runExtract } from '../services/extractionService.js';
import { getByTaskId } from '../services/childProcessManager.js';
import { apiGet } from '../../shared/api/client.js';
import type {
  ApiResponse,
  Topic,
} from '../../shared/types/index.js';

// =============================================================================
//  Channel name constants
// =============================================================================
//
//  The six channel names are declared as constants so a future
//  refactor that renames them (or a test that spies on them) has
//  one place to change. The preload's `src/main/preload.ts`
//  declares the same strings on the renderer side of the bridge —
//  the two must stay in sync, but neither file imports from the
//  other, because the preload is a renderer-context module and this
//  file is a main-context module.

const CHANNEL_SEARCH = 'research:search';
const CHANNEL_EXTRACT = 'research:extract';
const CHANNEL_GET_CANDIDATES = 'research:getCandidates';
const CHANNEL_GET_FAILURES = 'research:getFailures';
const CHANNEL_CLEAR_FAILURES = 'research:clearFailures';
const CHANNEL_CANCEL = 'research:cancel';

// ============================================================
//  On-disk layout
// ============================================================
//
//  The topic folder convention. Same values every other
//  main-process module that touches the topic folder uses
//  (`writerIPC.ts`, `validationIPC.ts`, `documentService.ts`).
//  Duplicated across files because the convention is a cross-file
//  contract and the plan's tree declares no shared layout module
//  for the desktop main process. A change here must be reflected
//  in the sibling files and on the Python side in the same commit.

/** The app slug used as the top-level folder under the user's
 *  application-data directory. Must match the Electron side's
 *  `main/store.ts` slug and the Python side's
 *  `core/file_io._APP_SLUG`. */
const APP_SLUG = 'writespeare';

/** The subfolder under the app slug that holds every topic. */
const TOPICS_SUBFOLDER = 'topics';

/** The filename of the per-topic candidate list, written by the
 *  search service after a successful search. */
const CANDIDATES_FILE = 'candidates.json';

/** The filename of the per-topic failed-URL records, written by
 *  the Python extraction dispatcher when a batch finishes with
 *  at least one failure. */
const FAILURES_FILE = 'failures.json';

/** The set of legal time-range bucket identifiers, as a runtime
 *  array. Its type is `readonly BucketKey[]`, which guarantees at
 *  compile time that every string is a legal member of the union
 *  the search service declares — a typo would be a type error,
 *  not a silent runtime surprise. */
const BUCKET_KEYS: readonly BucketKey[] = [
  'anytime',
  'year',
  'month',
  'week',
];

/** Narrow an `unknown` value to one of the four legal bucket
 *  identifiers. Used by `handleSearch` to validate the
 *  `timeRange` argument the renderer sends. */
const isBucketKey = (value: unknown): value is BucketKey =>
  typeof value === 'string' &&
  (BUCKET_KEYS as readonly string[]).includes(value);

// ============================================================
//  Shared types
// ============================================================

/**
 * One failed-extraction record, as stored in the topic's
 * `failures.json` file.
 *
 * The shape mirrors what the Python extraction dispatcher writes
 * (`research/extract_dispatch.py`) and what the renderer's
 * research store declares
 * (`features/research/store/researchStore.ts`). All three
 * declarations must stay in sync; the duplication is the same
 * pattern `SearchCandidate` follows between `searchService.ts`
 * and the renderer's store.
 */
export interface FailedUrlRecord {
  /** The URL that failed to extract. */
  url: string;

  /** A short classification of the failure — e.g. `'timeout'`,
   *  `'site_returned_404'`, `'empty_content'`. */
  reason: string;

  /** How many times this URL has been attempted across runs. */
  attemptCount: number;

  /** ISO-8601 timestamp of the most recent attempt. */
  lastAttemptAt: string;

  /** The full error message from the most recent attempt, if the
   *  dispatcher captured one. Empty string when none was. */
  errorMessage: string;
}

// ============================================================
//  Path helpers
// ============================================================

/**
 * Return the absolute path to a topic's folder. Same layout every
 * other main-process module that touches the topic folder uses.
 */
const getTopicFolder = (topicId: string): string => {
  return path.join(
    app.getPath('appData'),
    APP_SLUG,
    TOPICS_SUBFOLDER,
    topicId
  );
};

/**
 * Return the absolute path to a topic's `candidates.json` file.
 */
const getCandidatesPath = (topicId: string): string => {
  return path.join(getTopicFolder(topicId), CANDIDATES_FILE);
};

/**
 * Return the absolute path to a topic's `failures.json` file.
 */
const getFailuresPath = (topicId: string): string => {
  return path.join(getTopicFolder(topicId), FAILURES_FILE);
};

// ============================================================
//  File I/O helpers
// ============================================================

/**
 * Read a JSON file and return its parsed value, or null when the
 * file does not exist or cannot be parsed.
 *
 * Never throws. A missing file is a legitimate state — a fresh
 * topic has no `candidates.json` until its first search and no
 * `failures.json` until its first failed extraction — and a
 * malformed file is a bug in whatever wrote it, not something
 * the caller can act on. Both cases collapse to "no data", which
 * is what the callers expect.
 */
const readJsonFileOrNull = async (
  filepath: string
): Promise<unknown> => {
  try {
    const raw = await fs.readFile(filepath, 'utf-8');
    return JSON.parse(raw);
  } catch {
    return null;
  }
};

/**
 * Delete a file if it exists, swallowing ENOENT.
 *
 * Used by the `clearFailures` handler. The caller's intent is
 * "the file should not exist"; a file that is already gone
 * satisfies that intent, and reporting a failure for it would
 * produce a spurious error toast on the renderer. Any other
 * filesystem error (a permission denial, a directory in the
 * file's place) IS propagated so the caller can surface it.
 */
const deleteFileIfExists = async (filepath: string): Promise<void> => {
  try {
    await fs.unlink(filepath);
  } catch (err) {
    // ENOENT is the "file does not exist" code. Node surfaces it
    // as the `code` field on the thrown error; the narrow cast
    // reaches the field without widening the type of `err`.
    const code = (err as NodeJS.ErrnoException).code;
    if (code === 'ENOENT') {
      return;
    }
    throw err;
  }
};

// ============================================================
//  Backend helpers
// ============================================================

/**
 * Fetch the topic's `sourceCount` from the backend.
 *
 * The value is used as the search's `maxResults` — the number of
 * candidates to collect. The plan's preset-wiring work (PR-2 of
 * the approved runbook) replaced the previous hard-coded
 * `DEFAULT_MAX_RESULTS = 20` with a per-topic value read from
 * the topic's own record. Once PR-3 lands and the search runs
 * once per time-range bucket, the value the caller needs becomes
 * the topic's per-bucket configuration rather than the aggregate
 * `sourceCount`; the change is to this helper's return shape and
 * the caller's use of it.
 *
 * Throws when the topic cannot be fetched — a backend error, an
 * expired session, a 404 for an unknown topic. The caller
 * (`handleSearch`) catches and converts the throw into an error
 * envelope. A search whose source count is unknown cannot be run
 * meaningfully, so failing fast is the honest behavior.
 */
const fetchTopicSourceCount = async (topicId: string): Promise<number> => {
  const result = await apiGet<Topic>(
    `/topics/${encodeURIComponent(topicId)}`
  );
  if (result.success && result.data) {
    return result.data.sourceCount;
  }
  throw new Error(result.error ?? 'Topic could not be loaded.');
};

// ============================================================
//  research:search
// ============================================================

/**
 * Run a search operation on a topic.
 *
 * Arguments sent by the renderer:
 *
 *   topicId   — the topic's ID. Required, non-empty string.
 *   title     — the search query, normally the topic's title.
 *               Required, non-empty string.
 *   keywords  — the user's keywords. Optional; an empty or
 *               missing list means the search runs on the title
 *               alone.
 *
 * Returns:
 *
 *   `{ success: true, data: { candidates: string[] } }` when the
 *   search produced at least one candidate. The `candidates` array
 *   is the list of URLs — the full candidate objects (title,
 *   snippet, platform) arrive separately through the marker
 *   stream's `SEARCH_RESULT` payload.
 *
 *   `{ success: false, error: string }` on any failure. The error
 *   string is user-facing — the renderer's research store displays
 *   it in the SearchProgress panel.
 */
const handleSearch = async (
  _event: IpcMainInvokeEvent,
  topicId: unknown,
  title: unknown,
  keywords: unknown,
  timeRange: unknown
): Promise<ApiResponse<{ candidates: string[] }>> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  if (typeof topicId !== 'string' || topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'topicId is required.' };
  }
  if (typeof title !== 'string' || title.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'title is required.' };
  }

  // The timeRange argument identifies which time-range bucket
  // this search invocation is for. It is validated against the
  // closed set the search service's `BucketKey` union declares.
  //
  // The value is NOT yet passed to `runSearch` — the search
  // service's `SearchServiceOptions` interface does not accept a
  // `timeRange` option in this session; adding that plumbing is
  // PR-3 of the approved runbook. The handler accepts and
  // validates the argument now so that the renderer's per-bucket
  // call shape is stable across the PR boundary and so that an
  // invalid value is rejected before any service call runs.
  if (!isBucketKey(timeRange)) {
    return {
      success: false,
      error:
        'timeRange must be one of: anytime, year, month, week.',
    };
  }

  // The keywords argument is optional and may arrive as
  // `undefined` (the renderer omits it) or as an array of strings.
  // Any non-array, non-undefined value is a malformed call and is
  // rejected rather than coerced.
  let normalizedKeywords: string[] = [];
  if (keywords !== undefined) {
    if (!Array.isArray(keywords)) {
      return { success: false, error: 'keywords must be an array.' };
    }
    for (const k of keywords) {
      if (typeof k !== 'string') {
        return {
          success: false,
          error: 'keywords must contain only strings.',
        };
      }
      const trimmed = k.trim();
      if (trimmed.length > 0) {
        normalizedKeywords.push(trimmed);
      }
    }
  }

  // ---- Fetch the topic's source count ----
  // The search's max-results value comes from the topic's own
  // record rather than a hard-coded constant. See the
  // `fetchTopicSourceCount` helper for the reasoning and for the
  // PR-3 follow-up.
  let maxResults: number;
  try {
    maxResults = await fetchTopicSourceCount(topicId.trim());
  } catch (err) {
    const message =
      err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err);
    return {
      success: false,
      error: `Could not load the topic: ${message}`,
    };
  }

  // ---- Delegate ----
  const result = await runSearch({
    query: title.trim(),
    keywords: normalizedKeywords,
    maxResults,
    topicId: topicId.trim(),
  });

  // ---- Envelope ----
  if (result.kind === 'success') {
    return {
      success: true,
      data: {
        candidates: result.candidates.map((c) => c.url),
      },
    };
  }

  return {
    success: false,
    error: result.error ?? 'Search failed.',
  };
};

// ============================================================
//  research:extract
// ============================================================

/**
 * Fetch and extract a batch of URLs into doc files.
 *
 * Arguments sent by the renderer:
 *
 *   topicId  — the topic's ID. Required, non-empty string.
 *   urls     — the URLs the user selected for extraction.
 *               Required, non-empty array of non-empty strings.
 *
 * Returns:
 *
 *   `{ success: true, data: { savedCount: number } }` when the
 *   extraction completed. `savedCount` is the number of doc files
 *   successfully written; it may be less than `urls.length` if
 *   some pages failed, and the renderer's document list reflects
 *   the actual set of saved files (delivered via the marker
 *   stream).
 *
 *   `{ success: false, error: string }` on any failure.
 *
 * Forward reference: `runExtract` is imported from
 * `../services/extractionService.js`, which is created in
 * Session 12, step 91. Until that file exists, the import at the
 * top of this module produces a single `TS2307`. See the file
 * header for why the handler is written in full rather than
 * stubbed.
 */
const handleExtract = async (
  _event: IpcMainInvokeEvent,
  topicId: unknown,
  urls: unknown
): Promise<ApiResponse<{ savedCount: number }>> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  if (typeof topicId !== 'string' || topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'topicId is required.' };
  }
  if (!Array.isArray(urls) || urls.length === 0) {
    return {
      success: false,
      error: 'urls must be a non-empty array.',
    };
  }
  const normalizedUrls: string[] = [];
  for (const u of urls) {
    if (typeof u !== 'string' || u.trim().length === 0) {
      return {
        success: false,
        error: 'urls must contain only non-empty strings.',
      };
    }
    normalizedUrls.push(u.trim());
  }

  // ---- Delegate ----
  const result = await runExtract({
    topicId: topicId.trim(),
    urls: normalizedUrls,
  });

  // ---- Envelope ----
  if (result.success) {
    return {
      success: true,
      data: { savedCount: result.savedCount },
    };
  }

  return {
    success: false,
    error: result.error ?? 'Extraction failed.',
  };
};

// ============================================================
//  research:getCandidates
// ============================================================

/**
 * Read the topic's saved candidate list from disk.
 *
 * The list is written by `searchService.runSearch` after every
 * successful search. Its shape is the bucket-keyed
 * `CandidatesByBucket` record the renderer's research store
 * expects — four buckets, each an array of `SearchCandidate`
 * objects. In this session, all candidates land under the
 * `anytime` bucket and the other three are empty; PR-3 changes
 * the writer to route each per-bucket search's results to its
 * own slot.
 *
 * A missing file — a topic that has never had a search run, or
 * whose `candidates.json` was deleted — is a legitimate state,
 * not an error. The handler returns a fully-populated bucket
 * record with empty arrays in that case, matching the renderer's
 * `loadCandidates` action's expectation that an un-searched
 * topic populates its slot with an empty bucket record rather
 * than leaving it undefined.
 *
 * A malformed file — content that does not parse, or parses to
 * something other than an object — is also treated as "no saved
 * candidates". The alternative (surfacing the malformed file as
 * an error) would leave the user's Sources tab unable to load
 * until the file was deleted by hand, which is a worse outcome
 * than silently ignoring a corrupt cache.
 */
const handleGetCandidates = async (
  _event: IpcMainInvokeEvent,
  topicId: unknown
): Promise<ApiResponse<CandidatesByBucket>> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  if (typeof topicId !== 'string' || topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'topicId is required.' };
  }

  const empty: CandidatesByBucket = {
    anytime: [],
    year: [],
    month: [],
    week: [],
  };

  // ---- Read the file ----
  const filepath = getCandidatesPath(topicId.trim());
  const raw = await readJsonFileOrNull(filepath);

  // A missing or unparseable file → empty bucket record. See
  // the docstring for why this is not an error.
  if (raw === null || typeof raw !== 'object' || Array.isArray(raw)) {
    return { success: true, data: empty };
  }

  // ---- Narrow the parsed value into CandidatesByBucket ----
  // Each bucket must be an array; a bucket whose value is not an
  // array falls back to an empty array. Individual candidates
  // that do not match the `SearchCandidate` shape are skipped.
  const record = raw as Record<string, unknown>;
  const narrowBucket = (value: unknown): SearchCandidate[] => {
    if (!Array.isArray(value)) {
      return [];
    }
    const out: SearchCandidate[] = [];
    for (const entry of value) {
      if (typeof entry !== 'object' || entry === null) continue;
      const e = entry as Record<string, unknown>;
      const url = e['url'];
      const title = e['title'];
      if (typeof url !== 'string' || url.length === 0) continue;
      if (typeof title !== 'string' || title.length === 0) continue;
      const snippet =
        typeof e['snippet'] === 'string' ? e['snippet'] : '';
      const platform =
        typeof e['platform'] === 'string' ? e['platform'] : 'generic';
      out.push({ url, title, snippet, platform });
    }
    return out;
  };

  return {
    success: true,
    data: {
      anytime: narrowBucket(record['anytime']),
      year: narrowBucket(record['year']),
      month: narrowBucket(record['month']),
      week: narrowBucket(record['week']),
    },
  };
};

// ============================================================
//  research:getFailures
// ============================================================

/**
 * Read the topic's failed-URL records from disk.
 *
 * The list is written by the Python extraction dispatcher
 * (`research/extract_dispatch.py`) at the end of a batch that
 * had at least one failure. Each record carries the URL, a
 * short reason code, an attempt count, the last attempt's
 * timestamp, and the most recent error message.
 *
 * A missing file — a topic whose extractions have all
 * succeeded, or whose `failures.json` was deleted — returns an
 * empty array. The alternative (surfacing "no file" as an
 * error) would make the renderer's failed-URL banner appear for
 * every topic that has never had a failure, which is the wrong
 * visual state.
 *
 * A malformed file is treated the same as a missing one. A
 * corrupt cache should not leave the Sources tab permanently
 * unable to load.
 */
const handleGetFailures = async (
  _event: IpcMainInvokeEvent,
  topicId: unknown
): Promise<ApiResponse<FailedUrlRecord[]>> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  if (typeof topicId !== 'string' || topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'topicId is required.' };
  }

  // ---- Read the file ----
  const filepath = getFailuresPath(topicId.trim());
  const raw = await readJsonFileOrNull(filepath);

  if (!Array.isArray(raw)) {
    return { success: true, data: [] };
  }

  // ---- Narrow the parsed value into FailedUrlRecord[] ----
  const out: FailedUrlRecord[] = [];
  for (const entry of raw) {
    if (typeof entry !== 'object' || entry === null) continue;
    const e = entry as Record<string, unknown>;
    const url = e['url'];
    const reason = e['reason'];
    const attemptCount = e['attemptCount'];
    const lastAttemptAt = e['lastAttemptAt'];
    const errorMessage = e['errorMessage'];
    if (typeof url !== 'string' || url.length === 0) continue;
    if (typeof reason !== 'string') continue;
    if (typeof attemptCount !== 'number') continue;
    if (typeof lastAttemptAt !== 'string') continue;
    if (typeof errorMessage !== 'string') continue;
    out.push({
      url,
      reason,
      attemptCount,
      lastAttemptAt,
      errorMessage,
    });
  }
  return { success: true, data: out };
};

// ============================================================
//  research:clearFailures
// ============================================================

/**
 * Delete the topic's `failures.json` file.
 *
 * Called by the renderer when the user dismisses the last failed
 * URL from the Sources tab's failed-URL banner, or when the user
 * deletes a candidate and no other failures remain. The file's
 * absence is the state the UI is moving toward; deleting the
 * file is what makes the next `research:getFailures` call
 * return an empty list.
 *
 * A file that is already gone is a success — the caller's
 * intent ("the file should not exist") is satisfied either way.
 * Any other filesystem error (a permission denial, a directory
 * sitting in the file's place) is surfaced as an error envelope
 * so the caller can display a message.
 */
const handleClearFailures = async (
  _event: IpcMainInvokeEvent,
  topicId: unknown
): Promise<ApiResponse<null>> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  if (typeof topicId !== 'string' || topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'topicId is required.' };
  }

  // ---- Delete the file ----
  try {
    await deleteFileIfExists(getFailuresPath(topicId.trim()));
  } catch (err) {
    const message =
      err instanceof Error ? err.message : String(err);
    return {
      success: false,
      error: `Could not clear failures: ${message}`,
    };
  }

  return { success: true, data: null };
};

// ============================================================
//  research:cancel
// ============================================================

/**
 * Cancel the currently-running research operation for a topic.
 *
 * The cancel is a HARD kill — the process manager's `kill()`
 * method, which uses `tree-kill` to terminate the Python
 * subprocess and every child it spawned. The graceful path (a
 * `STOP` command written to the child's stdin, which the Python
 * script's checkpoint detects and acts on at its next safe point)
 * is available via the manager's `writeStdin` method; a caller
 * that wants the graceful behaviour uses that method directly
 * (it is what the automation store's Stop button does). The IPC
 * cancel is the escape hatch for the cases where the graceful
 * path does not apply — a hung script, an unresponsive network
 * call, or a user who wants the operation to end immediately.
 *
 * Arguments sent by the renderer:
 *
 *   topicId  — the topic's ID. Required, non-empty string.
 *
 * Returns:
 *
 *   Always `{ success: true, data: null }`. Cancelling an
 *   operation that has already finished — or that never started —
 *   is not an error; the user's intent ("this operation should
 *   not be running") is satisfied either way. Reporting a
 *   failure because there was nothing to cancel would produce a
 *   spurious error toast on the renderer.
 */
const handleCancel = async (
  _event: IpcMainInvokeEvent,
  topicId: unknown
): Promise<ApiResponse<null>> => {
  // ---- Validate ----
  if (typeof topicId !== 'string' || topicId.trim().length === 0) {
    return { success: false, error: 'topicId is required.' };
  }

  // ---- Look up the manager and kill ----
  // The task ID is derived from the topic ID by the same helper
  // the search service uses, so the two agree on the key. A
  // missing manager (no operation has ever started for this topic,
  // or the previous one finished and was disposed) is a no-op.
  const taskId = getSearchTaskId(topicId.trim());
  const manager = getByTaskId(taskId);
  if (manager !== null) {
    manager.kill();
  }

  return { success: true, data: null };
};

// ============================================================
//  registerResearchIPC
// ============================================================

/**
 * Register the research feature's IPC handlers.
 *
 * Called once, from `main/index.ts`, inside the
 * `app.whenReady().then(...)` callback — the same place the auth
 * and topics features' registration functions are called. The
 * function is not idempotent: `ipcMain.handle` refuses to register
 * a second handler for the same channel, and a second call would
 * therefore produce Electron errors. The single-call contract is
 * the same one every other feature's `register*IPC()` function
 * follows.
 */
export const registerResearchIPC = (): void => {
  ipcMain.handle(CHANNEL_SEARCH, handleSearch);
  ipcMain.handle(CHANNEL_EXTRACT, handleExtract);
  ipcMain.handle(CHANNEL_GET_CANDIDATES, handleGetCandidates);
  ipcMain.handle(CHANNEL_GET_FAILURES, handleGetFailures);
  ipcMain.handle(CHANNEL_CLEAR_FAILURES, handleClearFailures);
  ipcMain.handle(CHANNEL_CANCEL, handleCancel);
};

// ============================================================
//  Default export
// ============================================================

/**
 * The registration function as the default export, matching the
 * convention used by every other IPC module in the codebase.
 * `main/index.ts` uses the named import
 * (`import { registerResearchIPC } from ...`), so the default form
 * is a convenience rather than a load-bearing export.
 */
export default registerResearchIPC;
</file>

<file path="apps/desktop/src/features/research/services/chromeLauncher.ts">
/**
 * chromeLauncher — Chrome process lifecycle for the main process.
 *
 * Launches a Chrome instance with a Chrome DevTools Protocol (CDP)
 * debug port and a caller-supplied persistent user-data directory,
 * waits until the debug port is accepting connections, and returns
 * a handle the caller can use to kill the instance later. The
 * Chrome that this module launches is the browser the Python
 * automation scripts connect to over CDP — a script's
 * `core.browser.connect_to_chrome(port)` call attaches to the
 * Chrome instance this module started.
 *
 * The lifecycle model — Chrome outlives the caller:
 *
 *   A Chrome instance is not tied to the lifetime of whatever
 *   operation triggered its launch. The plan's user journey has the
 *   user log into DeepSeek ONCE, into a persistent profile, and
 *   then reuse that session across every future search and every
 *   future article — the login is not repeated per operation. The
 *   Chrome instance that holds the login therefore has to live as
 *   long as the app, not as long as one search.
 *
 *   This module reflects that model: it does NOT auto-kill Chrome
 *   when a caller's promise resolves. It returns a handle; the
 *   caller decides when (and whether) to kill. The app's own
 *   shutdown path (`before-quit`) calls `killAllChrome()`, which is
 *   the one place that reliably terminates every tracked instance.
 *
 * The instance registry:
 *
 *   Every successful `launchChrome` call registers the instance in
 *   a module-level array. The registry exists for two reasons:
 *
 *     1. Shutdown cleanup. `killAllChrome()` iterates the registry
 *        and kills each instance, releasing every allocated port.
 *        Without a registry, the shutdown path would have to hold
 *        its own list of instances, duplicating this module's
 *        tracking.
 *
 *     2. Diagnostics. `getRunningInstances()` returns a snapshot of
 *        the registry, which a future diagnostics panel or a test
 *        can use to confirm that instances are being cleaned up
 *        correctly.
 *
 *   The registry removes an instance as soon as it has been killed
 *   — either by `killChrome` or by `killAllChrome`. It never
 *   contains a dead instance, so a caller that queries it is
 *   looking at live Chrome processes only.
 *
 * Why killing uses `tree-kill` and not `child.kill()`:
 *
 *   Chrome spawns a tree of child processes — a browser process,
 *   renderer processes (one per tab), a GPU process, a network
 *   process, and others. Killing the root Chrome process with
 *   `child.kill()` leaves the children orphaned on Windows, where
 *   they keep running until they notice their parent is gone (which
 *   can take a while) or until the user kills them manually. An
 *   app that leaked orphaned Chrome processes on every shutdown
 *   would be a well-known complaint within a week.
 *
 *   The `tree-kill` package (already a dependency in
 *   `apps/desktop/package.json`) walks the process tree and
 *   terminates every descendant. It is the standard solution to
 *   this exact problem in the Electron ecosystem.
 *
 * The readiness check — why `waitForDebugPort` polls rather than
 * listening for a specific event:
 *
 *   Chrome has no "I am ready" event a launcher can subscribe to.
 *   The only signal that its debug server is up is the CDP HTTP
 *   endpoint at `http://localhost:<port>/json/version` responding
 *   with a JSON payload. The launcher polls that endpoint with a
 *   short interval until it answers or a timeout expires. This
 *   matches the behavior of the Python side's
 *   `core/browser.py:wait_for_debug_port`, which does the same
 *   poll for the same reason.
 *
 * What this module does NOT do:
 *
 *   - It does not connect to Chrome over CDP. That is the Python
 *     side's job (`core/browser.py:connect_to_chrome`). This
 *     module's only responsibility is to get a Chrome process
 *     running with a debug port open.
 *
 *   - It does not manage the persistent profile's contents. The
 *     caller supplies the `userDataDir` path; this module creates
 *     the directory if it does not exist (Chrome requires the
 *     parent to exist) but does not touch any file inside it.
 *
 *   - It does not decide when a Chrome instance should be killed.
 *     The caller (the app's `before-quit` handler, a cancellation
 *     path, or a test) makes that decision and calls `killChrome`
 *     or `killAllChrome`.
 *
 *   - It does not fall back to a different Chrome if the primary
 *     one fails to launch. A launch failure surfaces as a rejected
 *     promise with a specific error; the caller's error path
 *     decides what to do.
 *
 *   - It does not verify that the CDP port belongs to the Chrome
 *     instance it just launched. Two Chrome instances on the same
 *     port is impossible — the OS refuses the second bind — and
 *     the allocator (`portAllocator.ts`) guarantees that two
 *     concurrent launches get distinct ports. The check would add
 *     no value.
 *
 * Dependencies: Node stdlib + electron + `tree-kill` (declared in
 * `apps/desktop/package.json`) + `portAllocator` (Session 10,
 * step 71).
 */

import { spawn, type ChildProcess } from 'node:child_process';
import { existsSync } from 'node:fs';
import path from 'node:path';
import treeKill from 'tree-kill';

import { allocatePort, releasePort } from './portAllocator.js';

// =============================================================================
//  Public types
// =============================================================================

/**
 * A handle to a running Chrome instance.
 *
 * Returned by `launchChrome` on success. The caller passes it back
 * to `killChrome` when it wants to terminate the instance. It is
 * also what `getRunningInstances` returns for each live instance.
 *
 * The fields are all `readonly` — a caller must not mutate the
 * handle. Termination goes through `killChrome`, which updates the
 * registry; a caller that set `instance.process = null` directly
 * would corrupt the registry's bookkeeping without telling the
 * registry anything had happened.
 */
export interface ChromeInstance {
  /** The Chrome process. `null` after `killChrome` has been called
   *  — the registry sets it to null before removing the instance,
   *  and callers that hold the handle can still read the field. */
  readonly process: ChildProcess | null;

  /** The CDP debug port Chrome is listening on. Available for the
   *  lifetime of the instance — after kill, it has been released
   *  to the allocator's pool. */
  readonly port: number;

  /** The `--user-data-dir` Chrome was launched with. */
  readonly userDataDir: string;

  /** The absolute path to the Chrome executable that was launched. */
  readonly executablePath: string;
}

/**
 * The options accepted by `launchChrome`.
 *
 * Every field except `userDataDir` has a sensible default. A caller
 * that wants "just launch Chrome for a test" can pass only
 * `userDataDir` and get a working instance.
 */
export interface LaunchChromeOptions {
  /**
   * Absolute path to the directory Chrome should use as its
   * persistent user-data directory. The directory is created if it
   * does not exist. This is the directory that holds the user's
   * DeepSeek login across app restarts — the caller decides what
   * path to use (the plan's convention is
   * `path.join(app.getPath('userData'), 'chrome-profile')`).
   */
  userDataDir: string;

  /**
   * Launch Chrome in headless mode.
   *
   * Defaults to `false`. The plan's user journey requires Chrome to
   * be VISIBLE — the user solves CAPTCHAs and clears Cloudflare
   * interstitials in the same window the automation drives. A
   * headless Chrome would make those interactions impossible.
   *
   * The option exists because some future operation (a background
   * re-fetch of a page that has never required interaction) could
   * use headless mode for speed. Nothing in the current plan does.
   */
  headless?: boolean;

  /**
   * The port to launch Chrome on. When omitted, a port is allocated
   * via `portAllocator.allocatePort()`. A caller that passes an
   * explicit port is responsible for ensuring the port is not
   * currently in use — the allocator's guarantee does not apply to
   * explicit ports.
   *
   * The option exists so that a developer running the app against
   * an already-launched Chrome (a manual debugging scenario) can
   * point the app at that Chrome's port without the allocator
   * having to know about it. In normal operation the caller
   * omits this field.
   */
  port?: number;

  /**
   * How long to wait for Chrome's CDP port to open, in
   * milliseconds. Defaults to 30_000 (30 seconds).
   *
   * A cold Chrome start on a slow disk (a network-mounted profile
   * directory, a first-launch on a new machine that has to
   * initialize the profile) can take several seconds. Thirty
   * seconds is generous without being pathological.
   */
  readyTimeoutMs?: number;
}

// =============================================================================
//  Constants
// =============================================================================

/**
 * The Chrome launch flags the automation requires. Each flag is
 * documented with the specific problem it solves; a flag whose
 * problem is not currently present should be removed rather than
 * left in place "just in case."
 */
const CHROME_FLAGS = [
  // `--no-first-run` — suppress the first-run wizard. Without it, a
  // fresh user-data-dir triggers Chrome's first-run flow, which
  // blocks the debug port from opening until the user dismisses a
  // series of dialogs. Since WriteSpeare's profile starts fresh on
  // a new machine, this flag is required for the first launch.
  '--no-first-run',

  // `--no-default-browser-check` — suppress the "make Chrome your
  // default browser?" prompt. Same reasoning as above: a modal
  // dialog blocks automation until dismissed.
  '--no-default-browser-check',

  // `--disable-background-networking` — suppress Chrome's own
  // telemetry, update pings, and background Safe Browsing fetches.
  // The automation does not need these, and they add noise to the
  // network traffic on a machine that is already being used for
  // scraping.
  '--disable-background-networking',

  // `--disable-sync` — do not sync this profile with the user's
  // Google account. WriteSpeare's profile is its own independent
  // Chrome profile; it must not merge the user's bookmarks,
  // history, or extensions from their personal Chrome.
  '--disable-sync',

  // `--disable-blink-features=AutomationControlled` — remove the
  // `navigator.webdriver` signal that Chrome sets by default when
  // it detects automation. This is not a guarantee against every
  // detection method — Google's anti-bot in particular uses a
  // wider set of signals — but it is the standard baseline and it
  // removes the one signal that is trivially detectable.
  '--disable-blink-features=AutomationControlled',

  // `--disable-features=Translate,MediaRouter` — disable two
  // Chrome features that open background processes the automation
  // does not need. Saves a small amount of memory per instance.
  '--disable-features=Translate,MediaRouter',

  // `--no-sandbox` on Windows only. Chrome's sandbox uses a
  // Windows job object that conflicts with Electron's own
  // process-tree management; disabling it is the standard fix for
  // Chrome-in-Electron setups. NOT applied on macOS or Linux,
  // where the sandbox interacts differently with the OS and no
  // conflict exists.
] as const;

/**
 * The Windows registry-uninstaller locations to search for Chrome
 * when the standard `Program Files` paths do not contain it. Chrome
 * on Windows can be installed in a user-scoped location
 * (`%LOCALAPPDATA%`) rather than a system-wide one; checking only
 * `Program Files` would miss a user-scoped install.
 *
 * The tuple is ordered: stable Chrome first, then Beta, then Dev,
 * then Chromium. The first match wins.
 */
const WINDOWS_CHROME_RELATIVE_PATHS = [
  path.join('Google', 'Chrome', 'Application', 'chrome.exe'),
  path.join('Google', 'Chrome Beta', 'Application', 'chrome.exe'),
  path.join('Google', 'Chrome Dev', 'Application', 'chrome.exe'),
  path.join('Chromium', 'Application', 'chrome.exe'),
] as const;

const WINDOWS_CHROME_ROOTS = [
  'PROGRAMFILES',
  'PROGRAMFILES(X86)',
  'LOCALAPPDATA',
] as const;

const MACOS_CHROME_PATHS = [
  '/Applications/Google Chrome.app/Contents/MacOS/Google Chrome',
  '/Applications/Google Chrome Beta.app/Contents/MacOS/Google Chrome Beta',
  '/Applications/Google Chrome Dev.app/Contents/MacOS/Google Chrome Dev',
  '/Applications/Chromium.app/Contents/MacOS/Chromium',
] as const;

const LINUX_CHROME_BINARIES = [
  'google-chrome',
  'google-chrome-stable',
  'google-chrome-beta',
  'google-chrome-dev',
  'chromium',
  'chromium-browser',
] as const;

// =============================================================================
//  Module state — the instance registry
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Every Chrome instance this module has launched and that has not
 * yet been killed. See the file header for the registry's purpose.
 *
 * The array's order is launch order. `killAllChrome` iterates it
 * from the end backward (most-recently-launched first), matching
 * the natural "undo in reverse order" convention for cleanup.
 *
 * Module-private. Callers read a snapshot via
 * `getRunningInstances()`.
 */
const runningInstances: ChromeInstance[] = [];

// =============================================================================
//  Chrome discovery
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Locate the OS's installed Chrome (or Chromium) binary.
 *
 * Returns the absolute path to the executable. Throws if no
 * candidate exists — the caller is expected to surface that as a
 * user-facing error, not to catch and ignore it.
 *
 * The search is platform-dispatched:
 *
 *   - Windows: walks the three known roots × four known channels,
 *     returning the first match.
 *   - macOS: walks four absolute paths.
 *   - Linux: walks the `PATH` via `which`.
 *
 * The function is exported because a diagnostics panel or a
 * first-run setup screen may want to display "Chrome not found" as
 * a specific message rather than as a generic launch failure. A
 * caller that only cares about launching does not need to call it
 * directly — `launchChrome` calls it internally.
 *
 * @returns The absolute path to a Chrome or Chromium executable.
 * @throws  `Error` if no candidate exists on this machine.
 */
export const findChromeExecutable = (): string => {
  const platform = process.platform;

  if (platform === 'win32') {
    for (const rootVar of WINDOWS_CHROME_ROOTS) {
      const root = process.env[rootVar];
      if (!root) continue;
      for (const rel of WINDOWS_CHROME_RELATIVE_PATHS) {
        const candidate = path.join(root, rel);
        if (existsSync(candidate)) {
          return candidate;
        }
      }
    }
  } else if (platform === 'darwin') {
    for (const candidate of MACOS_CHROME_PATHS) {
      if (existsSync(candidate)) {
        return candidate;
      }
    }
  } else {
    // Linux / BSD — use PATH lookup. `which` is not directly
    // available in Node, but the shelling-out approach is fragile
    // (a shell that is not present, a quoting issue). Instead,
    // iterate the PATH entries from `process.env.PATH` and check
    // each candidate directly.
    const pathEntries = (process.env['PATH'] ?? '').split(path.delimiter);
    for (const binaryName of LINUX_CHROME_BINARIES) {
      for (const entry of pathEntries) {
        if (!entry) continue;
        const candidate = path.join(entry, binaryName);
        if (existsSync(candidate)) {
          return candidate;
        }
      }
    }
  }

  throw new Error(
    'Chrome or Chromium could not be found on this system. ' +
      'Install Chrome from https://www.google.com/chrome/ and try again.'
  );
};

// =============================================================================
//  Readiness check
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Poll Chrome's CDP HTTP endpoint until it answers, or until the
 * timeout expires.
 *
 * Returns a promise that resolves when Chrome is accepting CDP
 * connections, or rejects on timeout. The check hits
 * `http://localhost:<port>/json/version`, which is Chrome's own
 * status endpoint — it opens as soon as Chrome's debug server is
 * ready, and every other CDP endpoint is reachable from that
 * moment on.
 *
 * The polling uses `fetch`, which is available in Node 18+ (and
 * Electron 44 ships Node 20+). No external HTTP library is needed.
 *
 * @param port          - The debug port Chrome was launched on.
 * @param timeoutMs     - How long to wait before rejecting.
 * @returns A promise that resolves when Chrome is ready.
 * @throws  `Error` if the timeout expires without a successful
 *          response.
 */
const waitForDebugPort = async (
  port: number,
  timeoutMs: number
): Promise<void> => {
  const url = `http://localhost:${port}/json/version`;
  const deadline = Date.now() + timeoutMs;
  const intervalMs = 250;

  while (Date.now() < deadline) {
    try {
      const response = await fetch(url);
      if (response.ok) {
        return;
      }
    } catch {
      // The port is not yet listening, or the request failed.
      // Sleep and retry.
    }
    await new Promise((resolve) => setTimeout(resolve, intervalMs));
  }

  throw new Error(
    `Chrome did not open its debug port on ${port} within ` +
      `${timeoutMs}ms. The most common cause is another Chrome ` +
      `instance already using the same user-data-dir: Chrome ` +
      `refuses to open a second instance on the same profile and ` +
      `silently hands off to the existing one, which is not ` +
      `listening on our debug port.`
  );
};

// =============================================================================
//  Public API — launch
// =============================================================================

/**
 * Launch a Chrome instance and wait until its CDP port is open.
 *
 * Flow:
 *
 *   1. Locate the Chrome executable via `findChromeExecutable`.
 *   2. Determine the debug port. If `options.port` is provided,
 *      use it; otherwise allocate one via `allocatePort`.
 *   3. Ensure `options.userDataDir` exists (Chrome requires its
 *      parent directory to exist).
 *   4. Spawn Chrome with the flags in `CHROME_FLAGS`, plus the
 *      debug port and user-data-dir.
 *   5. Wait for the CDP endpoint to answer.
 *   6. Register the instance in `runningInstances` and return a
 *      handle.
 *
 * On any failure after the port has been allocated, the port is
 * released so it is not leaked back into the pool. A failure
 * before the port is allocated has nothing to release.
 *
 * The spawned Chrome process is detached from the main process's
 * stdio: its `stdio` is set to `['ignore', 'ignore', 'ignore']`,
 * so Chrome's own output does not pollute the main process's
 * console (which the developer is reading for the app's own logs).
 * Chrome's log output is available through
 * `chrome://inspect`'s "Open dedicated DevTools" button if a
 * developer needs it — the terminal is not the right place for it.
 *
 * @param options - The launch configuration. Only `userDataDir` is
 *                  required; all other fields have defaults.
 * @returns A promise that resolves to a `ChromeInstance` handle
 *          once Chrome is ready to accept CDP connections.
 * @throws  `Error` if Chrome cannot be located, fails to spawn, or
 *          does not open its debug port within the timeout. The
 *          port is released on failure.
 */
export const launchChrome = async (
  options: LaunchChromeOptions
): Promise<ChromeInstance> => {
  const executablePath = findChromeExecutable();

  // ---- Determine the port ----
  const explicitPort = options.port;
  const port = explicitPort ?? allocatePort();
  const portWasAllocated = explicitPort === undefined;

  // ---- Ensure the user-data-dir exists ----
  // Chrome requires the parent of `--user-data-dir` to exist. A
  // caller that provides a nested path
  // (`userData/chrome-profile/default`) must have every intermediate
  // directory in place. Rather than replicate `mkdir -p` here, the
  // caller is expected to provide a path whose parent exists — the
  // convention is `path.join(app.getPath('userData'),
  // 'chrome-profile')`, and `app.getPath('userData')` always
  // exists. We check the parent and throw a clear error if it does
  // not; we do not silently create a tree of directories the caller
  // did not intend.
  const parentDir = path.dirname(options.userDataDir);
  if (!existsSync(parentDir)) {
    if (portWasAllocated) {
      releasePort(port);
    }
    throw new Error(
      `The parent directory of the Chrome user-data-dir does not ` +
        `exist: ${parentDir}. The caller must ensure it exists ` +
        `before launching Chrome.`
    );
  }

  // ---- Build the argument list ----
  const args: string[] = [
    `--remote-debugging-port=${port}`,
    `--user-data-dir=${options.userDataDir}`,
  ];

  for (const flag of CHROME_FLAGS) {
    args.push(flag);
  }

  if (process.platform === 'win32') {
    // See the CHROME_FLAGS comment for why --no-sandbox is Windows-
    // only.
    args.push('--no-sandbox');
  }

  if (options.headless === true) {
    args.push('--headless=new');
  }

  // ---- Spawn ----
  let child: ChildProcess;
  try {
    child = spawn(executablePath, args, {
      stdio: ['ignore', 'ignore', 'ignore'],
      // `detached: false` — the Chrome process is in the same
